#5840. NRDC Assessment of BAAJA, the 2026 Permitting & Transmission Bill

The Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026 (BAAJA) is the result of years of negotiations on permitting reform. The product is a bill that combines critically important transmission and grid reforms with significant and concerning changes to federal environmental laws, permitting standards, and judicial remedies.

The transmission provisions in the bill would produce substantial and durable benefits, updating an aging power grid by allowing new investments. These reforms, which NRDC has long supported, are an essential component of accelerating the electrification and decarbonization of the power sector and achieving greenhouse gas reduction targets. On their own, these changes are much needed and overdue; they would also help any future clean energy incentives and standards succeed in cutting emissions. But transmission is an enabling technology rather than a substitute for policies that directly drive clean energy investment and reduce pollution.

However, there are a number of concerning provisions that raise substantial issues and reinforce the position of incumbent energy interests. The “project certainty” language is designed to ensure projects cannot be arbitrarily canceled. As drafted, it could benefit fossil fuel, mining, data center, and other industrial projects more than clean energy. It could also limit agencies’ ability to address newly discovered harms, constrain future pollution regulation, and create large taxpayer-funded corporate handouts.

The bill would also weaken key elements of core environmental laws, including the Clean Water Act (CWA), the Endangered Species Act, the National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA), and the National Historic Preservation Act (NHPA). The bill would narrow environmental review across those statutes, diminish state and Tribal authority, constrain public participation, and restrict courts’ ability to stop projects proceeding under unlawful permits. These changes erode fundamental protections for communities.

The backdrop, of course, is the Trump administration’s nonstop attacks on clean energy projects, tax incentives for clean energy, and climate standards. NRDC’s analysis finds in aggregate that the Trump administration’s actions could double carbon emissions from the power sector over the next decade while raising consumers’ electric utility bills by $30 billion per year by 2035. Lawmakers should push for tangible evidence that this has changed before voting on the legislation.

While NRDC is encouraged by the transmission provisions, other elements of the bill are deeply flawed. We will be seeking improvements in the coming weeks and will take a position on the bill before a floor vote.

Below is an assessment of the most positive and most negative elements of the bill. This is not intended to be comprehensive.

An aging power grid desperately needs updating, and with more extreme weather and surging demand from data centers, getting new electricity connected to the grid quickly is essential to keeping energy costs down and maintaining resilience. In one fell swoop, this legislation would address these issues, producing the necessary conditions to deploy more clean energy.

This legislation would provide a dramatic near-term boost to the nation’s grid by increasing both the amount of power that existing transmission lines can carry and the supply of electricity available to meet growing demand. It establishes procedures that could cut the time to build a major new transmission line to five to six years from 10 years—or sometimes significantly longer—today.

The Midwest’s multi-value projects: How agreeing on who pays can unlock transmission

In 2011, the Midwest grid operator, MISO, approved 17 high-voltage lines costing more than $5 billion and spread the cost across the region based on who benefits. Within about a decade, 16 of the 17 were in service, helping to connect wind power across the Midwest.

Between regions, where no such agreement exists, almost no major lines were approved in the decade after FERC’s 2011 planning rule. The bill tries to bring the Midwest’s approach to lines that cross between regions.

In the short term, it would require utilities to upgrade existing transmission lines and deploy advanced transmission technologies whenever their benefits exceed their costs. This technology can increase the capacity and efficiency of existing transmission lines, reduce congestion, and improve reliability, often at a cost that is 5 to 10 times cheaper than building new transmission. If deployed aggressively in the short term, this could free up more than 100 gigawatts of transmission capacity—enough to power 75 million homes.

Importantly, the bill goes beyond simply requiring utilities to consider these technologies: It directs the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission (FERC) to establish timelines for deployment, makes it easier for utilities to recover their costs, and establishes penalties for utilities that fail to deploy cost-effective technologies.

From PJM’s queue freeze to Southwest Power Pool’s fix

In 2022, PJM, which runs the grid for 13 states and Washington, D.C., stopped taking new requests to connect to the grid while it worked through a backlog of more than 300 gigawatts, 95 percent of it renewables and storage. It didn’t reopen until April 2026, so projects that applied after 2022 are only now starting their studies.

In March 2026, FERC approved a new approach from Southwest Power Pool, which runs the grid across much of the central United States. It plans transmission and new power plants together over 20 years, charges developers a standard up-front fee to connect, and studies projects once a year with a single decision point. The bill would require every region to adopt a process like this within two years and to speed up studies with better data and automation.

The bill makes major interstate power lines easier to build by strengthening federal authority over large interstate transmission lines. Currently, a state can block or indefinitely delay a project that would provide broad regional or national benefits. If a state rejects an eligible project or fails to act within one year, the bill requires FERC to issue a permit for a covered transmission line when a developer seeks federal review and the project satisfies specific criteria. FERC must then spread the costs fairly among all consumers who benefit from the projects.

The bill also requires neighboring grid regions to plan interregional transmission jointly, assess the benefits of potential projects, and establish methods for allocating costs. Despite a FERC order issued in 2011 that was meant to spur these cross-regional power lines, currently, virtually no major lines have been built between regions because there is no durable process for planning them or determining who should pay. The bill addresses this long-standing problem and codifies important elements of FERC’s updated regional planning framework from 2024, known as Order 1920.

The bill will also reduce the long wait times for energy projects to connect to the grid. Approximately 2,000 gigawatts of proposed generation and storage are waiting to connect to the grid, and 85 percent of that capacity is solar, wind, or storage. Projects often wait years for studies and may receive unexpectedly high upgrade costs late in the process. The bill encourages more coordinated planning, provides developers earlier information about costs, and improves data and automation.

Taken together, these transmission and interconnection reforms are a once-in-a-generation opportunity that would produce significant and durable benefits. The reforms would also facilitate the infrastructure needed so that future clean energy incentives and standards could succeed.

The data center provisions could provide protection for existing utility customers and support grid investments by requiring data centers to pay for their impact to the grid. It would direct FERC to develop pricing rules designed to prevent transmission costs created by data centers (generally 20 megawatts or more) from being shifted to other ratepayers and ensure data centers provide financial assurance to avoid costs shifting in the future. FERC would also have to revise transmission pricing policies and require standardized analysis of upgrades and cost-reduction options. These provisions address the transmission portion of the broader effects of rapid data center growth. The bill does not address local air pollution, water consumption, noise, land use, reliance on on-site fossil fuel generation, transparency, or public participation.

With geothermal energy poised to be a major player in the clean energy future, the bill provides policy reforms that could increase permitting efficiency and lower some of the barriers to geothermal development. But the geothermal provisions also come with their drawbacks, including the expansion of Section 390’s oil and gas activity categorical exclusion to geothermal development, which allows for significant drilling and production activities to take place on public lands without environmental review.

In an effort to respond to the Trump administration’s relentless attacks on wind and solar projects, this legislation includes “project certainty” language to protect developers from unreasonable attacks. Unfortunately, these provisions could have problematic unintended consequences, including locking in the Trump administration’s preferential treatment of oil, gas, data centers, and mining projects. This part of the legislation could also lead to a slew of industry lawsuits that result in taxpayers paying billions of dollars to private industry.

These “project certainty” provisions would largely prevent federal agencies from canceling, suspending, or changing federal approvals once issued. The provision’s reach is not limited to energy projects and is not limited to specific agencies or types of permits. The legislation sets out a small handful of circumstances in which agencies can modify an issued permit. Notably, while these circumstances include national security or defense concerns, they do not include environmental harm—even if newly discovered, severe, or widespread. Its broad language could even lead to industry arguments and legal claims that operating facilities are protected from subsequently adopted pollution requirements under the Clean Air Act and other statutes. State permits to implement future U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) standards for power plant carbon emissions, for example, could face legal challenges based on this provision.

And if a project is canceled or amended, a company can go to court to get its permit reinstated—as well as require the government to pay for its litigation expenses and the cost of project delays. The government could also be forced to use taxpayer money to make an additional payment of 25–50 percent of total project costs incurred to the company. For a single large energy project, this could easily amount to hundreds of millions, or even billions, of dollars.

A separate provision would allow energy-project developers to sue agencies for alleged “disparate treatment.” The approach uses a five-year comparison period that could embed the second Trump administration’s energy preferences in the legal baseline, and thus advantage oil, gas, and coal projects. And it prevents agencies acting in good faith from fully defending themselves in court. Successful claims could result in payments equal to 50–100 percent of projected construction costs plus actual damages and legal costs, creating a serious risk that the provision could be gamed and produce large taxpayer-funded payouts.

The bill makes sweeping changes to our ability to enforce a number of environmental laws. For example, a court that finds violations of the CWA’s “dredge and fill” permit system will be unable to invalidate the permit except in narrow circumstances; a court can never invalidate a permit based on a violation of the NEPA and the NHPA, no matter how severe it finds a legal violation to be. Though a deficient review can be returned to an agency, construction is allowed to continue while the agency corrects its mistakes. The result could be that by the time the agency completes its review, the project may already be largely or entirely built and the damage difficult or impossible to reverse.

Right now, if a court finds that an agency violated NEPA, it can stop the project from moving forward until the agency corrects the environmental review documents. For example, a federal judge put an oil and gas lease in Alaska’s ecologically rich Cook Inlet on hold until the responsible agency corrected its defective environmental review. That reasonable result may not have been possible had this bill been the law.

The bill also excludes activities from NEPA review, expands exemptions for others, narrows the analysis of alternatives, and potentially limits who can bring a case. Collectively, these changes substantially reduce the practical value of NEPA as a means to improve environmental outcomes. Tribes, local organizations, and affected communities may be particularly disadvantaged if they are cut out of the process or cannot stop harmful projects before they cause damage.

The bill would dramatically curtail the rights and powers of Tribes enshrined in Section 106 of the NHPA, effectively limiting Tribes’ ability to protect their lands and culturally significant places from proposed projects with a federal connection. The bill limits Tribal participation in decisions concerning federally connected projects and what Tribal interests are protected, which, under current law, includes not only artifacts but also sacred places, cultural landscapes, waterways, wildlife, and living ecosystems. Tribal Nations and Indigenous partners have made clear that these changes significantly undermine Tribal sovereignty and the federal government’s nation-to-nation responsibilities.

NHPA, specifically Section 106, has played a critical role in ensuring that permitting processes must integrate Tribal Nation feedback, helping to improve the environmental and cultural resource outcomes for projects, including the Pebble Mine and the Arches and Canyonlands National Parks, due to oil and gas drilling proposals. Proposed changes to Section 106 would place additional areas in further jeopardy—including mining proposals that would impact the Brooks Range and Gates of the Arctic National Park and Preserve, along with drilling proposals in the Chaco region, the Western Arctic, and the Arctic National Wildlife Refuge, respectively.

The bill weakens several CWA programs, which will make it easier for activities that pollute or destroy water resources to move forward without adequate safeguards. It limits the ability of states and Tribes to ensure the health of streams, lakes, wetlands, and groundwater when the federal government authorizes activities that impact them. It takes a fast-tracked permitting process meant for activities that have minimal impacts (like boat ramps) and applies it to major projects such as pipelines and highways.

The bill makes significant changes to the Clean Water Act program that gives states and Tribes the ability to demand water quality–based improvements to federal permits. For gas pipelines and transmission projects, reviewing authorities are limited to considering direct discharges, (e.g., just the effects of a concrete structure for a pipeline) rather than broader construction-related effects such as erosion, sediment, contaminated runoff, groundwater pollution, and damage to wetlands and streams, or ongoing impacts from the operation of the project.

For other projects, the bill places a substantially higher burden on states seeking to require protections. Currently, a state can identify a water quality concern, request reasonable changes, and allow a project to proceed with stronger safeguards. A state would now be required to demonstrate harm by clear and convincing evidence (versus reasonable evidence) and show that its proposed condition is the least burdensome means of addressing the water quality concern. In practice, these new hurdles to water quality conditions will be very difficult to overcome, which will likely result in fewer state conditions on permits needed to protect water quality. In addition, the bill would restrict states and Tribes from taking the actions they can today to protect habitat, groundwater (including drinking water supplies), and many small streams and critical wetlands.

How the Clean Water Act change could impact states

If the special provisions for gas pipeline projects had been the law, Pennsylvania likely would not have been able to require the Atlantic Sunrise Pipeline Project to comply with state erosion and sediment laws, and West Virginia likely would not have been able to require controls and compliance monitoring that enabled the state to enforce against the Mountain Valley Pipeline’s violations of runoff requirements.

By shifting and raising the burden of proof, this requirement would jeopardize precautionary state actions, such as a Tennessee decision this spring, when it refused to sign off on an Army Corps permit for a landfill because the project developer had failed to show that pollution from the site would not harm local waters. This kind of state engagement improves projects and does not end them.

The bill also weakens the Clean Water Act’s general permitting program (administered by the U.S. Army Corps of Engineers) for dredging and filling waters. The bill directs the Corps, when creating general permits, to look only at the harm that results from filling in waters. No other water impacts like water pollution or groundwater depletion could be considered. Additionally, right now, most general permits can only be used for impacts of one-half acre or less; this bill would quadruple this threshold, which could allow larger data centers or other major projects to qualify. It also extends the duration of general permits, reducing opportunities for public input on requirements for projects to once a decade. Finally, it requires the Corps to maintain fast-track permits for large “linear” projects that can have major environmental harms, like roadways and pipelines.

The bill weakens other Clean Water Act programs as well. It would curb the EPA’s ability to stop projects that dump dredged or fill material that “have an unacceptable adverse effect on municipal water supplies, shellfish beds and fishery areas (including spawning and breeding areas), wildlife, or recreational areas,” which the agency has invoked rarely, but done so to protect exceptional habitats threatened by highly damaging projects. It would slow the EPA’s development of scientific advice to states about the levels of pollutants that can cause harm to aquatic life or people, and it would delay updates to discharge requirements for industrial polluters and sewage plants; together, these two changes would constrain the ability to control “forever chemicals.”

Language relating to the “best available science” standard could be interpreted to restrict precautionary action when scientific information is uncertain. This is particularly concerning when an imperiled population of a species is difficult or expensive to study and waiting for definitive proof could result in irreversible loss.

The bill also allows states to assume responsibilities currently carried out by the U.S. Fish and Wildlife Service. Many states lack the capacity, expertise, or in some cases, the commitment necessary to perform this responsibility effectively, and if a state is not executing its responsibility, there is not a requirement for the federal government to reassume it.

The bill would exempt many wells drilled on private lands that tap into federally owned oil and gas reserves from federal permitting, opening at least 57 million subsurface acres of federal resources to exploitation without federal permits. If passed into law, this provision strips thousands of private landowners across the West of important protections like required notice of industry’s plans to develop on their property, increases the risk that landowners get stuck with orphaned wells and their associated environmental and financial costs, and creates new risks of fraud and abuse as it relates to royalty reporting.

The bill allows mining companies to claim an unlimited number of five-acre mill sites on public lands to be used for things like tailing storage—wastewater and rock often containing heavy metals and other dangerous residues—and waste dumps. And it allows mining companies to occupy public lands for any other use relevant to a mining project, regardless of its proximity to the actual mine, meaning roads, pipelines, powerlines, and any other activity formerly requiring a federal permit and environmental review would be allowed so long as the activity is included in the mine’s operation plan.

The modeling completed to date focuses on greenhouse gas and economic impacts. Available analyses from NRDC and other groups generally project modest to significant greenhouse gas benefits, especially over the long term, but the results vary substantially depending on the provisions modeled, policy baseline, fuel and technology costs, and implementation assumptions.

NRDC’s initial results show modest power sector benefits.

The benefits of additional transmission will generally be greater in a policy environment that drives clean energy deployment. In a baseline with limited support for new clean energy resources, transmission may improve reliability and reduce costs without producing sizable emissions reductions.

The oil-and-gas effects remain uncertain. NRDC has not quantified the bill’s effects on oil-and-gas development. Because the existing baseline already assumes high levels of domestic production growth (oil and gas production have both doubled in the last 20 years), faster permitting may not produce a large increase in emissions compared with business as usual. Nevertheless, the bill could accelerate individual projects, improve their economics, and make approved infrastructure more difficult to regulate or modify later. The risk of locking in that trajectory merits consideration, even if near-term emissions changes appear modest.

Data-center growth creates a particularly important unknown. The grid provisions may indeed enable data centers to finance upgrades and connect to an expanded transmission system. But transmission takes years to plan and construct. In the interim, faster permitting and weaker environmental safeguards could encourage additional on-site or nearby gas generation, particularly in regions with access to existing pipelines or gas fields.

Greenhouse gas emissions are only one measure of the bill’s effects. The modeling does not quantify potential consequences for drinking water, local air pollution, public health, endangered species and wildlife, or other metrics. It also cannot account for the practical importance of judicial remedies in ensuring that agencies and developers comply with environmental law.

NRDC is seeking significant improvements to the bill in five areas:

NRDC will continue to advance technical corrections, including changes needed to address drafting errors and unintended consequences. But technical corrections cannot substitute for changes to some of these structural provisions, for which we will continue to advocate.

NRDC is the nation's most effective environmental action group, combining the grassroots power of 1.3 million members and online activists with the courtroom clout and expertise of more than 350 lawyers, scientists, and other professionals.

#5838. Is Trump About To Reignite His Big EV Push?

There are so many ironies with the Trump presidency. I remember writing about many of them in his first term. Now, though, we have some additional ones. For example, despite his apparent hate for clean technologies (wind power, solar power, electric vehicles, energy efficient appliances), Trump has pushed a ton of people to buy electric vehicles, including people who really wouldn’t consider doing so even with normal EV incentives in place.

Of course, we all know what this is about. This was a key reason for the absolutely massive blue tsunami that took over the country this week. Trump’s war on Iran, which many Americans can’t explain in the slightest, drove gasoline prices up enormously and pushed people to go to the polls and vote Democrat.

Trump and his team also knew this. They knew that the war on Iran was raising gas prices and that high gas prices are one of the worst things you can have when running for office. So, even though Trump didn’t leave himself any easy and simple way to get out of the war on Iran, he quieted down or paused his most significant actions against the country for the midterm elections and gas prices are not as high as they were a few months ago. They are just above $4/gallon on average, whereas the rose above $4.50/gallon earlier this year. Just in the week leading into election day, the national gas price average dropped by 12 cents a gallon. Of course, it was too little too late, and Republican politicians’ lack of concern for families and their high gas costs didn’t fare well in the election. This was probably the leading single factor for the swing to the left.

Now that the election is over and the war is not close to any resolution as far as we can tell, Trump may be inclined to heat it up again. If he has been holding back, he may go full force again into trying to force Iran to give in, and this could lead to a bigger closure of the Strait of Hormuz … which would likely lead to spiking gas prices again. Naturally, even at $4/gallon, Trump is making a lot of Americans (and humans more broadly) think about buying electric vehicles, and some of them are going to go ahead and making the switch. What if Trump goes further, as many now expect he will, and oil supply is disrupted much worse and oil prices spike again? He could become one of the greatest EV salesmen in history. We’ll see what happens….

#5837. New York program funds $13 million in energy storage advancement projects

New York State’s Power Generation and Storage Innovation program has awarded nearly $13 million to 15 projects to support product development and demonstration of scalable long duration and other advanced battery energy storage solutions.

“Investing in next-generation energy storage technologies is critical to building a flexible and resilient grid that can make the most of our diverse energy resources,” said Doreen M. Harris, NYSERDA president and CEO. “These projects will advance promising solutions that can deliver power when it is needed most, strengthen grid performance and help drive down costs as we build a more affordable and reliable energy system for New York consumers.”

NYSERDA’s Power Generation and Storage Innovation program awards projects that develop and demonstrate innovative long duration energy storage (LDES) and other under-utilized, alternatives to traditional lithium-ion technologies. The awarded technologies include new designs for scalable LDES solutions that are over 10 hours in duration at rated power and will address cost, performance, and integration challenges such as grid congestion, hosting capacity constraints and lithium-ion siting limitations.

Public Service Commission Chair Rory Christian said, “Storage is poised to play an increasingly critical role in supporting a reliable, cost-effective, flexible, and lower emissions system as more clean energy resources are integrated into the grid, as well as enhance the efficiency of the state’s existing resources. NYSERDA’s dedication of these funds originating from innovation and research funding overseen by the Commission positions New York’s consumers to yield the promising long-term benefits expected by more complex and scalable applications of these technologies.”

All contracts are awarded to companies located in New York, partnered with New York companies or for development and demonstrations in the state. Today’s announcement supports NYSERDA’s Innovation and Research (I&R) program, which invests over $96 million per year to attract world-class energy innovators, reduce risk for private investors and remove barriers to clean energy adoption in New York State, leveraging $15 in additional investment for every $1 spent. NYSERDA has partnered with over 900 companies that have helped make more than 300 products commercially available for consumers, businesses and utilities.

Power Generation and Storage Innovation funding for comes from the state’s Clean Energy Fund.

#5836. GM Steps On Tesla’s EV Toes With EV1 Celebration

In a move that shocked and surprised nobody, last week Tesla CEO Elon Musk pulled the plug on his much-hyped Cybertruck. Production will end sometime next year. It may be small consolation, but at least the Cybertruck sold more units than another notorious flop in modern automotive history, General Motors’s ill-fated EV1 sedan. Was EV1 really a flop, though? In sales, yes, but GM wants everyone to know that the EV1 beat Tesla to the electrification punch in key technology milestones.

GM actually sold few, if any, EV1 units, preferring to lease the car with a focus on drivers in California. Production began in 1996 but that didn’t last long. In 2003 GM pulled the plug, recalled the vehicles, and sent them to the crusher, having produced barely more than 1,000 units overall.

Only a scant few escaped the junkyard. One of them was rescued by US filmmaker Alex Gibney, leading to his advisory role in the searing 2006 EV1 documentary “Who Killed the Electric Car?” (btw, Gibney ‘s newly released “Musk” documentary is available on streaming now).

This year marks the 30th anniversary of EV1 production, and GM is using the occasion to celebrate. Rather than shoveling all memory of the EV1 debacle into the memory hole, GM has embarked on sort of under-the-radar public relations campaign, seeking to position the car as a necessary, albeit short lived, step along the vehicle electrification journey.

In not so many words, GM also draws attention to the fact that its work on EV1 predates the launch of Tesla Motors, which came on the scene in 2003. The automaker’s Senior Writer and Editor at GM News, Chris Perkins, ran down the particulars in March, noting that EV1 was the first EV to deploy heat pumps for climate control, energy efficiency, and battery temperature management.

Perkins also takes note of the car’s regenerative braking system. “Engineers developed a system for the car that translated a driver’s brake-pedal input into an electronic signal that blended regenerative and friction braking,” Perkins wrote.

“It’s a precursor to the braking systems used in GM’s modern EVs, which also add One-Pedal Driving1 and paddle-actuated Regen-On-Demand2 capability,” he added.

EV1 also eschewed mechanical actuators in favor of fully electronic (aka by-wire) controls for the accelerator pedal, brake pedal, parking brake, and gear selector, with the power steering featuring electro-hydraulic control.

Additionally, Perkins elaborated on the regenerative braking angle in a follow-up article in August, by way of demonstrating a through line between the 1990’s technology and the automaker’s current EV lineup.

“Conceptually, the EV1’s braking system operates exactly like the braking systems in GM’s modern EVs,” Perkins wrote. “Press the brake pedal, and the car’s computers decide how much friction and regenerative braking to actuate. Should the electronic system fail, it’s connected to a mechanical backup, just as in the EV1.”

As part of the PR campaign, GM is also putting its 1994 concept sedan on display, the 1994 Impact (pictured above). “As one of the first modern, purpose-built electric vehicles from a major automaker, the Impact was a critical learning tool that shaped the final production version of the EV1,” GM emphasizes.

If you blink, you’ll miss it. The Impact is on display at the GM-sponsored Test Track at EPCOT, the Disney theme park in Bay Lake, Florida, until mid-December.

As for why all this fuss, why now, that’s a good question. Over the past several years, GM has put a jolly spin on its inability to catch up to Tesla in sales, let alone surpass it. As recently as last year, GM vigorously touted itself as #2 in EV sales in the US, second only to Tesla as a badge of honor.

Still, by August of 2025 the competition was beginning to take on a different tone, with GM pitching low-cost LFP (lithium-iron-phosphate) batteries as a near-term pathway to meeting, then beating Tesla.

The 2026 EV1 celebration elaborates upon the technology angle, underscoring GM’s claim on the pre-Tesla, 1990’s roots of innovation in the vehicle electrification field.

The new emphasis on technology also comes at a pivotal moment for EV sales in the US. The US market nosedived last fall after US President Trump and his Republican allies in Congress prematurely eliminated federal tax credits for EVs. However, activity continued to stir in some quarters, and Trump himself made the case for EVs more attractive when he decided to bomb Iran in February.

Nine months later, the price of gas remains stubbornly high, sparking renewed hopes that EV sales will rebound more quickly than first anticipated. In addition, a fresh round of innovation is on the horizon, with GM banking on its forthcoming LMR (lithium manganese-rich) battery formula to push costs down without sacrificing performance.

While Tesla is not the only competition in town, beating the startup at its own game would be a feather in GM’s cap, and Elon Musk made the effort a little easier when he canceled the Cybertruck, leaving Tesla without a foothold in the hot market for pickup trucks in the US. The automaker still has sedans, SUVs, the new Semi Class 8 truck, and the “flying” Roadster in its pocket, but the demise of the Cybertruck has left the pickup field wide open.

Meanwhile, Ford is on track to produce its new Fathom electric pickup next year starting at under $30,000, far less than the Cybertruck’s most recent starting price of $77,000 plus change. The Jeff Bezos-backed startup Slate Auto is also aiming at the affordability market with a stripped-down electric pickup starting at a base price of $26,000 and change, before accounting for add-ons.

On its part, GM is aiming for the middle ground with the 2027 Silverado electric pickup, starting at around $56,000 and reaching close to Cybertruck range at around $72,000.

Meanwhile, Rivian is also aiming upscale at close to $80,000 and up for its RIT pickup. The startup is on the move, having somehow managed to squeeze a loan guarantee of up to $4.5 billion out of the Trump administration. The funds are earmarked to expand Rivian’s production capacity with a new factory in Georgia.

Readers, what do you think? Does GM — or any other automaker — have a snowball’s chance in hell to catch up to Tesla in the US market? Drop a note in the discussion thread…

Photo: General Motors introduced the Impact concept EV in 1994, laying the groundwork for the automaker’s short-lived EV1 electric car (Impact concept; cropped, courtesy of GM).

#5834. Cold Seawater Is Supplying District Heat in Esbjerg, Denmark

Esbjerg, Denmark, is using large electric heat pumps to extract heat from the North Sea and supply its district-heating network. During a two-hour winter test, seawater entered the system at 3.3°C and left at 1.2°C. The two heat pumps consumed 19.9 MW of electricity and supplied 55.2 MW of heat at a district-heating water temperature of 90°C. The difference, 35.3 MW, came from the seawater. The project team’s May 2025 operating report recorded a coefficient of performance, or COP, of 2.8. The figures describe that test, not annual average performance.

A heat pump transfers thermal energy from a colder source to a warmer destination. Its refrigerant absorbs heat at a temperature below that of the seawater, and an electrically driven compressor raises the temperature so the heat can enter the district network. Esbjerg uses carbon dioxide as its refrigerant in a high-pressure cycle. For each unit of electricity used by the compressors during the test, the plant delivered 2.8 units of heat. Resistance heating would have required 2.8 times as much electricity for the same heat output under those conditions.

The installation has a nominal heating capacity of 70 MW and began delivering heat in late 2024, as part of Esbjerg’s replacement of its retired coal-fired combined heat and power plant. The operating report describes controls for variable conditions and protection against freezing as seawater temperatures fall. The recorded COP of 2.8 was lower than the more favourable performance figures used in the original project expectations, and full guarantee testing had not been completed when the results were published. The city’s heating supply also includes a wood-chip boiler and waste-incineration heat. Emissions reductions reported for the wider replacement programme cannot be attributed in full to the seawater heat pumps.

Electricity price and heating-network temperature have a direct effect on the cost. At an illustrative electricity price of €100/MWh, compressor power at COP 2.8 costs about €36 per MWh of useful heat. That excludes seawater pumping, equipment maintenance, capital recovery, financing and heat distribution. A network able to operate at lower water temperatures generally asks the compressor to make a smaller temperature increase, which can improve COP. Older networks supplying 90°C heat face a harder operating requirement than newer, lower-temperature networks.

Esbjerg builds on decades of seawater heating experience. Monaco began using seawater heat pumps in 1963, and Stockholm has used large seawater-sourced district-heating equipment since the 1980s. Creative Energy’s Horseshoe Bay development in West Vancouver takes a different approach. Submerged heat exchangers transfer heat through a closed loop, with no seawater intake or discharge to the marina. Denmark’s Aalborg project is developing 177 MW of seawater heat-pump capacity. These installations serve different loads and use different marine arrangements, so their costs cannot be assumed to match Esbjerg’s.

Marine heat systems require more than suitable water temperatures. Engineers must address corrosion, biological fouling, intake and discharge effects, pumping requirements, maintenance access and freezing protection. The environmental assessment has to consider the local marine ecosystem and the discharge of colder water. The distribution network can be the larger financial obstacle in areas without existing pipes or concentrated heat demand. Wastewater, industrial waste heat, ground-source systems or individual air-source heat pumps may provide lower-cost heat at some sites.

Hot-water storage can add value by allowing a utility to make some heat when electricity is inexpensive and deliver it later. The available flexibility depends on storage capacity, network temperatures and heat demand. Esbjerg’s test provides direct evidence that cold seawater can support high-temperature district heating. Coastal utilities can use that evidence when comparing local heat sources, network investments and the costs of replacing combustion-based heating.

My TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines Esbjerg’s operating results, experience in Stockholm and Monaco, the Horseshoe Bay system near Vancouver, and the cost and environmental factors that determine where seawater heating is suitable.

#5832. The Velotric GoMad Electric Utility eBike — CleanTechnica Tested

Velotric has emerged as one of the largest ebike brands in the US, offering a wide range of models for buyers to choose from. Having a wide range of models with differing capabilities is increasingly a key factor for buyers when deciding which ebike to buy, as it allows them to upgrade or swap out for a different model to accommodate different stages of life.

They reached out to CleanTechnica about a review and we were excited to play with the Velotric GoMad Electric Utility bike, because it is just so darn flexible in what it can do. You can throw a set of bags on the back rack or a passenger kit to bring a friend with you. You can put a basket on the back and deliver pizzas, or you can just throw a backpack on and ride around town solo. It truly looked like the jack of all trades, so we pulled the trigger, and after a few days, it showed up.

Disclaimer: Velotric provided the GoMad to the author for the purposes of this review.

Assembling the Velotric GoMad is similar to the process for most e-bikes. It can be done with a basic set of tools that are included with the bike, and takes between 30 minutes and an hour depending on how much of a hurry you’re in.

After that, it’s a simple matter of attaching the pedals, mounting the handlebars to the stem, installing the front wheel and tire, connecting a few of the electrical connectors between the handlebars and the bike itself, airing up the tires, and you’re on your way. Ours took a few minutes longer than normal to bolt on the optional Passenger Kit

Taking in the Velotric GoMad, it is an attractive bike with great proportions. The battery is integrated into the main downtube on the frame, which makes it look slightly bloated, but in a way that fits into the overall proportions of the bike. The oversized tires help with this, making it look like it can go absolutely anywhere.

The rear hub motor kicks out plenty of power, and with a range of pedal assist modes. You can choose just how much power you need for the ride you want. You can choose from the motor being completely off, Eco, Tour, Trail, Sport, and even the full-out Boost mode that can give you exactly the support you need.

It’s perfect for when you’re carrying a load, powering up a hill, or just feeling tired. The motor is always ready to step in to provide assistance. At the upper end of its power cycle, the motor controller is more sensitive to the pedaling cadence, resulting in the motor pulsing on and off, providing for a bit of a bumpy sensation when pedaling. It’s only something you’d notice if you’re really paying attention, but it’s there.

The GoMad is designed with an integrated rear rack and a hidden storage pocket. The rack can also be converted into a seat for a second passenger with Velotric’s passenger kit. The passenger kit itself is amazing if that’s what you’re looking for, and this is further accentuated by the bike’s compact footprint. The tradeoff is that the main seat has a flat rear, which does make it slightly less comfortable than more fully built-out options.

The passenger kit comes with a set of pegs down below for the passengers’ feet to rest on, a rear grab rail for the rack, and a front handle for the passenger to hang onto as you’re boosting around town. Given the compact footprint of the bike, it feels like it was designed to be used as more of a teenager-plus-one setup, but if you want to put it to work as a husband-and-wife date night setup, its 500 pound weight capacity is more than up to the task.

I rode around on it with my wife on the back and it was very comfortable and stable for both of us. The foot pegs and the solid support bars that come with the passenger kit make it feel much safer and more comfortable for the passenger. I took a few pics of us on the bike, but, hilariously, none of them had the bike in frame and she wasn’t a fan of my one handed stunts. Of course, that’s for you to decide what you want to do with your life and how you want to set up your bike and put it to use.

The Velotric Nomad is an extremely capable bike at a mid-level price point for bikes of this quality and capability. It’s currently listed as $1,899 with a special Black Friday offer that includes a free young passenger kit.

The fact that its 750W continuous power output motor has a peak of 1,300W means it’s got plenty of pep to take even the largest and least-in-shape riders wherever they want to go. The specs across the board and the integration of the torque and cadence sensor make it feel like a very natural ride that will surprise you with just how intuitively the power is coming from the motor.

I especially love the colorways this bike comes in. They have a nice modern-looking sage that feels classy and refined, a dark amber that’s somewhere between a bright orange and bright red that looks like a pop of lipstick that really sets the whole frame off, and finally an obsidian, which is what our review bike came in.

The obsidian has a base color that looks almost black, but then when you put it in the sunlight, it has a rainbow pop of color that peeks out every once in a while. It’s not so much that it feels like it’s covered in glitter, but it’s just enough to make it feel fun.

For more information about the Velotric Nomad e-bike, head over to their official website, where you can learn all about it or purchase one for yourself.

I'm a tech geek passionately in search of actionable ways to reduce the negative impact my life has on the planet, save money and reduce stress. Live intentionally, make conscious decisions, love more, act responsibly, play. The more you know, the less you need. As an activist investor, Kyle owns long term stock holdings in Tesla and Rivian.

#5829. REDD — Picking Up Our New Tesla Model Y

REDD, the name for our new Tesla Model Y (Standard Range ’26 build, ’27 model), was inspired by the movie of the same name – Retired, Extremely Dangerous Drivers (the wife and I, of course). As mentioned previously, there is nothing wrong with our beloved 2019 Model 3. It is our bodies that are aging and less able to get in and out of our beloved little sporty Tess. So it was with mixed feelings that we arrived at the Tesla delivery centre to pick up the new car.

As always, it was a hive of activity, with deliveries taking place all around us. Quite a contrast to 7 years ago when Tesla was operating out of one cramped delivery spot near the centre of Brisbane, and the handover took less than 3 minutes. This time there were plenty of sales representatives spending their time and making sure we knew what we were doing.

On arrival, we walked past a line consisting of ICE cars being surrendered as trade-ins for an electric. As we left, a car carrier was unloading half a dozen new Model Ys. All in all, a smooth operation. While Majella was getting her phone synced and lots of information from the sales rep, I had a wander around.

The delivery centre is divided in two by a screen. On our side of the screen, I could see at least 60 new cars anxiously awaiting their new owners — like puppies at the pound, I thought. I wasn’t allowed behind the curtain where cars were being detailed and prepped. I commented to our sales rep that there didn’t seem to be many Model 3s. He told me that they had just received a Model Y shipment; the 3s were on their way. Asked about what lay behind the curtain in Tesla wonderland, he said that there were hundreds of cars “back there.”

Majella drove REDD home and I drove Tess. I am having mixed feelings. I get attached to my cars; it was difficult to sell George (my ‘64 Wolseley) and Ruby (my Hyundai Accent). But they were old and tired and their time had come. Tess, at 180,000+ kilometres still feels young. It’s me that’s getting old. The letting go is being made easier because we are selling Tess to our youngest daughter. So, we will still be in contact with the car and watch the milestones as she grows.

As we sipped our celebratory champagne, I mused at the difference between buying the Model Y in 2026 and the Model 3 in 2019. Going from the Hyundai to the Tesla was a massive leap in both design and technology — the screen, the regen braking. Our Tesla was the only one in our suburb, and there was no high-speed charging. Electric vehicles were seen as a “soon to pass” fad on the way to hydrogen cars, and a stupid decision in a country as big as Australia. In 2019, less than one percent of new cars sold had a plug, Tesla was still on a deathwatch. We had taken a risk — would it work out?

It certainly has! Owning Tess and dealing with FUD gave me a cause to champion. I started exploring this new world and writing about it — driving long trips and proving that an EV can do what it has to. CleanTechnica published my ramblings — which have now reached over 1,000 articles. People contacted me and shared their stories of private ownership. CEOs of new startups (like recently introduced Thermal Dawn) gave me interviews and insights. This might sound weird, but moving from the Model 3 to the Model Y is nowhere near as exciting. Yes, I will continue to write, but the motoring world has changed. In Australia, the Model Y has been the highest selling passenger car of all drivetrains for the past few months. In October, it even beat the refreshed Toyota RAV4. My northern Brisbane suburb is full of Tesla Model Ys. The crossover has become common.

Motoring journalists are learning slowly to accept the new kids on the block, and most of their reviews seem to indicate a modicum of tolerance and truth. Of course, we still have vested interests proclaiming that Chinese cars are destroying the Australian car industry. Read: dealerships can’t sell ICE cars and aren’t making enough money. I wonder sometimes what there will be left write about as plugins take more and more of the market, and “real” motoring journalists write the articles I normally write. Relax, I am sure there will be room for all of us for some time. Especially in the USA!

Calls continue for more charging infrastructure, which is fair enough for highway and tourist travel, but seems to totally ignore home charging. “I live in a remote town and there is nowhere to charge my car!” wails one. I could scream if he would hear me: plug it into your house power socket, you moron!

Tess sits in the carport plugged into our home battery. Majella has taken REDD to her gem club. I am sure that instead of their normal lapidary, faceting, and jewellery making, they will be exploring REDD. Four other members of the club have now got Teslas or BYDs. One of them commented that they want a Model Y for Christmas — not sure if his wife will agree.

REDD will also be the guest of honour at our upcoming Coffee, Cake, and EV morning tea in a few days’ time.

Once we have done the roadworthy and registration transfer, Tess will be off to her new home. We will get to see her at family gatherings and when we pick up the grandkidlets from school. Majella is busy buying the accessories — mats, ceiling screen (no, the new heavy tint doesn’t do much in the Australian sun), rim protectors, etc. REDD came with front and back floor mats. She is buying all-weather mats for the frunk and trunk. The dog may even get to sit in the car! Shock! Horror! And we are slowly noticing subtle differences, like the reduction in noise driving on the highway — REDD has better seals and double-glazed windows.

We may have lost some of the apps that came free with Tess (Spotify, Karaoke, connectivity), but we have gained cooling seats and a heated steering wheel. And, of course, Hardware 4, which will give us access to FSD (Supervised) for our long trips. Speaking of longer trips, the range of our Model 3 (about 300 km with some degradation) pales beside the 460 km range of the LFP batteries in REDD. LFP batteries also mean less anxiety about charging to 100% when needed. REDD is longer, wider, and taller, but, thank goodness, still fits in our garage.

Majella is missing Enhanced Autopilot, for which we paid AU$5,000 just a few years ago. The Model Y’s cruise control is not as convenient. Stay tuned for updates as we enter the next leg of our electric journey.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5827. BMW Pitches Free EV Charging And You Ain’t Seen Nothing Yet

EV sales in the US are beginning to tick up again, and BMW is determined to grab a slice of the action. The German automaker has begun offering a free EV charging package to anyone who purchases a 2027 BMW i5, BMW i7, or BMW iX3. The package includes $500 in charging credits, which seems like small potatoes compared to the cost of a BMW. Still, it’s better than nothing, and it positions the company to compete in a rapidly evolving landscape that includes free charging hours.

The “free hours” trend has already emerged in the US, where Texas (of course!) is an epicenter of activity. The idea is to smooth out peak weekday demand spikes and take advantage of spare capacity on the grid by offering ratepayers an incentive to use more electricity during nighttime hours, with the leading utility Constellation being a representative example.

“When you sign up for a free night electricity plan, a smart meter monitors your energy usage by time of day,” Constellation explains. The company offers a “Free Power Hours” plan between midnight and 4 a.m. every night.

“Constellation also offers Discounted Nights & Fair Days, with discounted electricity supply rates from 10 p.m. to 6 a.m.,” the company adds.

Constellation notes that standard service charges and other fees apply. They also advise that the free plan is not a good fit for households with heavy electricity use during the day, when higher rates apply.

Still, access to EV charging at a minimal cost is a tantalizing incentive for some households. Constellation currently offers its “FreeCharge” plan between midnight and 4 a.m. to ratepayers in Pennsylvania as well as Texas.

Against this backdrop, a mere $500 EV charging credit for a BMW is not much of an incentive, at least not in states where free hours are available. The monthly lease for the 2027 BMW i5, for example, clocks in at almost $750. Purchasing the car outright will set you back almost $70,000, including the destination fee.

Still, any incentive is better than no incentive, and the new offer provides BMW an opportunity to hitch its star to the free hours trend. “The new offer, available beginning November 5, 2026, is designed to make public charging simpler, more convenient and more affordable for BMW customers,” explains BMW.

“The package includes a $300 BMW Charging Network credit that can be used at participating charging providers1, as well as a separate $200 credit for charging at IONNA locations,” the company elaborates, with IONNA referring to a consortium of EV makers (including BMW) focused on improving the driver experience at charging stations.

In addition to the $200 IONNA credit, BMW is also offering a one-year IONNA membership plan that provides for a 25% charging discount.

For the record, the BMW Charging Network includes more than 180,000 charging stations compatible with the company’s My BMW app.

Over and above the EV charging package, BMW is also among the electrification stakeholders that did not throw in the towel after last year’s EV sales crash. Despite the premature demise of the federal EV tax credit on September 30 of 2025, the number of public EV charging stations in the US has continued to increase, and charging networks have become more sophisticated and convenient.

In addition to the hookup with IONNA, for example, the BMW Charging Network includes Shell Recharge, Electrify America, ChargePoint, and EVgo among others.

“The company also has invested in Plug & Charge, which simplifies the charging experience; and ChargeScape, a collaborative effort to help manage the flow of energy between EVs and the power grid,” BMW added in a press statement last week.

ChargeScape represents another indication that Trump’s war against EVs was over before it started, tax credits or not.

ChargeScape launched three years ago as a joint venture between BMW, Ford, and the US branch of Honda. “Benefiting both EV customers and the electric utility industry in the U.S. and Canada, ChargeScape will unlock entirely new value that EVs can provide to the electric grid, while enabling EV customers to earn financial benefits through a variety of managed charging and energy-sharing services never before possible with traditional gasoline-powered vehicles,” the three automakers enthused in a press statement dated December 29, 2023.

“ChargeScape’s platform will give electric utilities access to EV battery energy across a wide pool of EVs. Participating EV customers will have the potential to earn financial benefits by charging at ‘grid-friendly’ times through flexible and managed schedules,” the partners elaborated.

“The development of this project comes at a time when electric vehicle sales and infrastructure growth are ramping up quickly,” the partners added.

That was certainly an optimistic take on the future of EV sales in the US, but that was December of 2023. Just about one year later, then-candidate Donald Trump sailed to victory on Election Day 2024, winning a second turn around the Oval Office with a plan to stop the vehicle electrification trend in its tracks.

Still, ChargeScape soldiered on. By September of 2024 the alliance was ready for launch, to be joined by Nissan just one month later. ChargeScape also added the startup Rivian to its rolls in June of this year, with an option to add bidirectional EV charging. Other automakers, including Tesla, also use the platform.

The bidirectional charging element is where things get particularly interesting. The technology emerged in the early 2000’s, enabling EV drivers to power their home from their car battery, or send electricity to the grid. Unlocking the mass market potential of bidirectional charging is a next-level challenge, and the free hours trend could help accelerate uptake. With free hours EV charging, drivers can store electricity at minimal cost during those periods, and discharge it to trim the cost of electricity for their homes during periods when higher electricity rates apply.

Ford is particularly excited about the potential for bidirectional charging to scale up. The company’s forthcoming Ford Fathom electric pickup truck comes with an internal inverter that converts the DC charge from the battery into an AC current for home use. The built-in inverter eliminates the cost of the external, wall mounted inverter otherwise needed for bidirectional charging at home.

Photo: BMW is sweetening the EV charging pot with a $500 charging credit on its 2027 i5, i7, and iX3 electric cars in the US (courtesy of BMW).

#5821. A New $1.5 Billion Wind Farm Will Play In Peoria

It ain’t over ’til it’s over, but the proposed $1.5 billion, 500-megawatt Four Creeks wind farm in Peoria County, Illinois has just passed a key step in the approval process. The new milestone is yet another indication that the domestic wind industry will continue to play a key role in the US energy transition, regardless of the topsy-turvy energy policy articulated by US President Donald Trump.

If you’re somewhat shocked to hear word of a new wind farm proposal in the US, join the club. The President has spent considerable time, energy, and taxpayer dollars in a Herculean effort to keep new wind turbines from cropping up around the US under his watch. However, his efforts have earned only mixed success so far.

Offshore wind turbines keep slipping through the cracks, even though virtually every offshore wind farm in the US is subject to federal authority. Opportunities for wind power also persist on land, where the President’s direct authority is limited to projects on federal property.

Trump attempted to work around that obstacle in August of 2025, when he weaponized an otherwise routine wind project review process that applies to projects on non-federal property. The change in policy threw at least 155 onshore wind projects totaling 45 gigawatts into limbo, prompting a lawsuit from a coalition of clean energy organizations and wind energy developers.

In July of this year, Attorneys General in 19 states intervened in support of the lawsuit. The participants included Arizona, California, Colorado, Connecticut, Delaware, the District of Columbia, Illinois, Maine, Massachusetts, Maryland, Michigan, Minnesota, Nevada, New Jersey, New Mexico, New York, Oregon, Rhode Island, and Washington State.

Illinois Attorney General Kwame Raoul, who co-led the intervention, notes that federal law does require wind developers to submit proposals for wind turbines more than 200 feet tall to the Federal Aviation Administration, which then refers the project to DoD to check for any conflicts with military operations and related activities. Raoul further notes that the normal process involves collaborating on mitigation measures to resolve any conflicts.

That all ground to a halt in August of 2025, when DoD dropped the ball on its part of the process. “Officials ceased countersigning mitigation agreements, stopped sending completed agreements to developers for signature, and delayed or halted communications with developers about mitigation,” Raoul explained in a press statement in July.

The abrupt change in policy impacted at least nine wind projects in Illinois alone. According to Raoul’s office the nine projects represent $4.3 billion in private investment, or more.

Separately, on June 16 of this year the same coalition of state Attorneys General won the final resolution of a series of legal challenges to an Executive Order issued in January of 2025, in which Trump ordered a halt to all federal approvals for wind projects.

The DoD lawsuit is heading down the same path, at least so far. On August 6, a federal judge with the US District Court for the District of Oregon ordered DoD to resume its normal wind turbine review process while the case continues to wind its way through the courts.

DoD has been slow-walking the process since then, reportedly approving just 11 projects since August. However, the US branch of the global clean energy developer Repsol already saw the writing on the wall. On August 14, just one week after the Oregon decision came down, Repsol Renewables began submitting paperwork for the proposed Four Creeks Wind project.

“Repsol Renewables has identified northwest Peoria County as suitable for wind development because of a robust wind resource, its proximity to the existing electric transmission system with available capacity, compatibility with existing land use, and minimal anticipated environmental impacts,” Repsol summarized, with existing land use referring to provisions in the Peoria County Code that permit commercial wind farms in certain agricultural districts.

Although the wind farm involves a total of 27,000 acres on private land, each of the project’s 113 turbines will only account for less than one acre. The bulk of the land is dedicated to required setbacks as well as adequate spacing between the turbines.

Things are moving along at a relatively fast clip so far. On November 6, Peoria public radio station WCBU reported that the land use committee of the Peoria County Board recommended the Four Creeks project by a 6-3 vote. The project almost appeared to be dead in September when the Zoning Board of Appeals issued a 4-1 denial, but the Zoning Board also voted to include conditions for consideration by the full Peoria County Board at its meeting on November 5. The County Board approved the Four Creeks proposal on a split vote of 12-5 alongside a list of 36 restrictions mainly governing construction and operations.

Wind fans, don’t break out the champagne just yet. Repsol still has to apply for construction permits, and the entire process could take up to five years or so before shovels meet ground.

If and when Four Creeks materializes, Repsol is prepared to pour more than $193 million into the coffers of the Princeville, Brimfield, Williamsfield, and Stark County School Districts, with an additional $44.7 million in total property tax revenues for Peoria County and associated jurisdictions.

Repsol also draws attention to more than $69.4 million in total property tax revenues earmarked for libraries, fire districts, Illinois Central College, local road authorities, and the Greater Metro Airport Authority among other community resources.

Local property owners will also rake in millions of dollars in escalating lease payments.

As for objections to the new wind farm, WCBU reporter Joe Deacon highlighted remarks by Peoria County Board member Terry Ruhland, who acknowledged those who spoke against the  project at a public meeting.

“I don’t know how many people are in this room; I won’t even guess. But there is at least 179,000 people in Peoria County who are not in this room,” Rhuland said.

“Of that 179 [thousand], for every school kid who deserves the best education we can give them, and for every family that’s struggling to pay their utility bills, I’m going to vote yes,” Ruhland added.

Perhaps Ruhland was also thinking of Laborers Local 165, which has been working with Repsol for more than a year on developing a local workforce for the project. In a hearing before the Zoning Board in September, union representative Dane Simpson asserted that each wind turbine would account for approximately 3,000 hours of work.

Image: The global energy developer Repsol is on track to construct a 500-megawatt wind farm in Peoria County, Illinois, regardless of last year’s sharp U-turn in federal energy policy (screenshot, courtesy of Repsol).

#5818. Tesla Hasn’t Had A Successful New Product Launch Since JB Straubel Stepped Down As CTO

As has been widely reported, Tesla is about to end Cybertruck production. The Cybertruck has been a shocking failure. I’m sure there are more detailed analyses out there somewhere, but this short response on X from Ben McCombe that Tina Casey included in her article on the matter sums it up very well:

“Installed tooling to build 125k Cybertrucks a year.

“Made just $100m in gross profits from sales.

That’s pretty wild. A net project loss of around $2 billion. Now, I have to admit that I haven’t fact checked the numbers here, but what we know for certain is that Tesla planned to produce a lot of Cybertrucks per year and ended up producing a small fraction of that target. In fact, the annual sales target for the Cybertruck was higher than 125,000 — it was 250,000, with a goal of getting up to 500,000!

The Cybertruck was hyped up as having more reservations than any vehicle in history and was supposedly going to shatter records and transform the market. I warned people on many occasions that the reservation total was not a real reservation total and was definitely not a reliable indicator of demand because a) a reservation only required a $100 refundable deposit, and b) a ton of people made reservations after Elon Musk said that you could lock in the price of lifetime Full Self Driving when making a reservation (so, you’d only have to pay $6,000 for FSD no matter how much the cost of it went up in the following years). Anyway, the Cybertruck had other issues as well when it finally arrived. It cost about twice as much as it was supposed to cost and it had about half as much range as it was supposed to have. In other words, it was not even close to the vehicle it was supposed to be — its range per dollar cost was about ¼ what it was supposed to be.

The Cybertruck was supposed to be the next big thing after the Tesla Model Y. It was supposed to disrupt the gigantic US pickup truck market. The Model S was a superb and widely acclaimed car, if a bit more expensive than the mass market could afford. The Model S did in fact compete superbly well with the leaders in its segment, the BMW 7 Series and Mercedes S-Class. The Tesla Model X brought a similar offering in a larger package with some fun extra elements (falcon-wing doors, self-presenting doors, a third row). The Tesla Model 3 then completely shook the automotive world as a tremendous market phenomenon and perhaps the biggest product launch in history (in terms of total revenue). The Tesla Model Y was basically the Model 3 in a more popular crossover package and eventually became a much higher selling vehicle. In fact, it became the highest selling vehicle model of any kind in multiple years. Then JB Straubel stepped down as CTO.

Due to their EV passions, JB Straubel and Elon Musk were introduced to each other and had lunch together to talk about electric airplanes (Elon’s dream) and then electric cars (because JB talked more sense into Elon). Then the two of them were introduced to Marc Tarpenning and Martin Eberhard, who had founded Tesla Motors and just barely got started on the idea. After a while, Tarpenning and Eberhard left the company, and Musk and Straubel truly led its development and growth into an automotive superpower (as briefly described above). Since JB Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO of Tesla, however, if one thinks about it, it’s hard to identify any successful product launch from the company or much significant improvement at all.

Straubel left his CTO position in July 2019. The Model Y was unveiled in March 2019 and was about ⅔ the same as the Model 3. It would seem that getting the Model Y designed and prepared for launch was the last big project Straubel worked on. (Note that the Semi was unveiled in November 2017 but was long delayed due to battery supply, according to Elon Musk.) Since the Model Y, what significant product launch have we seen from Tesla? There was the Cybertruck, as we’ve already discussed. That was unveiled in November 2019. When you think about the timing now, did Straubel disagree strongly with Musk on the Cybertruck and decide it was time to move on when Musk pushed forward with his idea for an electric truck? It took another 4+ years for Tesla to start delivering the Cybertruck, in contrast to Model Y deliveries starting in January 2020, less than a year after the model was unveiled. More recently, the Cybercab has been developed, unveiled, and is now in production. A consumer car smaller and cheaper than the Model 3 was never developed and unveiled — just a stripped down, decontented Model 3 — and there basically haven’t been any other product unveilings since Straubel left his role as CTO. Elon Musk has published a couple of somewhat scattered, rambling “master plans,” but we haven’t gotten any significant new products aside from the Cybertruck and the just arriving Cybercab.

Even looking over on the energy storage side of things, the Tesla Megapack was launched in July 2019, the month Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO. Did Straubel just decide he had done enough once he had finished the Megapack launch and gotten the Model Y essentially ready for market? Did he have conflicts with Musk on where to take the company from there?

Yes, there’s been incremental product development and refreshed versions of Tesla cars and energy storage systems. There are still plenty of engineers working on improving things. However, there doesn’t seem to be the same leadership in new product development, and there also isn’t the same practical, moderating element that Straubel seemingly provided to Musk’s more wild, grandiose, sci-fi inspired dreams and ideas.

Perhaps this is all misunderstanding the roles of the two men to some extent. Perhaps things would not be much different if Straubel had stayed in the CTO role and had the same influence he had before leaving. He is still on the board of directors. (What that actually means is unclear, though.) Perhaps Tesla’s success and business would still be primarily based on the Model 3 and Model Y, without new transformative products. Or perhaps we would have had a more normal pickup truck from Tesla, an electric van, better batteries, a better market rollout of the Semi, and other products. Perhaps the Cybertruck, an epic product failure, never would have been shown to the public and hyped up unrealistically with specs and figures disconnected from what was going to be possible. Perhaps we’d have a Model C (affordable, compact Tesla) rather than a Cybercab. Perhaps Tesla would be a very different company.

Who knows? We don’t know the ins and outs of JB Straubel and Elon Musk’s relationship, or how much Straubel actually led Tesla in his CTO role. We don’t know how much Straubel tamped down Musk’s worst instincts or guided product development. We still really don’t even know why Straubel left. According to Tesla, it was so that he could focus more time on Redwood Materials, but why wasn’t that business within the umbrella of Tesla in the first place? Vertical integration has long been a key element of the company. Who knows? There’s a lot we don’t know and never will. However, what we do know is that since JB Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO of Tesla, we haven’t seen a significant, successful new product launch at the company. We’ll have to wait a bit longer to see if the Cybercab is one, and whether that sets a new, positive course for the future of the company or not.
