#5814. Even California’s Biggest Ports Aren’t Betting On Hydrogen For Ships

California has been one of hydrogen’s most enthusiastic supporters for two decades. The state has subsidized hydrogen vehicles, backed refueling networks, supported fuel-cell buses and trucks, invested in hydrogen production and kept the technology prominent in its zero-emission transportation policies. Los Angeles and Long Beach, the two enormous ports that anchor America’s Pacific container trade, have been part of that enthusiasm. So when these ports produce a comprehensive assessment of future marine fuels and leave hydrogen out of their detailed deep-sea shipping plans, it deserves attention well beyond California.

The Clean Marine Fuel Feasibility Assessment, released in October, is the product of a substantial examination of shipping fleets, carrier preferences, fuel availability and infrastructure costs. The ports recommend developing commercial methanol bunkering demonstrations, continuing existing ship-to-ship LNG bunkering arrangements and avoiding major new fuel terminals until demand becomes credible. Hydrogen, the molecule that California has spent so much money promoting for transportation, did not make the cut for detailed quantitative deep-sea fuel planning.

This isn’t a state government announcing the end of hydrogen subsidies. It is arguably more revealing. Two commercially important institutions have examined what the ships serving them are likely to use, considered the infrastructure they would have to provide and declined to spend the next decade preparing a major hydrogen bunkering business.

California’s hydrogen story has repeatedly confused technical possibility with commercial demand. Fuel-cell passenger cars proved they could operate, but struggled with expensive fuel, unreliable stations and battery-electric competition. Hydrogen buses and trucks have faced many of the same issues, often compounded by the economics of supplying fuel to fleets. Ports have supported hydrogen demonstrations because eliminating diesel exhaust matters enormously to nearby communities, but a successful demonstration does not establish that the resulting system is the best commercial solution.

Deep-sea shipping adds a particularly difficult set of constraints. A container vessel requires immense amounts of energy over long voyages. Hydrogen’s low volumetric energy density, expensive handling and demanding onboard storage make the fuel system difficult to accommodate economically. The problem extends beyond the ship to production, transportation, storage and bunkering equipment. There is little reason for a port authority to build a hydrogen fuel network when shipping lines are not ordering a commercially significant fleet that would use it.

The ports’ survey reinforces the commercial judgment. Of 38 shipping lines contacted, 19 responded. Nine expressed varying degrees of interest or commitment concerning methanol, but only one did so for hydrogen. That is a survey of carrier intentions, rather than binding fuel contracts, and the researchers examined other evidence as well. Still, it is difficult to turn one interested carrier into a compelling investment case for expensive hydrogen infrastructure.

The contrast with methanol is instructive. Methanol is a liquid under ordinary conditions and fits into a mature global chemical-handling industry. Existing storage and bunkering equipment can be adapted instead of requiring an entirely new cryogenic or high-pressure fuel network. Long Beach is already offering a $1 million incentive for an initial commercial-scale methanol bunkering operation, an approach that can test operational feasibility without committing to an enormous permanent facility.

I have a personal interest in that choice because I had to correct my own shipping-fuel forecast. Last year, I published Mea Culpa: Biomethanol Will Be A Major Shipping Fuel. I had previously given too much weight to vegetable-oil-derived fuels for shipping and insufficient weight to aviation’s competition for those feedstocks. I had also underestimated how far cheaper batteries could displace marine fuel demand. My revised assessment favours electricity for inland and short-sea shipping, with sustainable biomethanol becoming a primary liquid fuel for the remaining deep-sea requirement. The ports’ decision is consistent with preparing infrastructure for that possibility, although it does not establish that their future fuel supplies will be renewable.

The distinction is important because methanol is not inherently a climate solution. It can be manufactured from natural gas, coal, biomass or electricity-based production pathways, and those routes have radically different greenhouse gas consequences. California’s ports can build a technically successful methanol bunkering service without achieving substantial shipping decarbonization if the fuel delivered is fossil-based. That question belongs alongside the commercial case, not behind it.

LNG presents another caution. The ports are accommodating existing LNG-capable ships without recommending major new shoreside LNG infrastructure. It is a conservative capital decision, although fossil LNG’s climate case remains weak when methane leakage and engine slip are included. Reductions in sulfur and particulate emissions are useful, but they are not the same as meaningful lifecycle greenhouse gas reductions.

The larger significance of the study is the change in what gets taken seriously. For years, California’s hydrogen ambitions helped keep the molecule at the centre of transportation discussions. At Los Angeles and Long Beach, the planning process has produced a different hierarchy. Existing demand and affordable infrastructure determine the near-term investment choices. Hydrogen enthusiasm has not produced enough evidence to justify a deep-sea bunkering buildout.

There are weaknesses in the ports’ assessment, including the separation between bunkering readiness and verified emissions reductions, and the question of how electrification will change future fuel demand. Those deserve more scrutiny than a short article can provide. But the decision to leave hydrogen outside detailed deep-sea fuel planning is already consequential. California’s most important maritime gateways have applied a commercial filter that much of the state’s transportation hydrogen strategy has resisted.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines the ports’ fuel research, infrastructure costs, commercial demand estimates and emissions evidence, including where the study’s conclusions are sound and where its assumptions need more scrutiny.

#5813. ECA Power completes community solar project in town seeking revitalization

Employee-owned solar and energy storage developer ECA Power, Luminace, ComEd, construction partner Miller Bros., and local community leaders celebrated the energization of the 7.4-MWDC Rockford Community Energy Initiative (RCEI), a community solar project in Cherry Valley, Illinois.

“The Illinois General Assembly has spent the past several years passing legislation to encourage and incentivize renewable energy projects like this one,” said State Sen. Steve Stadelman. “It’s always exciting to work on legislation and then see that work translate into real development on the ground.”

“Northern Illinois is becoming a national leader in community solar, and projects like this are helping bring the benefits of clean energy to more families and businesses,” said Gil Quiniones, president and CEO of ComEd. “We’re proud to work with ECA Power and Luminace to connect projects like this to the grid, and build a power system that can support continued clean energy growth across our region.”

RCEI was built in a state-designated R3 area, bringing new investment to a community targeted for revitalization. As part of its community benefit agreement with the Village of Cherry Valley, ECA Power contributed $150,000 to support the buildout, operation and maintenance of an EV charging station.

“At ECA Power, we believe the success of a project should be measured not only by the energy it produces, but by the lasting value it creates for the surrounding community,” said Todd Fryatt, founder and CEO of ECA Power. “The Rockford Community Energy Initiative reflects that commitment by expanding access to affordable, locally generated energy while bringing meaningful investment to the region. From supporting local revitalization to our partnership with the Village of Cherry Valley, we’re proud to deliver a project that will benefit this community for years to come.”

#5812. Every virtual megawatt needs a meter

Last month, Google announced a deal with Voltus for a virtual power plant (VPP) of up to 1,070 MW in PJM, roughly the output of a large nuclear reactor. Rather than build a plant, Voltus will offer homes and businesses financial incentives to trim usage when the grid is strained, through smart thermostats, batteries and flexible equipment.

The deal represents an inflection point for the power and utilities sector, where “speed to power” innovations are defining the moment. It also underscores the importance of the equipment that will decide how far such programs go: the electric meter.

NEMA's 2026 Grid Reliability Study projects U.S. economy-wide electricity demand as much as 55% by 2050, with data center consumption alone growing 300% over the next decade. New generation and transmission can't keep pace, as permitting, capital needs and equipment and skilled labor backlogs protracting project delivery timelines.

As a result, operators are looking for capacity in the system they already have. On the transmission side, grid-enhancing technologies show what's possible: PJM modeling finds they could unlock between six and seven GW of usable capacity.

At the grid edge, the opportunity may be larger. Demand response contributed 33.3 GW of enrolled wholesale capacity in 2024, about 6.5% of peak demand in organized markets and could reach 20% of U.S. peak by 2030. Behind-the-meter resources, which include microgrids and aggregations of customer-sited resources (i.e., VPPs), are scaling too.

What these grid-edge resources share is a need for measurement. A demand response event or VPP dispatch only has value if someone can prove the load dropped and pay the customer who dropped it. That job falls to the electric meter.

Despite the momentum, only 6.5% of U.S. electricity customers are enrolled in retail demand response programs. NEMA's research points to several reasons: metering and communication rules written for large power plants that burden small participants, too little public data on how flexible resources perform during grid emergencies and market rules that vary by region.

Underneath them is a question of trust. Some grid operators and utilities still doubt that distributed resources will deliver when called. But confidence at scale requires consistent, verifiable measurement across millions of devices and many vendors.

That is the role of ANSI NEMA C12.1-2026. C12.1 sets performance criteria for new types of watthour and demand meters, defines accuracy classes and test methods and establishes acceptable in-service performance for revenue meters. When a meter reading decides whether a customer is paid for curtailment, or whether an operator can count an aggregation as capacity, those criteria make the number credible.

Timing makes this pressing. Millions of first-generation smart meters, many installed between 2010 and 2015 with American Recovery and Reinvestment Act funding, are reaching end of life. Utilities in every region are planning or executing large-scale replacements through the late 2020s, driven by aging hardware, obsolete communications and the need to support distributed energy resources, EVs and resilience. Indeed, as NEMA member company Honeywell has noted, first-wave meters lack the speed and accuracy of newer models and networks and security practices evolve faster than meters can be replaced. This is a replacement market, not a first deployment and the meters chosen now will likely stay in service for 15 to 20 years or more.

The 2026 edition of C12.1 arrives at the right moment. It folds the former C12.20 accuracy-class standard into a single document covering the entire metering code and adds guidance on non-Blondel metering. Referencing C12.1-2026 in procurement specifications gives utilities, regulators and vendors a common baseline for safety, accuracy and interoperability across vendors and networks and protects ratepayers' two-decade investment.

The same long-term lens applies to where grid equipment comes from. NEMA's Make It American® program is an industry-led, third party-certification program. Accredited auditors evaluate manufacturers against the NEMA 70901-2026 process standard and certified facilities and products are listed in a public Build America, Buy America registry. The Federal Highway Administration and the Texas, Arkansas and Montana transportation departments recognize it and a pending bill in the U.S. Senate features language treating Make It American documentation as sufficient evidence of domestic-content compliance.

NEMA has prioritized product-level specifications for the equipment utilities buy most, including high- and medium-voltage distribution equipment, grid management and automation equipment and wire and cable. That lets procurement teams verify domestic content product-by-product, with certification that reflects actual production practices.

Virtual power plants may be the fastest new capacity on the market. But a virtual megawatt is only as real as the meter that proves it was delivered.

#5808. The next wave of energy storage capacity is already on the grid

Next week, much of the industry will gather in Las Vegas for RE+ to talk about what gets built next. It’s an important conversation, but I’d encourage asset owners to bring a second question with them: what more can we get from what’s already been built?

This question matters more than ever as electricity demand climbs and customers feel it on their monthly bill. EIA expects consumption to grow another 3.1% in 2027, driven largely by data centers and other commercial loads, while residential customers will pay nearly 5% more for power this year.

The industry is working hard to add capacity, but getting it built takes time. According to Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory and GridTracker, the median project built in 2025 spent more than five years moving from interconnection request to commercial operation and only 13% of the capacity that requested interconnection between 2000 and 2020 had come online by the end of 2025. New generation is essential, but the fleet already operating is an opportunity we can’t afford to pass up.

With nearly 52 gigawatts (GW) of utility-scale battery storage operating in the U.S. as of mid-2026, the installed base is too large to overlook and performance across it varies widely. When a battery energy storage system (BESS) falls short, the instinct is often to blame the battery and reach for augmentation or replacement, but in many cases the bottleneck is the controls, software and integration around it.

Higher prices should raise the value of delivering power when and where the grid needs it, yet revenues for many operating assets are moving in the other direction. In ERCOT, the average battery earned 84% less in 2025 than at its 2023 peak, as more storage competed for the same services. With incentive economics shifting too, asset managers face real pressure to get more from existing assets.

At the same time, utilities are leaning on storage for grid stability. As more gas units face revenue pressure and retirement, the grid loses the spinning mass that provides inertia in the first moments of a disturbance. Grid-forming inverters can replace that inertia, often by upgrading existing storage rather than building new. Providing that support, alongside peak shaving, load shifting and voltage and frequency regulation, takes controls that respond faster than many legacy SCADA and EMS platforms were built to handle.

Compliance requirements are moving just as quickly, with FERC Order No. 901 directing NERC to develop new reliability standards for inverter-based resources, including batteries, through 2030. Many plants operating today were designed before those requirements existed, increasing the importance of controls that can evolve as grid codes change. The same logic also applies to solar and wind plants. Once control functions are proven in the field they can easily be applied to other assets across the whole market.

A recent Gridmatic study of 30 California batteries shows what’s at stake, with fleetwide availability averaging just under 80% and execution and bidding strategy driving a wide gap between top and bottom performers. Had every asset matched the leader, the group would have earned an estimated $98 million more over two years. The lesson isn't that every performance gap can be solved with software. It's that owners shouldn't assume the battery itself is the limiting factor.

Before committing additional capital, owners should examine the full system: controls, software, market strategy, integration and plant-level coordination alongside battery health and hardware performance.

Think about the smartphone in your pocket. Its hardware may be several years old, but software updates can improve how it performs and add capabilities that weren’t available when you bought it. Storage assets aren't so different: the physical equipment may still have years of useful life, while the controls and software around it can evolve to meet new market and grid requirements.

A modern energy management and plant control platform layered onto existing assets can help owners:

We saw this firsthand in the U.K., where our team, then part of Wärtsilä, worked with Zenobē to retrofit an existing 100 MW/100 MWh battery site with a new GEMS power plant controller. The upgrade took over energy management, secured grid compliance and added stateful grid-code functions such as de-load, along with control for multiple tenants sharing one point of interconnection.

The batteries stayed in place, but the site gained new capabilities and is better prepared for future requirements. That same approach translates to U.S. markets, where interconnection rules and local regulations are evolving quickly.

New deployments will remain critical, but the next wave of value may come from making today’s fleet work harder. As the industry gathers at RE+, owners should ask not just what they need to build next, but what more the assets already on the grid could deliver. Before replacing hardware or adding capacity, diagnose the whole plant. The constraint may not be the battery cells.

#5807. Top Selling Electric Vehicles in the World — 32% EV Share in September!

Plugin vehicle registrations were up 3% year over year (YoY) globally in September, ending the month at around 2.16 million units, pretty darn close to the all-time record (2.17 million) set last December. Once again, BEVs (+8% YoY) and PHEVs (-9% YoY) are experiencing opposite dynamics. Pure electrics actually set a record high of 1.54 million registrations.

This means that, while plugin vehicle YTD growth remains low (+4% YoY), that is solely due to the PHEV blues (-10% YoY). BEVs are on their way back to normal (+12%).

Share-wise, September saw BEVs end the month at 23% share, with the plugin tally increasing to 32% if we add in PHEVs. This near-record performance pulled the 2026 plugin share to higher levels. BEVs are now at 18%, while plugin hybrids are at 7%. Therefore, 2026 EV share is now at 25%.

Looking at the best selling models, there’s some news. The leader Tesla Model Y hit a new all-time record (165,000 units), thanks to a 17% YoY growth rate. That larger six-seat version is doing wonders here.

The runner-up spot went to the Tesla Model 3. Despite being down 4% YoY, it picked up enough deliveries (65,000) to surpass both the small Geely Xingyuan, also known as the E2 or EX2 in export markets, and the BYD Song.

Regarding Geely’s hatchback, the export highlights were Brazil (2,724 units), Australia (1,211), Indonesia (1,890), and Thailand (1,759).

Despite being relegated to 4th, the BYD Song was actually the fastest grower among the top 5, rising 19% YoY and reaching 54,519 registrations. This was due to an export push (the highlights being Brazil — 5,892 units, Italy — 2,134 units, and Germany — 2,028 units) and the new-generation Ultra body. Flash charging capabilities promise to recharge the Song’s sales and make it a serious candidate, not only for the silver medal in 2026, but also as strong competition to the Tesla Model Y in off-peak months.

In a near-record month, records were plenty, with six models in the top 20 reaching record highs. Besides the aforementioned Tesla Model Y, all the other five record scorers were compact models — a coincidence or an indication of where the market is going?

The five record performers of September were:

Finally, a mention goes out to the surprise appearance of the Geely Starray / Galaxy 7, in 19th, thanks to 15,761 registrations, its best score in 20 months. With the compact PHEV crossover currently having a rather discreet career in China, export markets are giving it a new life, with the strongest markets being Australia (1,254 units in September), the UK (3,100), and … Russia (900).

Outside the top 20, there are a few models that deserve a mention. The big highlight was the landing with a bang of the MG 07, a fastback that kind of reminds me of the Porsche Taycan and Xiaomi SU7, but sits one category below, looking to compete with the Tesla Model 3 and the BYD Seal 06. It is using its sleeker silhouette to distance itself from those options. And with over 14,000 units in its landing month, one can say that MG’s new baby could become top 20 material soon.

Other Chinese EVs are also ramping up, like the Chery QQ3 (13,000 units), Xpeng L03 (12,000), BYD’s new Great Tang flagship SUV (11,000), and the Leapmotor A05 (B03 in export markets — 12,000 units). With Leapmotor on a roll, having placed two models in the top 20 in September, of all these models ramping up, I am betting that the small Leapmotor will be the one that will sit higher on the table.

Year to date, the leader Tesla Model Y is really in its own league, selling twice as many units as the runner-up BYD Song. The Model Y is so ubiquitous now that when regular people imagine what an electric car is, they think of it. Tesla’s crossover is the gold standard of EVs.

In the remaining podium positions, the #2 BYD Song and #3 Geely Xinguan held their positions, following the September high tide month of the Model 3. This is good news for both Chinese EVs. It seems the Model 3’s sales erosion continues (down 7% this year) despite the current EV sales rush caused by the future 2026 EV Salesperson of the Year, DJT.

Having said that, with 16,000 units separating Tesla’s Model 3 from the silver medal, not all is set in stone. But if I had to bet, I would say that BYD’s bread and butter model will stay in 2nd, closely followed by the Geely hatchback, and then the Model 3 ending in 4th.

(Adding context to this race, the Tesla Model 3 has been on every podium since 2018, while the Geely Xingyuan still hasn’t won any medal.)

Elsewhere, there were five changes in the table, with the first being the premium arm of BYD, Fang Cheng Bao, pulling its Land Rover Defender fighter (25,000 euros cheaper — this thing could be a huge success in Europe) into the 8th position. Below it, the Leapmotor A10/B03X continued its rise, with the crossover climbing to 12th.

Below the small Leapmotor, three compact crossovers were also on the rise. The BYD Yuan Plus/Atto 3 jumped two positions, to 14th, while the Nevo Q05 was up to 16th and its premium cousin, the Deepal S05, joined the table in 19th.

Compact crossovers are hot right now, so lucky for Changan to have two of the most competitive examples in its lineup. True, BYD also has two representatives in that category (Sealion 05 and Yuan Plus), but then again, that would have been expected — the depth of options that the Shenzhen make has is so big that the difficulty is finding a hole in BYD’s lineup. (I have a suggestion: affordable sports car? Like a Tesla Cybercab, but with pedals and a steering wheel?)

One of the biggest highlights was #2 Tesla having a record month (232,000 units, +9% YoY), but in a near record month, it wasn’t the only one to post strong results.

In fact, there were eight makes posting record results and four others performing year-best results.

#3 Geely also had something to celebrate in September, with its 130,000 registrations representing its best score of the year, mostly thanks to the fact that the Geely Xingyuan had some siblings to share the burden with this time (the Starray — 16,000 units; and the EX5 — 14,000 units). With B and C segments now covered, all they need is a strong seller in the D-segment.

Leapmotor continued on its record streak, with strong sellers in the B-segment (A10 and A05), C-segment (B10), and D-segment (C10). If there is anything currently holding back the startup, it is that the company lacks the dealership scale of BYD or Geely. But give it time….

Below the top four, we have something big happening — the rise and rise of Toyota (61,332 registrations, its fourth record in a row), which allowed it to end ahead of Volkswagen. Again. So, the Japanese globetrotter finished the month as the best selling legacy make. Again. All this despite Volkswagen placing a year-best result of 58,000 units.

Here, also, the establishment is being challenged, with the longtime leader of legacy brands being surpassed by a player that many still consider a sleeping giant.

And it’s not only Toyota’s RAV4 PHEV and BZ4X contributing to the volumes, as its Chinese operations are also delivering (the BZ3X compact crossover was the company’s third model to deliver over 10,000 units in September).

Another surprise was MG’s strong result, ending the month in 7th thanks to a record 57,000 registrations. Building on the MG 4 hachback’s success, if the MG 7 becomes the roaring success that its order list (over 50,000 units) and landing month point to, then Volkswagen will have yet another brand to worry about….

The #9 Kia is also on a record streak, having ended the month in 9th with some 50,000 registrations. With a freshly developed BEV lineup, the Korean is now sowing the seeds of an early investment in dedicated BEV platforms.

Still in Legacy Land, Mercedes has a similar story to Kia. With an ambitious BEV development plan, the German make is finally pushing its sales into record territory (40,000 units), and with new models coming in (GLA EV, C-Class EV, VLE, etc.), expect the Stuttgart brand to start getting in the rearview mirror of arch rival BMW. Maybe that pushes the Bavarian into more quickly deploying the next generation of BEVs (i4, iX1, iX2, i1, i2…).

But it seems BMW is in no hurry. I mean, even Audi had a year best result in September (34,000 units). BMW? Not even that.

The remaining highlights belonged to Chinese makes, with #15 Zeekr and #18 Chery posting record highs while #16 Fang Cheng Bao delivered year-best results. The highlight was Chery. Once known for being an ICE-heavy brand, it is now benefitting from the success of its new QQ3 EV (13,000 units) and joining the EV best seller rankings.

As for the year-to-date table, there was no major news in the top four positions, which already seem to be secured through the end of the year. However, below it, things are changing.

Rising Toyota surpassed Wuling and is now 6th. The Japanese make is some 20,000 units behind #5 Volkswagen, and while that might sound like a lot, there are still three months to go. With Toyota breaking records every passing month, it seems possible to shorten the distance by some 7,000 units every month and end ahead of Volkswagen by the end of December. To be continued…

Two more Chinese brands went up: SAIC’s MG jumped two positions, to #10, while Changan’s Qiyuan/Nevo mainstream brand joined the table at #20, much thanks to the success of the Q05 crossover.

Looking at OEMs, BYD (20.2%, down 0.1%) is stable in the lead, while runner-up Geely (9.8%, down from 9.9% in August) is holding a good distance over 3rd placed Tesla (8.7%, up 0.4% from August).

#4 Volkswagen Group (7%) is stable in 4th, just like SAIC (6.2%) is in 5th.

Behind the top 5, though, things are shaking up. Rising #6 Leapmotor (4.4%, up from 4.3% in August) is safe, but behind it, #7 Hyundai–Kia (3.9%, down 0.1%) is now being closely followed by #8 Chery (3.9%, up 0.1%). With just 3,000 units separating the two, we might see the Chinese OEM surpass the Korean in Q4.

Another OEM on the rise is Toyota (3%, up 0.1%), which joined the top 10 at the expense of BMW (2.9%, down 0.1%).

Looking just at BEVs, there were about 10.8 million registrations so far this year, or 71% of total plugin sales. A year ago, BEVs represented just 66% of plugin sales, so the PHEV slowdown is quite visible.

BYD (15.8%, up 0.3%) is at the top, benefitting from the OEM’s recent shift into BEVs. It is followed by Tesla (12.2%, up 0.6%). In 3rd place, we have Geely (8.8%), which is also safe in the last place of the podium, so the medal positions should be decided by now.

Meanwhile, there was a position change below the podium, with SAIC (6.3%) surpassing Volkswagen Group (6.3%, down 0.1%), so the Shanghai OEM is the new 4th placed. But with fewer than 4,000 units separating the two, anything can still happen.

Outside the top 5, Leapmotor (4.6%, up from 4.5% in August) climbed into the 6th position, at the expense of Hyundai–Kia (4.4% share, down 0.1%).

As an Easter Halloween Egg, here are the top sellers in the pickup truck, MPV, and sports car categories:

With the Ford F-150 EV now part of the past, a new pickup king is among us, and it is part of BYD’s stable. With the following two models selling roughly half of its volume, and a China market entry said to be coming soon, expect BYD’s Shark model to rule this category in the coming years.

Having said that, a certain Toyota Hilux BEV is ramping up, having delivered around the same number of units in September as the Cybertruck and the Ranger, so expect the 2027 race for the podium to be more entertaining, especially if a certain Slate ramps up production without major hiccups.

Now, this was a surprise. Media-friendly VW ID.Buzz? Way behind, at roughly half of the current winner. Giant Chinese minivans? The best one is only 3rd, closely followed by the Voyah Dreamer. MPVs are mostly surviving extinction because of Asian markets, and it shows. The category leader is a midsize MPV, and while its main market is its domestic China, others, like Thailand and Indonesia, where it’s known as Darion, also provide significant volumes to Wuling’s MPV.

But an even bigger surprise is the 2nd placed Vinfast MPV 7 (Limo Green in ride hailing/taxi spec). The compact seven seater, which is a sort of MPV disguised as a crossover (not unlike certain NIO designs), has most of its sales coming from native Vietnam, but some export markets are also contributing to the volume, like India, Indonesia, and the Philippines.

This is a category in crisis. With no new players; the 2026 winner, the Dodge Charger, experiencing crashing sales (down 89% YoY); and the Ferrari 296 also suffering from shrinking sales, the only highlight is the Mercedes CLE, which went from 3rd last year to it current uncontested leadership position.

Still, expect this category to receive some much needed fresh metal in 2027, like the Denza Z (of course BYD had to get into the action…), the Porsche 718 twins (Boxster and Cayman), Audi’s take on the Porsche 718 (Audi TT?), and the next-generation Alpine A110.

Always interested in the auto industry, particularly in electric cars, Jose has pioneered on documenting the plug-in sales evolution through the EV Sales blog, allowing him to gain an expert view on where EVs are right now and where they are headed in the future. Extending that work and expertise, Jose was co-founder of EV-Volumes and currently works with the European Alternative Fuels Observatory on EV sales matters.

#5804. Bigger than Godzilla!

Fortescue’s plans for the Pilbara include wind turbine blades that are bigger than Godzilla.

While Fortescue’s boss hobnobs with King Charles III and chats about sustainability, under the Commonwealth Nature Initiative, progress continues towards real zero in the remote Pilbara of Western Australia. See the video of Andrew and Charles here.

As part of Dr Andrew Forrest’s vision of real zero by 2030, Fortescue is installing wind turbines with a blade length of 89 metres. Bigger than Godzilla! Have we got your attention now? The blades will be deployed at the Nullagine Wind Farm in the Pilbara region of Western Australia. They will arrive by ship at the Port Headland project site. Fortescue has done major upgrades to allow the blades easy access and to minimise impact on local traffic. The community will also receive regular updates throughout the delivery program to keep residents informed.

For global readers, I would like to point out that Fortescue’s mining operations and land tenements in the Pilbara region of Western Australia cover more than 87,000 square kilometres (34,000 square miles). This is roughly the size of Austria (83,879 km²), Ireland (84,421 km²), or Serbia (88,361 km²), and more than twice the size of countries like the Netherlands (41,850 km²) or Switzerland (41,285 km²). And for USA readers, Fortescue’s land holdings alone are larger than South Carolina, West Virginia, or Maryland, and roughly the same size as the entire state of Maine or Indiana. But only one eighth the size of Texas.

You will have read about Twiggy Forrest’s achievements and vision of real zero mining operations here and here. Fortescue is taking steps to educate the public on decarbonisation. You need to take the people with you, especially the shareholders. Here is a great video produced by Fortescue to show how to decode decarbonisation. The Godzilla-sized wind turbines are part of an ecosystem. They will provide the power to recharge the electric dump trucks. But just how do you recharge a 240 ton dump truck in a timely manner? Using 6MW robotic fast charging, of course, thus eliminating 26% of Fortescue’s emissions — and saving a dump truck of money in the process.

CleanTechnica reported on the acquisition of Williams Racing 5 years ago, in order for Fortescue to access Williams’ fast-charging technology. It has taken some time, but it looks like everything is coming together. I still have the picture in my head, inspired by this acquisition, of huge dump trucks doing F1 races in the red dust of the Australian Outback. Beyond the fantasies of Mad Max, even. While we are indulging in mental gymnastics, the Fortescue video tells us that 6MW of charging is like boiling 1,875 electric kettles all at once. That’s a lot of tea or coffee! A less than 30-minute charge will give 4 hours of operation for the truck. The charging equipment has to be resilient, as the Pilbara is hot and dusty, with temperatures in summer over 40 degrees and down to below freezing on winter nights.

Episode 2 answers the question “Can a green grid power a mining operation 24/7?” It’s a big ask. Fortescue is the fourth largest iron ore producer in the world, exporting 200 million tons a year. The advantages: Fortescue achieves cost savings, does good for the climate, and keeps control of its energy. In the current global situation, I would add energy security.

The Pilbara has 300 days a year of irradiation and Fortescue plans to harvest that with a planned 1.5 gigawatts of solar farms. Eventually, this will be backed up with 900 MW wind and balanced by 5 GWh of battery storage — enough electricity to power 800,000 homes on solar alone. This is all connected by a high-voltage green grid consisting of 600 km of transmission lines. “A city scale power system in the middle of nowhere.” The green operation does not draw from the public grid; not the distant future, but right now.

Fortescue is not just content to reduce emissions for its mining operations; it is also working on green steel — a product for the future. Fortescue reports that it has produced its first hot metal via an electric smelting process in a pilot project at Christmas Creek. Initially using a blended feedstock, as the commissioning process continues, it is expected to be able to handle Pilbara iron ore. Larger scale production is the aim. At the moment, the focus is on testing, refining, and optimising the process.

Fortescue Metals Chief Executive Officer Dino Otranto said: “This is a significant milestone for our Green Metal Project and another step towards producing commercial-scale green metal in Australia.

“For decades Australia has exported iron ore to the world. The next opportunity is to create more value from that ore by producing green metal here at home.

“The real opportunity goes beyond green metal. It’s about building a new industrial economy around Australia’s renewable energy advantage.

“Nobody has solved green metal production using Pilbara ore at commercial scale yet. That’s the challenge we’re taking on.”

I’m looking forward to more good news of progress towards real zero from Fortescue Metals. Have CleanTechnica readers noticed any other mining companies heading down the same path? If so, make a comment below. Even in the “hard to decarbonise” mining sector, electricity sourced from renewables and stored in batteries is the way to go for cost saving, climate protection, and keeping control of energy inputs. In Australia’s Pilbara, the future is bright and electric.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5802. Beyond megawatts: Nuclear’s other capacity expansion challenge

Nuclear growth plans are accelerating as data centers, large-load customers, uprates, restarts, extended operations and new generation projects reshape the industry’s outlook. But the challenge of capacity expansion extends far beyond megawatts. Every utility must also expand the capacity of its workforce.

The workforce challenge is not simply about hiring more people. Recruiting is critical, but so are retention strategies, qualification timelines, knowledge transfer processes and the availability of experienced employees and contractors. Utilities must continue operating existing plants safely and reliably while extending asset life, modernizing infrastructure, preparing for growth and developing the next generation of workers. In many cases, the same people are asked to do all of it.

An experienced engineer supporting an uprate may also be needed for aging management reviews. The supervisor helping qualify new employees may also be responsible for outage preparation. The skilled craft resources needed for capital projects are often the same resources supporting ongoing plant reliability and maintenance activities. To execute an ambitious growth plan, your workforce needs support and clear expectations as it navigates the demands of the present and the future.

That reality makes employee engagement and retention critical operational necessities.

Across the industry, organizations are managing a growing list of demands. Employees are operating plants, supporting outages, resolving equipment issues, onboarding new hires, transferring knowledge, implementing modernization efforts and preparing for future growth. Subsequent license renewal and long-term operations programs add further demands through inspections, evaluations, documentation and equipment replacement activities.

At the same time, many organizations continue to face shortages in experienced operators, engineers, technicians and other critical roles. Long qualification timelines and retirements only increase the pressure.

Growth introduces another challenge that is less visible but equally important: cognitive load. Experienced employees must continually sort through competing priorities, interpret changing guidance and determine where to focus limited time and attention. When this becomes a constant state, organizations risk change fatigue, burnout and disengagement.

For nuclear leaders, the consequences extend beyond employee experience. The loss of experienced personnel can slow qualification programs, reduce knowledge transfer, limit an organization’s ability to respond to emergent issues and increase execution risk across both life-extension and capacity-expansion efforts.

As a result, workforce planning must address not only how organizations attract talent but also how they retain, support and fully leverage the people already in place.

Three leadership responsibilities can help organizations strengthen workforce capacity while navigating growth and modernization: simplify priorities, enable leaders and engage employees.

A common challenge in growing organizations is that new priorities are added without older priorities disappearing. Individually, each initiative may be justified, but collectively, they can create confusion about what matters most.

Organizations should begin with an integrated view of operational commitments, major projects and workforce capacity. Leaders must identify where critical resources are supporting multiple high-priority activities and make deliberate decisions about sequencing, staffing, or scope rather than assuming employees can absorb additional work indefinitely.

One nuclear organization recently addressed this issue by consolidating multiple layers of strategic imperatives into a small number of employee-facing priorities. These priorities allowed employees to make a clearer connection between their daily work and fleet strategy, while leaders retained the layers behind the scenes. Simplifying the message at the right level reduced complexity without reducing rigor.

Even clear priorities require interpretation. Supervisors and managers play a critical role in helping employees understand what work matters most, where trade-offs exist and how decisions connect to organizational goals.

To do this effectively, leaders need more than communication materials. They need context, practical guidance, opportunities for discussion and the authority to resolve or escalate conflicts when competing priorities emerge.

Workforce plans should also acknowledge the time experienced employees spend mentoring, coaching and qualifying newer workers. Organizations cannot develop future capability if their most knowledgeable employees are fully consumed by current demands. Creating capacity for knowledge transfer is an investment in future operational performance.

Employees closest to the work usually identify constraints long before they appear on a project dashboard or management report.

Whether discussing outage activities, modernization efforts, operational improvements, or growth initiatives, leaders should regularly seek employee input on assumptions, risks, bottlenecks and execution challenges. Just as importantly, organizations must demonstrate how that feedback influences decisions.

When employees see concerns acknowledged and acted upon, trust grows. When feedback disappears into a process without visible outcomes, engagement often declines.

One utility has invested in dedicated engagement coordinators to help leaders stay more connected to employee feedback, concerns and ideas. By creating additional channels for communication, sense-making and listening, the organization is strengthening its ability to identify issues early and improve execution across the fleet.

Delivering the next generation of nuclear capacity requires leaders to address workforce capacity constraints that go beyond the ability to hire and train new employees. Nuclear organizations must also create space for the existing workforce to sustain today’s operations while helping build tomorrow’s future.

By simplifying competing priorities, equipping leaders to translate strategy into daily work and engaging employees as active contributors to change, nuclear organizations can strengthen retention, protect institutional knowledge and create the workforce capacity needed for growth.

The people being recruited to support tomorrow’s nuclear expansion will depend on the knowledge, experience and commitment of the people doing the work today. The most sustainable growth strategies recognize that retaining and engaging those employees is not separate from capacity expansion. It is a prerequisite for it.

#5801. Solar and storage companies voice support for federal permitting reform

Hundreds of solar and storage companies have co-signed a letter addressed to Congress in support of the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026 (BAAJA) (S. 5653).

“You can’t lower energy costs if you can’t build the energy projects, and these companies know that this bipartisan legislation will help get America building again,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA), in a press release.

BAAJA was introduced in the Senate on Sept. 30 by Sen. Shelly Moore Capito (R-WV), and co-sponsored by Sen. Mike Lee (R-UT), Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse (D-RI) and Sen. Martin Heinrich (D-NM). The legislation is focused on quickening permitting for a range of projects with federal oversight, many of them related to energy production. BAAJA specifically calls out solar photovoltaic systems, energy storage and on- and offshore wind, but also includes language for fossil fuel sources, such as natural gas, oil and coal.

If passed, the Senate bill would amend sections of the National Environmental Policy Act, Clean Water Act and Endangered Species Act of 1973 with the goal of shortening project permitting windows. According to the text, it would change and reduce which projects would need to be reviewed under those acts, such as water quality certifications; shorten the length of review periods for projects; and would not require permits for oil and gas drilling on land not owned by the federal government.

BAAJA would also grant the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission the ability to issue permits for some electricity transmission projects.

According to a SEIA analysis, if BAAJA was passed, solar energy output could increase by 167 GW between 2028 and 2035. The companies that signed this letter to Congress believe that federal permitting practices are outdated, and permitting reform is a solution to lowering energy costs.

“The message from more than 300 companies comes as voters made clear in the midterm elections that lowering energy costs is a top concern,” the group stated in a press release. “Permitting reform is urgently needed to meet rising demand and get more reliable, affordable solar and storage projects built.”

BAAJA is currently under review by the Committee on Energy and Natural Resources.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5799. The Jackery HomePower 2000 Plus v2 Red Cross Edition — CleanTechnica Tested

Jackery was one of the earliest portable power station producers to bring these beautiful creations into the mainstream. They have a line of portable power stations and proprietary portable, foldable solar panels that continues to make them one of the top go-to options for anyone looking for power on the go.

Recently, they launched a Red Cross Edition of their popular HomePower 2000 Plus v2 that donates a portion of the profits to the Red Cross to support first responders. We covered these limited edition units when they first hit our radar during our visit to Jackery’s headquarters in Shenzhen, China, and now they are finally moving into production.

Jackery donates 10% of profits from this series to donate portable power stations to rescue organizations and emergency response teams, supporting disaster relief and recovery efforts. At the volumes Jackery is producing, that’s a meaningful step in the right direction to putting portable power stations into the hands of the first responders who need them most.

Disclaimer: Jackery provided the Red Cross edition of the 2000 Pro V2 portable power station to the author for the purposes of this review.

Charging up the unit is a simple matter thanks to its ability to pull a charge from multiple sources. Right out of the gate, you can charge from a normal 120V AC wall outlet at around 1,600 watts. This is typically the maximum you can safely charge from any wall outlet without overheating cables or outlets, and the Jackery HomePower 2000 Plus V2 makes full use of it. In our testing, we pulled 1,590W from the wall for a full charge from empty in an hour and a half.

You can also charge up the unit with solar panels, which with 800 watts of solar can recharge the battery from fully drained to overflowing in 3.2 hours. Jackery uses a proprietary solar connector, so keep that in mind before you buy your battery and/or panels.

You can also charge it up via your car’s 12V automotive outlet (requires optional car charging adapter for $29.90), which can push power out at about 100 watts. This varies slightly from car to car, but the fact of the matter is that you’re generally limited based on the factory wiring for what that outlet can handle.

Jackery knew about that, so they developed their own DC-to-DC charger for combustion vehicles. This optional accessory costs $239, but taps into the full power of your alternator and can pull up to 600 watts. This is a faster charge and can top up the battery in about 4 hours, but requires a bit of extra planning and work and assumes you’re running a combustion vehicle. It’s worth consulting with a professional if you’re going to go this route, as alternator wiring and max output varies greatly from car to car.

Electric vehicles typically don’t have a 12V outlet, opting for USB-C instead. Unfortunately, the Jackery HomePower 2000 Plus V2 Red Cross Edition is not able to charge up via USB-C at any power level. Fortunately, many electric cars come with integrated AC outlets, which can be used to charge the battery up in a pinch.

I tested this in my Cybertruck and achieved similar charging rates to what I was able to achieve at home, which tops the battery up in about an hour and a half. This is a great option to use while fast charging on a road trip because you can charge for about 30 minutes at a time to keep your portable power station charged up without having to run the inverter while driving, which drains the battery faster.

When it’s time to tap into all that stored power, the Jackery HomePower 2000 Plus v2 Red Cross Edition is ready. It can push out 2,400 watts of continuous power with surge capability of up to 4,800W. Given that this is a compact 2 kWh unit weighing just 21 lb, that’s a pretty solid level of power output that it can handle. Power output has continuously been improving with each successive generation and Jackery continues to be near the top of the pack with its numbers.

It’s obviously up to the task of powering lightweight items like drones, laptops, portable lights, charging radios, keeping the internet on, and even powering your fridge in an emergency. On the home front, it’s also a great option. Thanks to its LFP chemistry, Jackery expects you to get 6,000 charging cycles out of this battery, which equates to more than 16 years of daily cycling.

Even though it is such a compact unit, it should be able to keep essentials running at home — including your refrigerator, freezer, computer, lights, smartphone, Wi-Fi, etc. — for nearly 10 hours. That should get you through most outages, and, honestly, if you’re having longer outages than that, you probably want to look into a home battery system. Or you can tap into the HomePower 2000 Plus v2 Red Cross Edition’s ace up its sleeve….

The Jackery HomePower 2000 Plus V2 Red Cross edition can actually add more capacity. It has a special port on the left side of the unit that lets you plug in any of Jackery’s Battery Plus expansion batteries for up to 12kWh of total capacity. That’s a 6× increase over this unit’s installed capacity and lets you scale your storage capacity as you find new use cases for it.

The use case for this at home is really keeping the lights and and the refrigerator running during an emergency and using solar or a generator to charge up when it runs low. It can absorb up to 800 watts of solar from an attached array, meaning you can fully charge it in about an hour and a half depending on the sunlight you’re getting. Even in terrible conditions, it’s still going to charge up in just a couple of hours.

We put this beast to use running a portable refrigerator, lights, USB, charging our laptop and phones, and even charging a few portable batteries for us to take with us hiking. This unit kept our devices charged up for several days of fun in the desert. It was great knowing that after we were done charging our devices, the battery would top itself up again from the attached solar panels.

No matter how many devices we plugged in, including our usual kitchen torture test, its 2,400W of power output was more than enough to keep everything running. The surge power is pretty cool too because it allows the unit to absorb the spikes in consumption that come from a lot of devices when you first turn them on.

But we wanted more. So we pulled out some new winter heaters and really made the Jackery sweat. Each of these heating units can pull up to 1,500 watts each, which is about the max you can get from a standard 15A outlet. We plugged both into the Jackery and cranked them up to the max. I’m personally thrilled to have more energy-hungry appliances to torture — I mean test — portable power stations with.

Playing with all of their settings, we had them pulling almost 3,100 watts constantly before the Jackery finally decided to throw in the towel. It kept them running for several minutes over its own 2,400 watt constant power output rating, which was impressive, especially considering how compact this thing is.

It’s exciting to see Jackery continuing to push into new battery technologies, but in this case, this is the same battery they sell as a normal unit, just rebranded to highlight the great work being done at the Red Cross and to give some of the profits back in the form of product.

That’s worth celebrating, and it’s exciting to see Jackery leading the charge to enable first responders with technology without charging governments or consumers more money for the same product.

Or maybe you just like a bright red unit that’ll make it hard to lose or forget. Whether you’re heading out on a camping trip or heading back to the house after a long week out in nature, the bright red colorway of this unit is sure to stand out.

For more information about the Jackery HomePower 2000 Plus Red Cross Edition or to purchase one for yourself, head over to Jackery’s official website.

I'm a tech geek passionately in search of actionable ways to reduce the negative impact my life has on the planet, save money and reduce stress. Live intentionally, make conscious decisions, love more, act responsibly, play. The more you know, the less you need. As an activist investor, Kyle owns long term stock holdings in Tesla and Rivian.

#5795. Behind-the-meter power: An effective tool for bringing large utility customers online faster

Data centers and AI infrastructure are intensifying the strain on existing power grids across the U.S.

Large-load facilities are built quickly, only to face years-long waits for grid interconnection and new generation capacity, such as gas turbines. Behind-the-meter (BTM) power is becoming an effective solution to bridge the gap between what the grid can provide and what data centers need to operate smoothly.

Utilities and large-scale customers alike are embracing BTM power: 80 percent of utilities view BTM power generation as permanent infrastructure and expect it to serve a significant share of large-load demand by 2030, according to Bloom Energy’s 2026 Utilities Insights Report. Nearly two-thirds of data center developers cite onsite power as their primary response when grid timelines slip. “BTM addresses a major issue, which is avoiding increases in grid retail prices,” says Nik Nunes, Chief Executive Officer at Industrial Development Funding (IDF), a top investment partner of Bloom Energy. “For that reason, several governors have come out in support of it.”

BTM power isn’t always a simple fix. It involves complications related to regulatory requirements, power storage and permitting. Yet, when used in tandem with local utilities in front of meter power resources, it can provide an effective bridge for bringing large-scale customers online faster, helping them bypass lengthy wait times for a grid connection. BTM can also be affordable over the long term relative to other power sources.

To launch a successful BTM power effort, data center developers must address growing public concerns about noise, water usage and emissions. In many jurisdictions, permitting has become a challenge, and BTM power projects are often delayed. To simplify air permitting, it’s helpful for BTM solutions to produce few or no combustion-related emissions. Bloom Energy fuel cells run on natural gas today and are fuel-flexible—they can also operate on biogas, hydrogen or blends. They generate energy without combustion, cutting criteria pollutants like NOx and SOx to near zero. Other factors that can simplify permitting include minimal water use, low noise and a small footprint. “Prioritizing these needs can ease local zoning and community concerns,” Nunes says.

Utility coordination is another complication for developers. Depending on the data center’s location, developers must align with local interconnection and compliance requirements to ensure safety and regulatory compliance. To streamline permitting, large-scale utility customers should look for a BTM power solution that adheres to local regulations.

BTM power systems emerged to solve a problem: the power gap between the average time to construct a data center (18–24 months) and the average time to achieve a new grid interconnection (4–7 years). Large-load customers could wait years for a grid connection, making BTM power a much-needed bridge.

But BTM power is more than a stopgap solution. Today’s utility leaders see BTM power generation as a permanent fixture in the modern grid. Many developers do not plan to shut down BTM power operations immediately upon achieving grid interconnection. Instead, they intend to continue operating in tandem with the grid. “Utilities are considering BTM as a permanent solution to supplement the grid, given extended equipment lead times, interconnection queues and the expectation of continued energy demand growth,” says Nunes. “BTM is shifting from a backup role to a primary power strategy.”

BTM power deployments, such as microgrids with low-emissions fuel cells, can provide essential power to data centers and manufacturers and support the wider grid through interconnection. Bloom Energy’s modular framework facilitates the redeployment of fuel cells when they are needed elsewhere. According to the 2026 Utilities Insights Report, 36% of utility leaders see distributed power as a vital supplement for peak shaving and grid backup.

As large-scale energy users build out onsite power generation, utilities should consider how BTM power fits into a long-term growth strategy. With rising demand, utilities are taking steps toward flexible energy architectures that integrate onsite power generation into the wider grid.

Both regulated and unregulated utilities are exploring the possibilities of utility-owned BTM assets to create fresh revenue streams. Such BTM power systems can serve as a much-needed capacity reserve to support large customers and the grid itself during peak-load periods.

For example, a large investor-owned utility in the Midwest recently faced mounting demand from two data center operators that needed more capacity than the utility could deliver. To meet this need, the utility presented a plan to install solid oxide fuel cells at both sites, which the state regulator approved. The data center customers covered the full project cost through long-term agreements that protect ratepayers, and the utility now owns and operates the fuel cells. “Ultimately, the project gave the customers speed to power while the utility continued its long-term grid investments,” says Nunes.

Nunes stresses the importance of preserving long-term flexibility by designing a BTM installation that can change roles over its lifetime rather than being removed once it’s no longer the primary power source. Choosing modular, scalable technology helps utilities maintain flexibility. “Capacity should be addable, movable or able to retire in increments consistent with needed power, such as Bloom Energy’s fuel cells,” he explains. “It’s also helpful to bring regulators in early, as an approval framework built for the first project makes the next one quicker.”

Tolling agreements and co-development partnerships also offer utilities a chance to participate in the growth of BTM power. With a tolling agreement, utilities can generate recurring revenue by contracting with customers to provide power from utility-owned BTM power facilities. Co-development partnerships allow utilities to partner directly with developers to design, finance and build power projects. Both types of arrangement allow utilities to invest in BTM power and incorporate it into their ongoing operations. The contract should define who owns the asset. In the case of Bloom Energy, its investment partners, such as IDF, can provide a cost-effective lease solution that allows the utility to acquire the asset after a specified period.

Utilities continue to explore how BTM solutions can bridge grid constraints today while moving toward a more flexible future grid. “Utility leaders should consider planning a hybrid grid,” Nunes says. Large-load customers such as hyperscalers and neoclouds will increasingly rely on both grid power and onsite generation, a shift that should be reflected in integrated resource plans and load forecasts. “Requiring large customers to carry the cost and risk of their incremental capacity will help protect ratepayers. Given the long backlog of large-capacity turbines, utilities should partner with technology providers to secure early access to manufacturing slots.”

With the ability to support large-scale loads with minimal emissions, Bloom Energy’s fuel cells can support BTM power projects for utilities and customers. To learn more, contact Bloom Energy.

#5792. Ford Drops Hint About New Fathom EV Battery Life

The Ford Motor Company has been playing its forthcoming Fathom electric pickup truck close to the vest, releasing few details ahead of volume production except for the MSRP of $28,350. Still, some clues have dropped. The latest news involves a newly released real-life data analysis of five-year battery capacity for the Ford Mustang Mach-E. The findings raise expectations for the next generation of Ford EVs, namely, the Fathom, which is the first vehicle to roll down the tree-like assembly line of the company’s new “Universal EV” platform.

On one level, the comparison between the Mach-E and the Fathom EV battery is apples-to-oranges. The 2021 Mach-E deployed [correction: NCM (nickel-cobalt-manganese) batteries with Standard Range at 68 kWh usable capacity as launched, and Extended Range at 88 kWh usable capacity as launched]. Ford is equipping the Fathom with an LFP (lithium-iron-phosphate) EV battery. LFP is a relatively new, more economical formula than Li-ion. All else being equal the tradeoff is lower range, though some Ford watchers point out that drivers can order their Fathom with an optional extended-range battery.

The point is that the 2021 Mach-E is old news. The new news is the forthcoming Fathom, which Ford has been regularly teasing as the first product of its secret, not-so-secret “skunkworks” team for the past five years or so. The addition of a cost-cutting, internal inverter for bidirectional charging is the topic of the most recent news drop, indicating that Ford expects Fathom drivers to use their EV battery for stationary applications as well as drive time.

Drivers who routinely use the bidirectional feature will likely put their Fathom through more charging cycles than a typical EV, and prospective buyers will need some assurance that their zero emission pickup will be up to the task.

And, that’s where the 2021 Mach-E data comes in. Ford has just released the 5-year numbers for retained battery capacity over time, and they look pretty good. By releasing the data now, when the Fathom is nearing volume production, Ford has raised expectations that the new electric pickup truck will deliver similar, if not better, longevity.

Correction: The Mach-E data doesn’t just look pretty good, it looks very good. Ford deployed its telematics platform to assess more than 36,000 Mustang Mach E’s in the “class of 2021,” driven in the US and Canada over an average ownership period of 5.2 years as of August 2026, during which time drivers clocked a collective 2 billion miles.

The 2021 Mach-E was sold with both standard and extended range battery packs, and the data combines both variations.

Relative to the Fathom’s bidirectional charging feature, the key finding is that high-mileage vehicles scored well. “More than 2,000 owners have crossed 100,000 miles on their original battery pack while maintaining >91% average health,” Ford explains. In the 2021 cohort, 30 drivers also ran past 200,000 miles while retaining an average capacity of about 88%.

Ford, which graciously shared an embargoed press statement with CleanTechnica over the weekend, also emphasizes that 95% of the 2021 Mach-E’s landed between 87% and 97% health, indicating that a relatively small number of outliers brought the average down to 92% overall.

The Mach-E data will surprise nobody who has been paying attention to the latest news about EV battery lifespan. As it turns out, EV batteries last far longer than first anticipated. That’s significant enough when an EV battery is deployed for mobility purposes. Capacity retention takes on extra importance when the same battery is used to manage electricity use in homes and other buildings.

Ford provided some insights last month, when it posted a long form article describing the Fathom’s built-in inverter. An inverter is a device that converts the DC current from an EV battery into the AC current used in homes. A typical bidirectional charging system requires the property owner to install a wall-mounted inverter, adding another layer of expense. Ford has absorbed the cost of the inverter into the Fathom itself, a decision aimed at encouraging drivers to make use of bidirectional charging at home. The company has also been working with utilities to help motivate drivers to take advantage of the feature.

With bidirectional charging, EVs realize their full potential as rolling, zero emission power generating stations. Drivers can use bidirectional charging on an emergency basis, to keep the lights on at home in case of a grid power outage. They can also take advantage of utility incentives, which reward EV owners for deploying their stored ectricity during periods of peak demand or other stresses on the grid, benefiting all ratepayers in the service territory.

Some US utilities already offer discounted or even free rates for EV owners who charge up their battery during off-peak hours. Try that with your gasmobile!

Stay tuned to this channel for further details about the Fathom and its battery. In the meantime, some Ford dealers have been passing along information from the automaker’s HQ. Here’s a representative example from the DC-area dealer Koons Ford:

“Battery options beyond the standard-range configuration have not been announced yet. Ford has confirmed a long-range battery option is planned and that its upcharge will be significantly lower than what Lightning buyers experienced, but specific range figures and pricing tiers are not yet public. This page will be updated as Ford confirms additional specifications.”

Okay, so much for that. Tonkin Hillsboro Ford in Oregon is hardly more forthcoming:

“Ford Fathom pre-orders will begin in early 2027, with a starting MSRP of $28,350 for the standard-range battery. While some specifications are still to come, there’s already plenty to know about this new Ford electric truck.”

“Ford has not yet released projected EPA-estimated driving range figures for the Fathom,” Tonkin elaborates. “The vehicle is currently being introduced with a standard-range battery, but an official EPA range estimate isn’t available yet.”

“This is one area where shoppers will need to wait for additional information,” the dealer adds.

Hmmm…let’s turn to the automotive press, where EV Charging Stations was somewhat more forthcoming. “The 2027 Ford Fathom electric pickup truck will not exceed 300 miles of range in its base form, but it likely will with the optional larger battery,” EVCS reports.

“The Ford Fathom has a 400-volt LFP battery system and 48-volt auxiliary electrical architecture. The truck features a cell-to-pack battery system (no modules), with prismatic battery cells being structural elements of the vehicle,” EVCS elaborates, further speculating that the extended-range battery could involve a higher-performing LFP formula or a switchover to the familiar NMC (nickel-manganese-cobalt) concoction.

EVCS also calculates that the standard EV battery will yield a range of under 220 miles, with the extended range version coming in at 300 miles, as Ford has indicated.

Meanwhile, the Detroit Free Press has noticed that a fully loaded Fathom with all the bells and whistles still comes in around the $40,000 mark, well below the $54,000 average cost of new EVs.

We won’t have long to wait for the full details to come out. Last week, Torque News was among those reporting that the Fathom is well into the final validation stages leading to full volume production.

With sales expected to begin late next year, the Fathom will face some competition from other EV makers that are aiming at the electric pickup truck market. One pickup stakeholder that Ford won’t have to worry about, though is Tesla, which is planning to halt production of the Cybertruck next year.

Photo: Ford is drawing attention to the long lifespan of the 2021 Mustang Mach-E EV battery, setting high expectations for the forthcoming Fathom electric pickup truck (courtesy of Ford via CleanTechnica archive).

Tina has been covering advanced energy technology, military sustainability, emerging materials, biofuels, ESG and related policy and political matters for CleanTechnica since 2009. Follow her @tinamcasey on LinkedIn, Mastodon or Bluesky.

#5791. Inside the ‘pressure cooker’: Electric utility regulators gather in wake of electoral earthquake

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Newly elected leaders and customers are “questioning our foundational regulatory approaches,” said Ann Rendahl, president of the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners.

Ann Rendahl, president of the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners, did not mince words while addressing her peers Sunday in San Juan, Puerto Rico, where NARUC is holding its annual meeting in the wake of a midterm election where concern over electricity costs helped Democrats surge to power in Congress and many states.

“Our newly elected leaders and customers just want rates to go down,” said Rendahl, a state regulator in Washington.

“They don't really care how we do it,” but they are “questioning our foundational regulatory approaches,” she added.

Rendahl was speaking at the first meeting, held on the sidelines of NARUC’s main conference, of a new initiative by state regulators, ratepayer advocates and investor-owned utilities aimed at developing approaches for evaluating long-term grid investments while delivering measurable value to consumers. The effort is led by the Critical Consumer Issues Forum, which includes NARUC members, as well as representatives from the National Association of State Utility Consumer Advocates and the Edison Electric Institute, a trade group for investor-owned utilities.

The midterm election results last week will affect state energy policy in different ways, according to speakers at the meeting.

It also could have implications for relations between states and the federal government, which have become fraught in some places over Department of Energy interventions — including ordering coal plants to run past their planned retirement dates.

In September, a federal appeals court ruled that the DOE’s “emergency” order requiring the J.H. Campbell plant in Michigan to remain operational was illegal, noting that state regulators and the Midcontinent grid operator signed off on the plant’s retirement.

“It's this fight between who does resource adequacy — that state role, that federal role — and everything in between, " said Michael Moody, NASUCA president and Michigan assistant attorney general.

Moody said he hopes the midterm results will spur more federal-state cooperation.

In Arizona, the state’s House and Senate flipped to Democratic control, while two Democrats were elected to the previously all-Republican Arizona Corporation Commission.

Lea Márquez Peterson, the longest-tenured ACC commissioner, who was not on the ballot, predicted the state’s new legislative majority will take up energy policy.

“I think we're going to see a lot of bills come out that will influence our world,” she said.

The midterm results will also likely increase the differences between states on energy policy, said Jeff Lyng, Xcel Energy’s chief sustainability officer and vice president of external affairs and policy.

“I think we're going to see a bit more of that patchwork,” he said. “But ultimately, [while] the words that we use in certain jurisdictions may be different … the strategy fundamentally doesn't change.”

Ana Ortega, commissioner on the Florida Public Service Commission, warned about pressure from the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission for increased regional transmission.

“Florida is a large peninsular state, and it's got all of its load at the bottom,” she said. “So, if you're looking to regionalize, it doesn't necessarily make sense for us. I'm not saying that we're not complying, but it doesn't [make sense].”

“We're all in the pressure cooker,” and concerns about data centers have added to the heat, Márquez Peterson said.

Options for balancing the need for grid investments with affordability include maximizing the value of the grid through grid-enhancing technologies and energy efficiency, she said.

In the face of affordability issues, Moody said utilities need to prioritize their spending plans, evaluate lower-cost alternatives and present strong cost/benefit analysis.

“Sometimes, there's these great ideas that have to be pushed off,” he said.

However, Ortega cautioned that delaying grid investments can sometimes be more costly in the long run.

“You have to also think about the opportunity costs of not planning and not investing in the grid,” she said. “I know that's unpopular. I know it's hard, but in some ways, it's better for long-term customer value.”

Load growth can help reduce customer costs, but “growth has to pay for growth,” according to Lyng. A gigawatt of hyperscaler load has the potential to put about $1 billion of downward pressure on rates over a 15 or 20 years, he said.

Lyng also said that investing in renewable energy, especially wind, has helped Xcel keep residential rates that are roughly 30% below the national average.

Xcel’s customers have saved about $6 billion on wind energy, with another $12 billion in savings expected by the end of this decade, he said.

However, amid shifting priorities, Xcel may not meet its commitment to be net-zero by 2050, a goal set in 2018, according to Lyng.

Now, sustainability plays “fourth fiddle” to safety, reliability and affordability, he said.

“We are not walking away from the [sustainability] goals,” Lyng said. “We still have those commitments, but we have to move at the speed of value. We have to move in a disciplined way. We can't force a clean energy transition.”

Still, Xcel sees a “very clear pathway” to achieving individual state clean energy targets, he said.
