#5789. A review of ‘Solar Roots: The Pioneers of PV’

By Tor “Solar Fred” Valenza | November 10, 2026

Was today’s modern solar industry inspired by off-grid California-living, marijuana-growing, Grateful Dead-loving hippies? That’s the main question explored in “Solar Roots: The Pioneers of PV,” a new documentary that recently premiered in Grass Valley, California.

The entertaining documentary traces the history, colorful characters, off-grid homeowners, solar educators and the entrepreneurs who embraced solar photovoltaic technology for vastly different reasons.

Shot on a shoestring budget with volunteers, “Solar Roots” may not have the polished production of a Hollywood documentary, but it is an untold story about the people and early solar and technology that led to the modern solar industry.

Since 2009, when I first started in the solar industry, I had heard jokes and anecdotes about the marijuana-growing hippies who sparked the residential solar industry. Until “Solar Roots,” no one had ever set up a camera to talk to those early solar pioneers and document why rural America became a hub of off-grid solar innovation.

Enter Jeff Spies, a longtime solar veteran and the documentary’s executive producer. In 2015, Spies organized a weekend party for the off-grid solar pioneers of Northern California, where he started his career in the mid-2000s. After hearing so many colorful stories about why these rural homesteaders bought $14/watt solar panels and started new solar installation and distribution businesses, Spies enlisted another solar industry veteran, Jason Vetterli, to travel to Southern California, Arizona, Oregon, Colorado and Wisconsin to capture these unique and personal stories on video.

This isn’t the first documentary about the solar industry or its challenges, and to be clear, Vetterli and Spies’ narrative is not about creating “the solar biz.” Truly, it’s about a particular tribe of solar early adopters. Through interviews, we learn about the 1970s, ’80s and ’90s counterculture movement and the people who stumbled into solar businesses out of necessity and good timing.

Listening to these stories, you quickly understand that none of these solar pioneers intended to get into the solar business to make millions or solve climate change. Their real motivations were tied to maintaining their independence from cities while having enough electricity to keep the lights on and the music playing while raising a family. Beyond the home applications, solar became a new water-pumping solution that supported the pioneers’ cash-only “agricultural” businesses.

Vetterli sets the film’s tone and theme with the first scene. Instead of fading in to a utility solar farm or suburban residential roofs, the film opens with a young hippie using a wire to connect a single 35-watt ARCO solar panel to a car battery that powers an old cassette boombox. He then inserts a Grateful Dead tape and presses play.

The Grateful Dead-inspired musical score, composed by Midnight North, a band whose members include another solar veteran, TJ Kanczuzewski, appropriately plays under the rest of the film’s narrative and interviews.

After a brief solar technology history lesson, Spies interviews the solar pioneers about the roots of their solar PV fascination, how they came to realize that solar could grow and impact their lives and their off-grid movement.

This realization wouldn’t have happened without the back-to-the-land movement. Buying land was inexpensive but getting a utility to build a power line to DIY houses in rural California cost millions. While living without electricity, we learn about their lives before and after PV.

Before PV, the pioneers had to jerry-rig their pickup trucks’ batteries to listen to music and use dangerous kerosene lanterns for light. Gasoline generators were loud, costly, required maintenance and were contrary to the quiet, remote lifestyle they wanted. After PV (and lead-acid batteries), the solar pioneers could enjoy light, music, small DC refrigerators for cold beer and fresh food, 12-volt TVs and other DC devices that were designed for camping.

After implementing solar PV into their own homesteads, the pioneers wanted to share their find with their neighbors. It was that desire to share the off-grid solar PV love that led to the early solar distributor businesses, publications and training centers — not a desire to make millions.

As businesses grew, cultures clashed. We hear the story of when corporate solar salespeople traveled to Humboldt County in three-piece suits to meet their best customers who are wearing T-shirts and jeans and carry the scent and cash of a “weedpreneur.”

While the majority of the film is about the people who wanted an off-grid lifestyle, Vetterli and Spies also interview the late 1990s PV code and policy pioneers. These solar people also wore suit, but they also saw grid-tied potential to make solar mainstream.

“Solar Roots” does a great job covering this off-grid to grid-tied transition, but the film only covers a fraction of the stories and people who inspired the multi-gigawatt and global solar industry that exists today, and that’s O.K. A single two-hour documentary could never cover all of the world’s solar pioneers, but we’re fortunate to have met these particular solar people. They may not have started the residential solar industry, but they certainly contributed to it.

Spies and Vetterli plan to release the entire documentary online in the coming months and have set up a mailing list at www.solar-roots.com for people to receive updates.

Tor “Solar Fred” Valenza is a former film and TV writer (Stargate SG-1, Dharma & Greg, The Dead Zone) and current chief solar public relations geek at UnThink Solar, a solar PR and communications firm.

Tor, thank you for this review. As you know, I was at the Grass Valley premiere too, and the film is a delight. Its account of how solar cells, battery charging, and inverters developed is the best I have seen: clear, even-handed, and grounded in the people who made it happen, with phenomenal historical footage.

Having spent those years at ARCO Solar, I smiled at the opening scene with the 35-watt ARCO module, the car battery, and the Grateful Dead tape. The homesteaders in this film put those early modules to work, solved practical field problems, and showed what solar could make possible.

The path runs from the 12-volt appliances of those early days to today’s 800-volt DC data centers, and it is far from finished. I look forward to sharing the film with students when it is released online, so the next generation can see whose work they are building on.

Congratulations to Jeff Spies, Jason Vetterli, TJ Kanczuzewski, and everyone who helped preserve this history.

#5787. EVs Displace 5 Times More Oil Globally Than They Did 5 Years Ago — Analysis

Five years on from Glasgow, EVs have proved the single most effective way to curb oil use, says T&E.

At COP26 in Glasgow, twenty-four countries and a group of leading car manufacturers committed to ending the era of fossil-fuel powered vehicles by 2040 “or earlier”. Five years and two energy crises later, oil savings from EVs globally are up fivefold, new T&E analysis shows. EVs are the single most effective way of cutting oil demand, improving energy security and reducing emissions, says T&E.

While battery electric vehicles (BEVs) represent just 4.2% of the global fleet, they now account for 25% of all new car sales worldwide – up from 6.6% five years ago. This surge has pushed oil displacement from 215,000 barrels a day in 2021 to 1.2 million barrels a day today, or roughly 1.2% of global consumption.

At the same time, electric buses now make up 5% of the global fleet, battery electric trucks are at 1% but in some countries exceed 50% of new sales. T&E estimates these vehicles avoid as much as 320,000 barrels a day. As road transport burns close to half of all the world’s oil, transitioning to EVs is crucial in reducing oil dependency. As the global car and truck fleet continues to grow, EVs are the only thing stopping runaway oil demand, warns T&E.

As electrification takes center stage at this year’s COP, current policies put battery electric cars on track to displace roughly 5.8 million barrels of oil a day by 2034 — matching the sustained disruption seen during the 2026 crisis. Accelerating EV adoption will be crucial to preventing future oil shocks, according to T&E.

Antony Froggatt, senior director at T&E, said: “Electric vehicles are the single most effective way of cutting oil demand. Right now the only winners in our oil addiction are fossil fuel giants and petrostates. Places with near 100% sales of EVs like Norway, Denmark and Hong Kong have effectively insulated themselves from future shocks. Five years after Glasgow, EVs stand as one of the clearest victories in global climate history. At this year’s COP, world leaders must set binding electrification targets. Not just for the climate, but for national security.”

BEVs on the road now avoid more than 200 Mt of CO2-equivalent a year. According to T&E, rapid transport electrification is a vital part of the wider energy transition by providing essential grid flexibility and storage. Just as past COPs accelerated the global boom in wind and solar, backing for electric vehicles at COP31 can deliver the same breakthrough, slashing oil demand and driving emissions down.

[1] Displaced oil is estimated from daily per vehicle savings: about 0.019 barrels per car (derived from Ember), 0.2 per truck (IEA) and 0.15 per bus (bottom-up from typical fuel use and mileage). The 2026 supply loss has been estimated at 5.8 million barrels a day. We use IEA fleet projections for 2030 and 2035 fleet sizes to calculate the offset year of the BEV car fleet. Results exclude plug-in hybrids, vans and two- and three-wheelers.

For the chart, global capacity and generation for up to 2024 come from the US EIA. For 2025, we used IRENA capacity additions and Ember generation data. Values for 2026 are T&E estimates based on Chinese installation data to September 2026 and generation on IEA mid-year growth forecasts. Global BEV sales from Ember.

Transport & Environment’s (T&E) vision is a zero-emission mobility system that is affordable and has minimal impacts on our health, climate and environment. Created over 30 years ago, we have shaped some of Europe’s most important environmental laws.

#5786. PPL increases capital plan to $25B with proposed Kentucky gas plant

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The company projects some 50 GW of potential load growth, mostly from data centers, across Kentucky and Pennsylvania.

PPL Corp. raised its base capital plan to $25 billion for 2027 through 2030 — $2 billion more than the prior plan, primarily due to a proposed combined-cycle gas turbine plant in Kentucky, President and CEO Vincent Sorgi said during a Friday third-quarter earnings call.

If approved by regulators, the new plant would have 645 MW of capacity, and be expected to reach commercial operations in the second quarter of 2032, according to PPL’s earnings presentation.

In Kentucky, where PPL operates Louisville Gas and Electric and Kentucky Utilities, the company’s spending is set to increase from $2.3 billion in 2026 to $3.6 billion in 2027.

“I personally think we're going to be building generation for the foreseeable future in Kentucky, just given what we see with the quarter-over-quarter movement in the pipeline,” Sorgi said during the call.

Regarding the Kentucky Public Service Commission’s previous denials of PPL’s requests to build new gas capacity, Sorgi said, “Of course, the commission is going to be diligent in their review of all of that, but conservatism is not cost-free.”

“Sometimes, being overly conservative in regulatory outcomes ends up costing our customers, and we've seen that in the past,” he continued.

“I clearly want to try to stave that off in this next [Certificate of Public Convenience and Necessity],” which is anticipated in the first quarter of 2027, he added.

Sorgi said that the next “next CPCN filing will occur once we have signed a data center [electric service agreement]” in the state.

Kentucky’s development pipeline “has expanded by 1 GW to 14.7 GW of potential load growth through 2032,” with data center-related load representing 12.8 GW, according to PPL’s earnings presentation.

PPL also continues to see a strong data center pipeline in Pennsylvania, where projects in “advanced stages” increased for the 11th consecutive quarter to 35.3 GW, up 3.5 GW from the second quarter, Sorgi said.

Sorgi said the company is seeing “significant progress” in converting that pipeline into committed projects.

“Projects with a probability of success of 50% or greater have increased to 4 GW of additional peak demand, with the average probability for these projects sitting at almost 85%,” he said.

Invitium Energy – which PPL owns 51% of in a joint venture with Blackstone – signed a letter of intent on Friday with QTS Data Centers “to be their generation solution provider in portions of [Pennsylvania],” said PPL’s earnings presentation. Blackstone also owns QTS.

“Invitium has agreed to all key commercial terms with QTS providing for ~2GW of generation capacity to supply power to QTS’s Salem Township data center project with a 20-year initial term with multiple five-year renewals permitted,” the presentation said.

The proposed generation portfolio “provides for two combined cycle natural gas plants complemented with fuel cells and battery storage,” it said.

The structure of the deal “provides a regulated-like risk profile with fixed monthly payments (with annual escalation) covering return of and on invested capital plus fixed operating costs and variable payments when the plants run, covering fuel and variable operating costs,” the presentation added.

Sorgi expressed confidence about the company’s data center-related business in response to an analyst’s question about the potential impact of the midterm election results.

“The national election results could actually support our pipelines that we have both in Pennsylvania and Kentucky,” he said. “Both of our governors have signed executive orders for developing data centers and doing it the right way.”

He suggested that restrictions on data centers in other states could be a net benefit to PPL.

“I do think some of the election results could, in some other states, potentially have a chilling impact, which could drive some of those projects or future development projects to both Pennsylvania and Kentucky,” Sorgi said.

#5784. BAAJA Remains Fundamentally Flawed Despite Manager’s Amendment

Senate Must Allow for an Open Amendment Process

Washington, DC — In the continued rush to hold a vote during the lame duck session on the “Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026”, the senators crafting the bill released a manager’s amendment on Saturday. The Senate has scheduled a procedural vote to begin consideration of the draft legislation tomorrow.

On Friday, Sierra Club issued a statement that it could not support the permitting bill as currently written, outlining fundamental changes that must be made. The manager’s amendment partially addressed these concerns, but major substantive issues remain.

In response, Sierra Club Executive Director Loren Blackford released the following statement:

“The Sierra Club appreciates the improvements made by the manager’s amendment, but the draft legislation remains deeply flawed. The Senate must allow an open amendment process for additional changes to be made.

“Updating and expediting transmission and clean energy permitting is essential to delivering lower costs, reduced air, water, and climate pollution, protecting communities, and expanding the grid for the American people. But rushing legislation that erodes Tribal sovereignty and people’s right to have a say in what is built in their communities, abandons clean water safeguards, and wildlife protections, while further enabling a rogue president that is waging a war on clean energy and our public lands is not an acceptable outcome.

“Senate Democrats have been given a mandate to act decisively to deliver relief on energy bills and level the playing field for clean energy. They can and must fight for a better deal. Unless additional dialogue and changes can happen, the Sierra Club cannot support this legislation.”

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5782. Arizona’s longest-tenured utility commissioner on large loads, affordability and Western markets

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“We have been talking for years that growth must pay for growth, and I think customers are asking for proof [of that],” Lea Márquez Peterson told Utility Dive on the sidelines of NARUC’s meeting.

Utility Dive sat down with Lea Márquez Peterson, a commissioner on the Arizona Corporation Commission, on Sunday at the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners’ annual meeting in San Juan, Puerto Rico, to hear about her views on the challenges facing the ACC and other state regulators.

Márquez Peterson was appointed to the ACC in 2019 by the governor and won elections for four-year terms in 2020 and 2024 — making her the longest tenured commissioner at the agency today.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

UTILITY DIVE: It seems like affordability and data centers emerged as top issues at NARUC’s annual meeting two years ago. To what extent have those issues changed since then?

Lea Márquez Peterson: I think in Arizona, we are growing so rapidly, and we're seeing a lot of large load requests. But that's not just data centers — it's semiconductors. And so we're having conversations, probably a little more in depth and more publicly than we did two years ago, with water use leading the conversation.

The top thing, when you come to Arizona to build anything, is you tell us about your water use, and the public is very sensitive to this. They've seen a lot of requests and development projects come through our state, and not all have been as transparent as they should be.

What about concerns that large loads are causing rates to go up?

We saw that play out in this last election cycle. We have been talking for years that growth must pay for growth, and I think customers are asking for proof [of that]. What are the components of cost? How are you structuring this as a commission, and how do we ensure that growth is really paying for growth? And so you see that playing out across the nation, whether it's large-load tariffs, or, in [Arizona], energy supply agreements.

Has the commission set any rules around those agreements?

They evolved over time. I think they were simpler years ago, and as more and more load comes through … the utilities are getting even more sophisticated in how they're handling it.

Is there any interest in standardizing it with a large-load tariff?

Not statewide. We have our upcoming large rate cases coming up, and I think you'll see some development of their large load activity in those rate cases.

What levers does the commission have on affordability?

It could come to rate design, how that's structured. It's looking at the supply agreements. How much of the cost allocation should a large load be covering? I think we have a lot of different tools. Are the [large loads] going to be partners in some of the new generation?

I've been defined in the media as skeptical. I’d stick with that. We passed it as a policy statement in Arizona, and I thought it was interesting conceptually, but I wanted proof that it really was in the best interest for customers, and so I'm kind of working through that case by case as they come before us. We have [Tuscon Electric Power] before us next week and we have [Arizona Public Service] in mid-December.

On market development in the West, Arizona's big utilities are opting for the Southwest Power Pool’s Markets+. With Bonneville potentially switching to the Enhanced Day-Ahead Market, you asked the utilities to provide updated information about their decision. So how are you thinking about market development and opportunities for the Arizona utilities?

The ACC doesn’t have a choice [on which market the utilities join], but I wanted to better understand how that impacts customers — the exiting of the Energy Imbalance Market — whatever regional markets are decided, and I wanted to understand the [cost/benefit] analysis that was done by [the utilities].

I'm going to follow up with a workshop on Feb. 3 where I want to ask different questions on the components of those questions I asked in the letter, and then have some stakeholders ask questions, too … and just make sure that we're all aware. There’s no reason there can’t be transparency.

I want to just see that the benefits, whatever market they choose, is in the best interest of ratepayers. It makes a difference — whether from a reliability perspective, transmission availability, resource planning, whatever it is. I want to better understand the benefits.

How comfortable are you with Arizona’s generating resource situation?

I supported an “all the above” approach. Reliability is so key. That means you cannot have power outages, especially during the heat of summer.

I'm very focused on nuclear, but it's very expensive. About 30% of the state is served by the Palo Verde nuclear generating station. So we might see new nuclear in the future, but who's going to pay for that? What role do large load users have, perhaps?

Looking ahead to next year, what are the top issues that you and the ACC will be dealing with?

Next year, we will have voted on a number of formula rate cases. We will have annual true-ups, and I think there's a lot to explore and develop with those true-ups. Was there enough analysis done? Was there enough time for the [ACC] staff to do the analysis? How much could stakeholders weigh in?

#5778. Utilities should consider ‘taking a breather’ from rate cases: Minnesota PUC vice chair

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“Filing rate cases right now is really painful,” Joseph Sullivan told Utility Dive in an interview. He also discussed his concerns with overly complex data center cost allocation.

Utility Dive met with Minnesota Public Utilities Commission Vice Chair Joseph Sullivan on Monday at the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners’ annual meeting in San Juan, Puerto Rico.

We wanted to hear his perspective on the top challenges facing regulators in Minnesota, home to utilities like Minnesota Power, Otter Tail Power and Xcel Energy’s Northern States Power-Minnesota.

Sullivan joined the PUC in April 2020, and was reappointed by Gov. Tim Walz in January to a second six-year term.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

UTILITY DIVE: What are the top challenges you're facing at the commission?

Joseph Sullivan: Certainly affordability. In recent rate cases, it's abundantly clear that many people are struggling to pay their bills, and they're going to struggle to pay a new higher bill.

On top of that, you have the compounding impact of the system that's changing. We have a system that is getting older. We have older power plants that need to be replaced. We have clean energy targets and requirements in our state.

And then you put on top of that new large loads that are coming, so we have transmission lines that are coming in. Many people are looking at these transmission lines, and they say, "Why is this transmission line going in? Is it going to increase the bills that I pay?”

In addition, people don't trust the institutions. That's becoming more and more evident and more difficult. You can see that in our hearings, which are becoming more contentious. What we receive in the dockets and the record is becoming more and more contentious, and in some cases, very worrisome.

How important is transparency in terms of rate cases, large loads?

Transparency has certainly been an issue with some of the data centers that have been proposed. People are concerned about what is happening within their community.

I would say, though, it's not just transparency that's an issue. It's context. People oftentimes just don't understand how the system works, and why would they?

But when you add that lack of transparency, plus the lack of contextual knowledge about the system, plus a degradation of faith in the institutions — it ends up being a really foul mix.

On the affordability issue, what can the commission do?

The biggest affordability program that our state has are our demand side management programs. They are taking millions of megawatt hours out of the system every year, and they're making us more productive. If you put those megawatt hours back in the system, our system would be about 20% larger. So that is a very real savings to people. All ratepayers benefit because we have to build fewer power plants and transmission lines. So, that's one of our main go-tos.

We have put a lot of work into setting up programs that our gas utilities run and our electric utilities run to help people avoid being disconnected, to help them with the arrearages.

When our legislature passed data center legislation last year, they put in a component that the data centers would have to pay into the state's weatherization fund.

What about things within a rate case: return on equity, debt to equity ratios?

Why that didn't spring immediately to mind is because that's what we do. Every rate case is about prudency. Is this expenditure just and reasonable, and therefore prudent? We focus like a laser beam on that.

Under our state statutes and under my thinking of how our law is, you don't include affordability in that ROE determination.

Is there anything that the utilities could be doing better to meet some of these challenges?

This is going to be really hard for them, but filing rate cases right now is really painful. I recognize that they have capital needs. But taking a breather from rate cases could be very helpful. We've had a lot of rate cases in the last year. They come at a — politically — really not great time.

Things that they could do also would be to be supportive of affordability programs that are going to be helpful to keeping people connected or paying down arrearages. I want the utilities to be creative in how they are addressing affordability.

The utilities also have to really clearly articulate that they are primarily investing in resources without fuel clauses, that don't have a fuel impact. Like, “We're investing in resources that are less expensive in other parts of your bill.”

And keeping rate increases to below the rate of inflation.

What effect do you think the midterms may have on state energy policy (In Minnesota, Democrats maintained control of the governor’s seat and Senate and took full control of the House in the midterm elections)?

For six of the last eight years, we've basically been on Democratic control. We already have a 100% carbon-free statute. We have our new energy efficiency framework. A lot of these big pieces are already set in stone, and we're in implementation mode on them.

The discussion in Minnesota will certainly be on data centers. Data center moratorium legislation was offered last year, and I think it'll be offered again this year. The politics has definitely changed, and some of those initiatives may have more momentum.

For me, the interesting question will be do the advocates who are more interested in small solar, are they on the ascendancy? Or are the people who are supportive of the vertically integrated model, are they on the ascendancy? Restructuring versus continuing the regulated approach — where does that land?

How are things going with the Midcontinent Independent System Operator?

In general, MISO is doing a good job with juggling between a lot of stakeholders.

We've continued on the pathway with the [Long-Range Transmission Planning] Tranche One [transmission projects]. Minnesota is done with our permitting for Tranche One. We're deep into our permitting for Tranche 2.1. So MISO has been moving forward — not as fast as I'd like — approving new transmission.

What I am worried about with MISO right now is the cost allocation for large loads, and in particular, I'm concerned that MISO has developed, along with the transmission owners, a very complicated cost allocation framework. We don't understand how it is consistent with not just Minnesota state law, but with what the Trump administration has said [on ratepayer protection], with where it appears that Congress is going, with making sure that data centers are paying both their incremental and their embedded costs.

#5777. West Yorkshire’s £229 Million Bus Decisions Will Outlast Its Operating Contracts

West Yorkshire has made two substantial commitments to its bus network. On November 2, FirstGroup announced a £109 million agreement with the West Yorkshire Combined Authority covering five depots, associated infrastructure and 330 buses, including 79 battery-electric vehicles. The authority is also spending £120 million on more than 250 new buses through a separate programme. These investments will help establish the region’s new franchised Weaver Network, but many of the assets being acquired will remain in use long after the first operating contracts have ended.

West Yorkshire’s fleet and depot commitments illustrate a problem explored in my 83-page report, The Bus Is Not the System. The accompanying TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines which decisions are becoming expensive to reverse and what authorities should establish before making their next commitments.

The Weaver Network changes the relationship between public transport planning and private bus operations. The Combined Authority will specify services and retain responsibility for passenger revenue under its initial large contracts, while private operators deliver the routes. The region is securing assets that will remain important regardless of which company operates the buses. In principle, that can make future competition easier because bidders do not each have to provide a new depot and fleet.

The challenge is that buses, operating contracts and infrastructure follow different clocks. An operating contract might last five or seven years. Vehicles can work through successive contracts, while depot buildings and electrical connections may remain useful for decades. A change in route patterns, population or employment locations can make an established depot less convenient. A bus can often be reassigned, but a multi-megawatt electricity connection cannot be transferred to another site because the network needs charging capacity elsewhere.

West Yorkshire has arranged a staged transition. The five depot sales have completed, while the buses are scheduled to transfer as new franchises begin operating between May 2027 and October 2028. First Bus will continue delivering existing services under temporary depot leases and will manage electrification at Bradford and Huddersfield, with the authority paying for that work. The arrangements allow services and infrastructure development to proceed while ownership and operating responsibilities change.

Securing strategically important depots is a defensible approach. It reduces dependence on an incumbent operator’s control of scarce infrastructure and can help new companies compete for future contracts. The authority also assumes long-term responsibilities for the assets, including maintenance, future electrical capacity and their suitability for a changing passenger network. A depot can be worth purchasing without every potential alternative having been examined, but the consequences of losing those alternatives should be understood before the agreement becomes binding.

Electrification makes this especially important. Charging equipment, substations and electricity connections involve different suppliers, construction schedules and contractual responsibilities from the buses themselves. Vehicle orders can proceed before chargers are fully commissioned when the infrastructure delivery pathway is sufficiently assured. Problems arise when a fleet commitment depends on electrical capacity that cannot be delivered in time, or when the fallback arrangements are inadequate.

The passenger network can also change in ways that alter the original investment case. Better bus priority can reduce the number of vehicles needed to maintain a timetable. New housing developments can shift demand toward different corridors. A major employment centre or hospital relocation can change the value of existing routes and depots. The infrastructure secured today should support those possibilities rather than make sensible network improvements prohibitively expensive.

None of this argues for delaying every investment until uncertainty disappears. Authorities need to secure land, procure vehicles and obtain electrical connections years before some services begin. The useful distinction is between preparation that reduces uncertainty and commitments that substantially increase the cost of changing direction. Both need to be assessed against the passenger services the authority intends to deliver.

West Yorkshire’s programme offers a concrete example of the opportunity and challenge. Securing assets and specifying passenger services across the metropolitan network may produce a more coherent system than leaving every operator to optimize its own routes and depots. The long-term measure of success will be whether the authority can continue improving that network through changing demand, successive contracts and further electrification without having to reconstruct infrastructure it has already paid to provide.

This assessment concludes my five-part series drawn from The Bus Is Not the System, an 83-page TFIE Strategy report examining four decades of British and Irish metropolitan bus governance. The full West Yorkshire Briefing connects the report’s findings to current fleet, depot, electricity infrastructure and franchising decisions.

#5775. Are data centers really paying their share? AES Indiana’s Google deal highlights a thorny question

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

While local grid infrastructure projects are fairly easy to trace back to a specific data center, regional system upgrades raise more complex questions about who benefits and cost allocation.

The Indiana Utility Regulatory Commission recently approved AES Indiana’s plan to serve a Google data center in a contested case that highlights the complex challenges regulators and utilities face in allocating system costs for large computational loads.

According to the Oct. 7 order, AES will acquire or build new generation, storage and grid infrastructure for a 390-MW data center planned for Monrovia, a town Southwest of Indianapolis, at an estimated project cost of about $737 million.

The new generation, storage and transmission assets included in the Monrovia Project will go into the utility’s rate base.

AES says its electric service agreement with Google is designed to ensure the revenues will be sufficient to “reimburse at least 100%” of the project’s incremental direct costs. But the contract itself is heavily redacted, and an estimated $74 million in network upgrades has been explicitly allocated to all customers as a reliability need, prompting criticism from consumer advocates that the arrangement is shifting costs onto other ratepayers.

Indiana state law requires large load customers to reimburse at least 80% of project costs “reasonably allocable” to them.

Claire Wayner, a senior associate at the clean energy nonprofit RMI, said in an interview that while local projects are fairly easy to trace back to a specific data center, broader regional system upgrades raise more complex questions about who benefits and how costs are allocated across jurisdictions.

“Maybe they're upgrading a part of the grid that they were going to have to upgrade anyway because it's aging infrastructure,” Wayner said. “If that's the case, they should have to demonstrate that, and then maybe cross socialization makes sense.”

But, she continued, “If that upgrade was not going to be built without the data center, without benefits modeling or just a demonstration of the benefits to the rest of the zone, it's difficult to make the claim that all ratepayers in that zone should pay for it.”

AES Indiana will serve Google’s data center from its portfolio of shared generation and battery assets. The new generation and storage assets built to meet the additional demand will be owned through a subsidiary by the utility and go into its rate base.

In testimony to the commission, a utility representative said the transmission infrastructure included in the Monrovia Project falls into three basic categories: interconnection infrastructure for the generation and storage; “direct connection” infrastructure for connecting the data center to the system; and network upgrades necessary “primarily” to serve Google “but also to meet other customer demand or energy needs.”

Google will pay about $32 million upfront for the direct connection, but $73.8 million in network upgrades will be included in AES Indiana’s transmission rate base and recovered through future rates from all customers, including residential ones.

Commissioners approved the proposal 5-0, saying in their order that “although the Customer may be the impetus for the network upgrades being made, this does not preclude the upgrades from providing system-wide benefits.”

“Network upgrades, as the name implies, are upgrades or additions to the transmission network intended to provide reliability, resiliency, and stability to the system at large,” it said. “Just as the Customer will be allocated a portion of the existing network transmission infrastructure, it is reasonable for existing customers to also be allocated a portion of the network upgrades.”

The Midcontinent Independent System Operator approved some of the network upgrades, which include a new substation, citing reliability and load growth.

Although the order says the portfolio proposed in AES’ filing is for 390 MW of new load with 460 MW of new 4-hour storage and 130 MW of solar generation, the substation planned for the project is designed for 1,200 MW of load, creating opportunity for future expansion at the site.

The generation and storage resources consist of one 200-MW and one 130-MW battery energy storage system, called Foundry Works and Hardy Hills, respectively, as well as a 130-MW solar facility paired with a 130-MW BESS called Crosstrack. Their cost is redacted in the case filings.

Ben Inskeep, program director at the Citizens Action Coalition, a consumer advocacy group that intervened in the case, sees the deal as running counter to President Donald Trump’s Ratepayer Protection Pledge signed earlier this year by tech companies and utilities, including Google and AES Indiana.

“These network upgrades are specifically driven by the Google data center,” Inskeep told Utility Dive in an interview. “Instead of making the cost causer pay, AES Indiana has decided to spread the costs out to other ratepayers, and our commission has unfortunately approved that.”

Ben Gavelek, a spokesperson for the commission, declined to comment on the deal. Google did not respond to requests for comment.

Claire Rice, a spokesperson for AES Indiana, said in an email that although the transmission assets are being allocated through the normal cost-of-service process, Google’s rate includes a “separate incremental-cost test designed to recover the incremental costs required to serve Google, including the network upgrades.”

“Cost allocation and cost recovery are two different concepts,” she added.

Many of the exact terms of the service agreement, including monthly energy and demand charges and riders, are redacted in the public record. Parts of other contracts underpinning the deal are also confidential.

The assets will be included in future base rate case filings. Until those new rates are established, the utility plans to recover those costs through its Environmental Cost Recovery and netted with contract revenues.

The commission’s order says AES Indiana’s accounting and ratemaking proposals enable it to “match” the project costs with revenue collected through Google’s electric service agreement and “flow” those contributions “back to existing and future customers” through riders.

AES says using this arrangement, Google will pay $300 million toward fixed costs through the end of the contract period at the minimum demand level, including 100% of the annual revenue requirement associated with the network upgrades. The utility expects Google to pay approximately $770 million at the expected level of demand.

Company representatives told regulators that its cost allocation framework is not based on “direct cost method” but instead uses a modified “slice of system” approach.

Another point of contention is that the tech giant’s rates will be based on a 90% load factor, which AES says is high compared to all other customers, but critics said amounts to a 10% discount.

The commission’s order said using the 90% estimated load factor “recognizes the Customer’s substantial and continuous utilization of the system capacity without assuming that the Customer’s maximum demand occurs exactly when the system peaks each month.”

Even though the network upgrade costs are not allocated to Google alone, AES Indiana Vice President of Commercial Patrick Maguire said in an interview that revenue from the company will “more than offset” residential customers’ portion.

“Customers’ rates are not impacted by those transmission investments at all,” Maguire said.

State regulators approved a $71 million rate hike for AES customers in June, attracting the ire of Gov. Mike Braun and setting off a chain reaction of turnover at the commission, which voted in September to rehear the case, the Indiana Capital Chronicle reported. The first of two increases has already taken effect, with another scheduled for January, despite the rehearing.

The utility’s next rate case is scheduled for 2029. That is also when AES plans to share a full cost-of-service study, cost allocation and rate design principles associated with the Monrovia Project.

Inskeep said it’s not clear if there will be a net savings for Indiana utility customers from Google’s contract with AES when factoring in other costs, such as congestion pricing, rising labor and equipment costs, new generation and potentially more network upgrades.

“We really do need some legislative reforms to increase transparency and establish better protections for ratepayers,” Inskeep said.

In the meantime, the grid operator is undergoing reforms to address the rapid scale-up of large load data center additions and associated transmission upgrades.

Jonathan Neal, a spokesperson for MISO, said in an email that the grid operator has undertaken an Expedited Project Review and Expedited Resource Addition Study to try and bring generation and transmission online more quickly.

MISO is also “working with our stakeholders” to make improvements around cost transparency, speed to partial power, a non-firm service option and market operations and processes.

Many of these reforms respond directly to concerns noted by the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission when it issued Show Cause Orders to six grid operators, including MISO, directing them to overhaul their processes for interconnecting large loads.

“MISO is committed to addressing these challenges efficiently, reliably, and fairly,” Neal said.

#5773. ‘Walk to Stop the SERPent Pipeline’: Connecticut Groups Protest Proposed Eversource Pipeline

EAST HAMPTON, Connecticut — On Saturday, November 7, Sierra Club Connecticut supporters, other community groups, and allies walked for four hours across seven miles from Hurd State Park to Eversource Headquarters in East Hampton to protest the utility’s proposed Southeast Resilience Pipeline. Fifty people attended the Walk to Stop the SERPent Pipeline. Of the 50, 37 participated in the seven-mile walk to Eversource Headquarters.

Sierra Club and partner organizations have given the proposed Eversource pipeline the name “SERPent” to highlight its potential harm to public health, energy affordability, climate, and the Connecticut River which, if approved, the pipeline will run under.

“Prioritizing profit over the health of the environment is to our own detriment,” said Erin Day, Owner of Ten Summit Yoga and Earth Based Wellness. “Protecting our natural landscapes is essential, as the connection to the earth is what sustains our own well-being. Preserving this land from the pipeline is a vital step in honoring our relationship with nature and upholding our responsibility for a healthy future.”

“Building communities resilient to climate and economic crises requires improving people’s health and environments while preventing the danger and destruction posed by this pipeline and similar projects,” said Diana Martinez, of Chicks Ahoy Farm. “We can’t allow our futures to be sacrificed for corporate gain.”

“Eversource wants to build a pipeline under the Connecticut River because they make more money building pipelines than they do selling gas; they are a for profit company making a billion IN PROFIT per year so we know what their priorities are,” said Martha Klein, Sierra Club Connecticut and the No Pipeline Expansion Northeast Coalition. “Eversource is blocking the transition to the modern energy economy by expanding fracked gas infrastructure in our state, which locks us into methane use for decades. Burning fossil gas kills people, disrupts the climate and makes energy unaffordable, but it has been very profitable for Eversource. The time for change is here.”

“As young people, we are critically aware of the fallacies embedded in the short-term logic of fossil fuel infrastructure,” said Jo Almond, Sunrise Movement Wesleyan. “I grew up in a town with one hundred and forty-four active methane gas leaks; a result of relying on natural gas pipelines imposed by our utility company. I want to be part of the last generation who has to deal with the toxicity that fossil fuels introduce into our communities; this pipeline would guarantee that Connecticut’s youth will be growing up alongside poisoned water, air, and soil for generations to come.”

“Environmental racism harms all communities and it is important to hold corporations and utilities accountable for expanding poisonous methane gas into our communities and exposing us to environmental harms that exacerbate health, housing, education and economic barriers,” said Tenaya Taylor, Nonprofit Accountability Group.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5772. The AI boom could shift diesel generators from backup to everyday use

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Last week’s election results demand community-friendly data centers. Overlooking the role of diesel backup could create more pollution and noise for residents, write experts from Inlyte Energy.

Antonio Baclig is CEO of Inlyte Energy, which develops iron-sodium battery technology. Ben Kaun is Inlyte chief commercial officer and Jon Wellinghoff is a former chairman of the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission and a member of Inlyte’s advisory board.

In October, during what is supposed to be a slack season for the grid, when generators are taken offline for maintenance, a spell of unseasonable fall warmth prompted the U.S. Department of Energy, at PJM Interconnection’s request, to issue an emergency order to avoid blackouts. The event is yet another indication of the strain on grids from demand growth from data centers.

But the Oct. 13-16 event also reveals a crutch that is increasingly being leaned on during grid stress, which may be an unwelcome surprise for communities living next to data centers: the diesel generators that are installed for emergency backup. The DOE’s 202(c) order, consistent with similar orders begun this year around Winter Storm Fern, authorized PJM to direct backup generation at large loads such as data centers to run. Earlier this year, on July 2, as an intense summer heat dome settled over the Mid-Atlantic, PJM recorded its highest-ever unrestricted demand, reaching 168 GW. In Northern Virginia’s Data Center Alley, utility executives said they came within minutes of needing to call on the vast fleets of backup generators installed at data centers to keep the system supplied.

This is a remarkable development. For decades, those backup generators had a simple job: If the grid fails, keep the data center running. Now, as electricity demand grows faster than it has in decades and extreme weather increasingly tests the grid, the generators themselves are beginning to look like a potential source of grid capacity.

That may seem expedient. But this development is happening at the same time as the public backlash against data centers, seen in last week’s mid-term election results, is prompting states to impose requirements for data centers — in September alone, new rules on data centers were signed in Virginia, Massachusetts, Maryland and California. If policymakers and regulators do not pay attention to what is likely to happen during periods of grid strain, increased reliance on diesel may end up being the default for the U.S. grid.

The existence of huge fleets of behind-the-meter diesel generators is a new development brought on by the AI boom. A modern hyperscale data center is no longer simply another large electricity customer. Its electrical infrastructure can resemble that of a small city, with substations, private distribution networks, sophisticated power electronics and enormous amounts of backup generation. Because even a momentary interruption can be extraordinarily costly, data centers install enough backup capacity to keep operating through a major grid failure.

Diesel generators have been the default because they are proven, reliable and relatively compact. Diesel fuel is also extraordinarily energy-dense and can be stored on site. But the generators were designed to sit idle most of the time and operate only during emergencies.

Rapid load growth is changing that equation. New generation and transmission take years to permit and build, while enormous new data center loads can arrive much faster. During periods when the grid is short of capacity, thousands of idle diesel generators can therefore look like an irresistible resource.

Federal emergency authority has already been used to make such backup generation available during periods of severe grid stress. In January, DOE authorized grid operators to tap as much as 35 GW of idle backup generation at large facilities. Similar authority has been invoked 13 times this year, seven of those in PJM.

The danger is that an emergency exception becomes a routine operating model. Once policymakers and utilities begin counting on data-center diesel generators as a source of capacity, there will be less incentive to develop cleaner resources that can provide the same reliability services.

This extracts a local price. Diesel generators produce nitrous oxides, particulate matter and other pollutants. A study of Northern Virginia data centers found that operating their diesel generators up to permitted limits would increase the region’s total nitrous oxide emissions by 45%. The study also found that the census blocks most exposed to those emissions tend to have lower household incomes than other census blocks.

The noise is also disruptive. During a previous electrical emergency in Loudoun County, residents described the sound of data-center generators operating continuously as being like airplanes landing for more than 24 hours.

That may be tolerable as an emergency measure. It is a poor model for the everyday infrastructure of the electric grid.

Virginia is starting to ask the question: its order directs environmental regulators to review the cumulative impact of data center backup generation and to evaluate alternatives including on and offsite battery storage, load flexibility, fuel cells, and other non-diesel options. But will state and federal regulators, and data center companies themselves, take the next steps to prevent diesel generators from being the default solution?

This question is particularly important because data centers are being asked to become more flexible participants in the grid. Recent regulatory initiatives are creating pathways for large new loads to interconnect more quickly if they agree to reduce their demand when the system is short of capacity.

A data center cannot simply turn itself off. However, that does not mean it cannot be flexible. Many strategies are being developed to make data centers appear flexible to the grid, including throttling low-priority compute, shifting compute to other regions, and putting flexible resources such as batteries at the data center site, or nearby, connected front-of-the-meter or behind-the-meter at businesses and homes.

Batteries can reduce the data center’s demand on the grid during periods of extreme system stress. They can absorb electricity when supply is abundant and return it when electricity is scarce.

However, the lithium-ion batteries being installed today have short durations, for example two or four hours. For any longer period of outage or grid stress — and local congestion can create longer periods of stress than the system-wide peaks — if the data center still needs to be curtailed by the grid, where is that power going to come from?

Today, the default is the backup diesel generator, or in some cases natural gas generators. Therefore, once the battery runs out, the data center that is providing a grid resource instantly switches from doing so in a clean way with a battery to doing so in a dirty way with fossil fuels.

It is clear that data center flexibility can use the grid more efficiently, providing faster access to power at lower societal cost. However, there are many details to be worked out. As policymakers and regulators formulate and implement new data center rules, they should look carefully at how data center flexibility is likely to be met and only allow methods that are consistent with the overall goals of states. If they do not, the expediency of diesel generators could result in it being used more often, creating more pollution and noise for local communities.

Looking to the future, the most secure way to ensure that on-site fossil fuel generators are not used for data center flexibility is to not have them there at all. Why not just replace the diesel backup entirely with batteries, as recommended by the Better Data Center Project? A common objection is that the batteries do not provide enough backup duration.

One alternative is simple: long-duration energy storage, either on-site or proximate within the potentially congested area. A storage system capable of providing 24, 48, or even 72 hours of backup could give a data center a high level of resilience, while at the same time allowing the facility to strengthen the grid productively on an everyday basis.

And long-duration storage does not necessarily mean installing more lithium-ion batteries. New battery chemistries, and other clean backup technologies, are emerging that use more abundant materials, are designed for stationary rather than transportation applications, and can potentially offer different combinations of safety, cost, footprint and duration.

For data centers, footprint is particularly important. Land around a hyperscale facility is scarce. Technologies that can safely pack long durations of stored energy into a relatively small area could make it possible to replace diesel backup without requiring any more land. Additionally, LDES could be placed for the benefit of the data center at other consumer locations such as commercial and industrial sites.

The immediate objective should not be to pick a single winning technology today. It should be to make sure that data-center developers and regulators do not assume that diesel generators are the only practical answer, and that they work to incentivize and develop alternative technologies. So far, LDES has been framed as a front-of-meter bulk storage solution, and state incentives have only rewarded behind-the-meter short duration storage. There should be a signal to the data center or other customers that the resilience contribution of behind-the-meter LDES is valued for bulk system reliability and the elimination of local emissions. This could take the form of allowing behind-the-meter LDES to be accredited towards system capacity and state procurement mandates while taking into account the full effective load carrying value of their durations, as well as modifying existing behind-the-meter rebate and demand response incentives to reward longer durations rather than capping them at shorter duration.

Across the country, residents are increasingly questioning the impacts of data centers: their electricity consumption, water use, noise, and local pollution. Some of those impacts may be unavoidable. But many are the consequence of choices about how these facilities are designed.

Virginia and other states are beginning to define what a community-friendly data center looks like. The industry should meet that standard by building data centers that are clean and quiet stewards of their communities from the start, rather than locking yesterday’s infrastructure into tomorrow’s grid.

#5771. US to install 36% more wind turbines in 2025 than previous year

The United States is expected to add more than 7 GW of wind installations in 2025, a 36% increase over the previous year, and the five-year outlook remains unchanged quarter-over-quarter from previous forecast, according to the “U.S. Wind Energy Monitor” report released by Wood Mackenzie and the American Clean Power Association (ACP).

Amid a challenging market environment, the report shows that the United States is on track to add 46 GW of new wind capacity from 2025 to 2029, with total projected volumes unchanged quarter-on-quarter from previous forecasts. However, timing has shifted, as 2026 and 2027 will deliver significant gains, at 10.7 GW and 12.7 GW, respectively, as more assets advance through the development pipeline.

While Q3 installations came in 23% below forecast at 932 MW, the market has shown progress with 3.8 GW queued for Q4 2025 — representing 52% of the year’s total expected capacity. This back-loaded installation pattern is consistent with typical project-commissioning timelines.

U.S. turbine order intake has rebounded to pre-One Big Beautiful Bill Act (OBBBA) levels quarter-over-quarter, supported by 2+ GW of firm commitments in Q3, the biggest intake in the region in the last nine months and a 79% quarter-over-quarter increase. However, true visibility remains limited as OEMs increasingly withhold project details and much qualifying “start-of-construction” activity occurs through off-site component manufacturing.

The market will then see a noticeable drop off, as 2029 weakens meaningfully quarter-over-quarter following project cancellations and inactive designations for late-decade capacity, driven by permitting and broader development challenges.

“The U.S. power market is facing mounting strain after a decade of flat demand, with utilities committing to 160 GW of large-load additions. This represents a significant opportunity for wind energy, which benefits from strengthened economic fundamentals and a compelling business case driven by its competitively low LCOE,” said Leila Garcia da Fonseca, director of research at Wood Mackenzie. “However, turbine costs remain elevated due to tariffs and mid-term wind growth will depend on resolving permitting and policy uncertainty.”

Power demand growth through 2029 is expected to average around 3% compared to just 0.7% over the previous decade, with data centers accounting for approximately 59 GW of the 90 GW total peak demand growth. This surge in baseload demand positions wind as a natural fit to meet rising power needs.

The five-year capacity outlook remains unchanged at 39.8 GW of added capacity quarter-over-quarter. The 2025-2027 pipeline is fully committed with all projects having turbine orders in place. More than 60% of the three-year capacity outlook has been commissioned or is under construction.

Activity is led by western states, such as Wyoming, New Mexico and others, that will account for 34% of activity in this time period. Major projects driving the outlook include Pattern’s 3.5-GW SunZia project in New Mexico, which will position the developer as 2026’s top installer, and Invenergy’s 998-MW Towner Energy Center in Colorado, the largest single project expected in 2027.

The market continues expanding geographically, with Arkansas bringing its first utility-scale onshore wind project online through Cordelio’s Crossover Wind.

The repowering market remains strong, as Wood Mackenzie projects that 18 projects will drive 2.5 GW of capacity additions in the next three years.

Wood Mackenzie expects the offshore installation pace to slow in Q4 2025 due to harsh winter weather conditions, pushing remaining capacity into 2026. Despite these near-term adjustments, Vineyard Wind has demonstrated strong execution, connecting 15 turbines in Q3 and delivering 200 GWh through nine months.

“U.S. offshore wind shows diverging momentum,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Projects under construction with COD estimated for 2026 continue to hit key milestones, but post-2027 developments face potential delays amid constrained wind turbine installation vessel capacity, driving delays and contract terminations.”

The offshore sector is also experiencing significant financial pressure.

The report highlights that uncertainty around tariffs is a threat to projections. Wood Mackenzie projects that tariffs will drive up turbine costs in 2026, before moderating in subsequent years. In total, U.S. onshore wind capex is projected to increase by 5% through 2029.

“U.S. wind turbine pricing is experiencing unprecedented uncertainty as conflicting market and regulatory forces interact,” said Garcia Da Fonseca. “Domestic manufacturing overcapacity relative to permitted project volumes, particularly after 2028, would normally place downward pressure on prices. Despite this, onshore wind costs are expected to continue rising due to tariff exposure on raw material inputs and subcomponents.”

#5770. Senate advances federal energy permitting bill

The Senate is fast tracking legislation that would reduce permitting times for energy projects, but possibly to the detriment of longstanding policies protecting the environment.

The Associated Press reported that the Senate voted 73-25 to advance the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026 (BAAJA). The bill was drafted in response to growing electricity costs from sources like grid-connected hyperscale data centers. By reducing permitting times, energy projects, like solar energy or onshore oil drilling, could address rising energy costs.

However, environmental groups consider BAAJA a reckless response to an immediate need for more power. The bill would amend sections of the Natural Environmental Policy Act, Clean Water Act and Endangered Species Act of 1973 to shorten review periods for new projects, and reduce which projects actually require reviews under these acts.

“We shouldn’t and don’t need to endanger our wildlife just to save a few dollars on gas. We shouldn’t and don’t need to weaken the rules that protect our water from pollution in order to build more clean energy. We shouldn’t facilitate more global-warming air pollution. And we definitely shouldn’t be taking away the public’s right to weigh in on projects that can ruin their environment, make them sick and lessen their quality of life,” said Lisa Frank, executive director of Environment America.

Despite the bill’s threat to environmental measures, the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA) has voiced its support for BAAJA, as have hundreds of companies in the solar and energy storage industry. More than 300 groups co-signed a letter addressed to Congress yesterday imploring the House pass this bill.

“Americans urgently need lower energy costs, a better electric grid and more nergy made in the U.S.A. This bipartisan legislation delivers all three,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of SEIA.

SEIA conducted an analysis that claims passing BAAJA could spur 167 GW of new solar energy on the grid between 2028 and 2035, while curbing the country’s CO2 emissions by 20%.

“Updating and expediting transmission and clean energy permitting is essential to delivering lower costs, reduced air, water and climate pollution, protecting communities and expanding the grid for the American people,” said Loren Blackford, executive director of Sierra Club, in a press statement on Sunday. “But rushing legislation that erodes Tribal sovereignty and people’s right to have a say in what is built in their communities, abandons clean water safeguards and wildlife protections, while further enabling a rogue president that is waging a war on clean energy and our public lands is not an acceptable outcome.”

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5769. Sierra Club Welcomes CalPERS’ $71 Billion Climate-Solutions Investment Milestone, Urges Fund to End Financing of Fossil Fuel Expansion

SACRAMENTO, CA — On Monday, the California Public Employees’ Retirement System (CalPERS) reported that the value of investments it classifies as climate solutions reached $71 billion as of June 30, 2026, up from $59.7 billion a year earlier. The increase brings the nation’s largest public pension fund closer to its goal of $100 billion in climate investments by 2030.

Sierra Club welcomed CalPERS’ continued commitment to climate-solutions investing and called on the fund to adopt stronger standards and transparency around what counts toward its $100 billion goal. Sierra Club also urged CalPERS to prioritize investments that support real-world emissions reductions and address climate risks across its broader portfolio to protect the retirement security of the workers and retirees it serves.

In response, Ben Cushing, Director of Sierra Club’s Sustainable Finance Campaign, released the following statement:

“It’s encouraging to see CalPERS make progress toward its climate-solutions goal, but a growing investment total doesn’t tell us how much new money is helping build clean energy and cut emissions. Reducing climate risk also means looking at where CalPERS invests across the whole portfolio. CalPERS has already taken the important step of using its votes to hold company directors accountable for how they handle climate risk. It can build on that work by adopting a policy to stop financing fossil fuel expansion and backing climate projects that need new investment to get built or expand.”

CalPERS said the $11.3 billion increase came from both new investments and gains on assets it already held. CalPERS also reported that financed emissions across the asset classes covered by its analysis rose by approximately 6 percent over the past year, largely reflecting investments in higher-emitting assets that it expects to decarbonize over time. For its global public equity and U.S. corporate credit holdings, CalPERS reported an implied temperature rise of 2.9°C, up from 2.7°C a year earlier.

Last year, Sierra Club and the California Common Good coalition urged CalPERS to publish a full list of the investments it counts toward its $100 billion goal, adopt science-based standards for what qualifies as a climate solution, and exclude fossil fuel investments from that total. Sierra Club has raised these concerns at CalPERS board meetings, and elaborated on them in a December 2025 CalMatters op-ed questioning the credibility and real-world impact of CalPERS’ reported climate investments.

Sierra Club’s 2025 Principles for Climate Solutions Investments outline standards for credible investing, including prioritizing real-world emissions reductions and establishing safeguards for transition financing. Its January 2026 Climate Solutions Gap report likewise highlighted CalPERS as an example of why ambitious climate-solutions targets must be backed by credible definitions, clear eligibility criteria, and transparent reporting.

The Sierra Club is America’s largest and most influential grassroots environmental organization, with millions of members and supporters. In addition to protecting every person's right to get outdoors and access the healing power of nature, the Sierra Club works to promote clean energy, safeguard the health of our communities, protect wildlife, and preserve our remaining wild places through grassroots activism, public education, lobbying, and legal action. For more information, visit www.sierraclub.org.

#5768. IIHS Founder Warned Drivers About The Dangers Of Speed Almost A Decade Before He Started The Organization: What's Streaming

Not long before the Insurance Institute for Highway Safety was informing drivers of how dangerous or safe a car was, James S. Kemper was on a mission to improve road safety. Kemper worked in the insurance world in his first job out of high school. He was an "office boy" for Central Mutual Insurance according to his New York Times obituary, but he went on to produce his own driver safety materials: educational films.

The films were made by Kemper's personal insurance company, Lumbermen Mutual Casualty, and in partnership with the LAPD and Traffic Safety Films Inc. The LAPD connection helped bring "Sergeant Bruce" to the films, who starred on-camera several times as the presenter of important safety lessons — each video with its own specific theme. Maybe it's Sgt. Bruce's personality, or maybe it's a cop-thing, but he also delivers a pseudo 1950s-version of scared straight energy with every specific dangerous driving situation.

Join me as I introduce a new(er), sometimes nostalgic video worth adding to your Watch Later lists. Leave a note in the comments if you find an older or interesting film you'd like to see featured.

In "Speed, The Accident Multiplier," a film restored and posted by 16mm Time Machine on YouTube, the on-camera narrator, Sergeant Bruce of the LAPD, explains to viewers that speed is dangerous. He uses charts and figures to show how faster speed increases stopping distances. The biggest takeaway is that the faster you go, the longer it takes to brake, and the more likely the damage to whatever the car hits will be catastrophic. That same messaging carried over into the IIHS organization decades later.

Sgt. Bruce ended the film in a familiar way that you'll find with most of these films: A message about how engaging with these dangers will lead to death. In "Speed," he tells viewers, "Speeding is a two-edged sword. It makes an accident more probable, and at the same time, it makes death more certain." He went on to explain that one out of every three fatal accidents involved a speeding driver, "which means in one out of three cases, a driver took a chance with death and lost."

There are a couple more films Kemper and his organizations had made in 1950 that are also available on YouTube. The next two, or rather four, were uploaded by PeriscopeFilm — specifically, they're two short double-features. The first pairs "Following Too Close" and "Driver Irritations," with the second pairing including "Child Safety" and "Driver or Pedestrian" — with the latter admittedly sounding more like a dangerous game, and that was likely the point. The opening to "Driver or Pedestrian" doesn't mess around either. Within the first minute, a camera follows a man driving a car as Sgt. Bruce tells viewers, "This is a driver..." before cutting to a tombstone, where Sgt. Bruce adds, "...who was a pedestrian. This is how it happened." Quite a way to set the scene.

Nine years later, Kemper's company joined forces with another two insurance companies to form the IIHS. Another decade after that, the IIHS would become its own scientific research organization, conducting studies to better understand road safety and finding ways to help improve it. By that point, IIHS had begun to do its own vehicle safety testing, as well. Those tests — although they have changed some as vehicles have evolved quite a bit since the 1960s — still provide consumers with important information regarding the safety of their vehicles today, like its latest look into the safety of heavy-duty trucks and cargo vans.

Kemper's work in insurance also helped push mutual insurance to the forefront in the U.S., which, if you're unfamiliar, it means the insurance company is owned by policyholders. There are no stock shareholders to appease. He was adamant about ensuring people were paid for their claims. In his New York Times obituary, he's quoted saying, "I made up my mind that this company was going to settle claims for cash in the shortest time possible. I did not see then and do not see now the value of contesting claims you know you will settle." Anyone feel like a few insurance companies could take some notes? Kemper's legacy endures with his insurance company over a century later, which now exists as Kemper Insurance, and the place where he left the biggest mark — in his role establishing the IIHS.
