#5767. New BYD Seal 07 Illustrates Chinese EV Evolution

Last week, BYD introduced the next generation Seal 07 EV. This model replaces the original BYD Seal, which entered the market in late 2022 and put the brand on the map in the minds of many people around the world. While the new vehicle is important to BYD, it also illustrates many of the changes that have happened to the EVs and the EV market in China over the past few years.

The new Seal 07 grows to 5080 mm long by 1960 mm wide by 1495 mm tall. That compares to 4,800 mm long, 1,875 mm wide and 1,460 mm high for the previous Seal. That also makes it slightly larger than the late Tesla Model S.

Like many new BYD cars, the interior has become more premium and technologically advanced.  Interiors offer front zero gravity seats with heating, ventilation and massage. An onboard refrigerator is offered in the center console and a power sunshade in the roof. Speakers have proliferated. The screens have improved,  programmable buttons can be added, and voice interaction has improved. Basic versions come with BYD’s God’s Eye C with BYD covering autonomous parking, while higher trims offer LiDAR-based God’s Eye B with BYD adding coverage for city navigation.

Seal 07 models now come with DiSus-C adaptive damping on lower trim models and DiSus-M magnetorheological active dampers on higher trims. Suspension is double wishbones up front, multilink at the rear.

Of course, the new car offers BYD’s flash charging, capable of charging from 10-70% in 5 minutes and 10-97% in 9 minutes, while taking 12 minutes to go from 10-97% at -30 C with no preconditioning. 20,000 BYD flash charging stations are planned to be in operation by the end of the year, which will be more than all DC charging stations in the US combined. Meanwhile, the previous Seal took 26 minutes to go from 30-80%.

Looking at the closest version of the original Seal from 2022, battery capacity has dropped from 82.5 kW to 76.4 kW for the new model. Meanwhile, range increased to 775 km CLTC from 700 km in the old model due to greater efficiency. While efficiency may have once been a criticism of the Seal, it is now a highlight. Power is up from 230 kW to 245 kW (329 hp). Meanwhile, a curb weight of 1975 kg is lighter than the previous 700 km model (2055 kg), with increased use of UHSS in the structure.

Overall, it is a larger and more luxurious car than the vehicle it replaces, while losing a little weight. Performance, charging and efficiency have improved. Equipment levels are higher. Technology is more advanced. And then we get to affordability.

In 2022, the original Seal offered a 550 km range trim for 209,800 RMB ($31,424 USD at current exchange rates), but the 700 km range version, which is closest to the new model, started at 259,800 RMB ($38,913).

A little over 4 years later, the new Seal 07 EV starts at 149,900 RMB ($22,437 USD) with 775 km of range, more power and much faster charging. It costs 10,000 RMB ($1498) more for a version with God’s Eye C intelligent driving. For the top-of-the-line flagship with LiDAR-based God’s Eye B, the price is 184,900 RMB ($27,694 USD). In addition, there are launch discounts and perks. Of note, there is no AWD version of the new Seal 07, with that role filled by other BYD models in China.

For the default comparison, the standard RWD Model 3 is smaller (4720 mm long) slower, comes with less equipment and only 634 km of CLTC range (612 km with optional 19” wheels). Price starts at 225,500 RMB, although paint colors and interior options increase the price (included with the Seal). To get Enhanced Driver Assistance, which is roughly equivalent to God’s Eye C, you need to add on 32,000 RMB, while Tesla Driver Assist (similar to God’s Eye B) adds on 64,000 RMB ($9,586 USD). To get as close to a top spec Seal 07 in a standard Model 3, would take you to 307,500 ($46,057), but you would still be under-equipped with less range in a smaller, less powerful vehicle. The Long Range RWD would take you to a bit above the Seal 07 with 800 km of range on 19” wheels, even though that ternary  battery is recommended not to charge to 100% on a regular basis, unlike the LFP-based second generation Blade Battery in the BYD. The Long Range RWD Model 3 costs 331,500 RMB ($49,652 USD) with paint, 19” wheels and Driver Assist, but still lacks in equipment.

However, the Seal 06 (4870 mm) is a better match for the standard Model 3 but comes in at 119,900 to 159,900 RMB ($17,958 to $23,950 USD). There is also a no-compromise, larger (5150 mm) Seal 08 with more available range, power and amenities from 196,900 to 239,900 RMB ($29,492-$35,932 USD). And if you want something sportier, there is now the Fang Cheng Bao Formula S (5060 mm) at 189,900 to 229,900 RMB ($28,443-$34,434 USD). For something more luxurious, there is the Denza Z9S at 255,800 to 325,800 RMB ($38,313-$48,798 USD). None of those BYD models existed when the original Seal was launched. In addition, there are also Dynasty Han and Qin sedans from BYD for a little different flavor. And those are just the BEV models. There are also PHEV versions.

Looking beyond Tesla and BYD, the Seal 07 fits in the price gap between the Mona M03 and Next P7 at XPENG. It slots below the SU7 from Xiaomi. Below a Zeekr 7X or Nio ET5. At its current price, size and capability, the Seal 07 is in a good competitive niche between entry level sedans and more premium vehicles. If the Seal 07 had kept the original Seal’s higher price point, the competition would be much more challenging.

Compared to 2022, the new Seal 07 does not need to cover as many bases. This lets it better meet the needs of specific customers, while related models can be tailored to different customer needs. Undoubtedly, these different models will divide sales numbers, which may not be good for bragging rights. Analysis will become more challenging and may require combining sales of related models for relevant comparisons. But the increased choice is better for customers.

It may be a while before we have a clear picture of what impact this all has on sales. Chinese regulations that began earlier this year prevent manufacturers or dealers from selling below cost. This essentially prohibits the model-year-end clearance sales that are typical throughout much of the West. In addition, Chinese regulations stopped the practice of registering a vehicle and then exporting it as a zero-mile used car. In combination, it becomes much more difficult to manage inventory. As such, many models that are about to be replaced have their deliveries cut well in advance of the new model launch and have sales essentially evaporate. Then, it can take a while for the new models to ramp up production and deliveries to take hold. We may not have a good picture of BYD’s sedan sales in China until next year.

It also may be a while before we have global distribution plans or pricing for the new models. The three new Seal models are not yet offered outside of China. BYD has taken the approach of waiting to bring their latest models to export markets, often introducing models overseas as new that have already been discontinued in China. When they do offer current models, they tend to be at a significant premium (see Denza Z9GT). Meanwhile, companies like XPENG are introducing their latest models globally.

However, with intensifying competition from Chinese automakers in some export markets, BYD’s approach may change. Some flash charging cars are starting to reach export markets. The Seagull (ATTO 1) has recently been selling in Australia for $19,990 AUD ($13,752 USD), representing a significant reduction in export markup. We might see more advanced models at lower markups in markets where protectionism has not taken hold.

For comparison to the US, the least expensive car of any powertrain is the gasoline Kia K4 at $23,535, which is more than the base Seal 07 ($22,437). The least expensive EV is the soon-to-be-discontinued Chevy Bolt at $28,995, which is more than the most expensive Seal 07 ($27,694). Meanwhile, the least expensive EV sedan in the US is once again the Tesla Model 3 at $38,630 (including destination and fees, which are included in Chinese prices). A number of other EV models were canceled.

In China, EV choices have expanded, cars have become more capable, and prices have come down over the past four years. Meanwhile, in the US, cars remain unaffordable and EV choices have diminished with aging products.

Of note, the original BYD Seal had already attained regulatory approval from Transport Canada for import prior to Biden and Trudeau implementing prohibitive 100%+ tariffs on Chinese cars. Due to regulatory harmonization between the two countries, that meant it would have also been approved to enter the US. The US EV market could have looked dramatically different now, even with the rollback of consumer subsidies. With free trade, we could be looking at an EV growth in market share, expansion of options and improvement in affordability similar to China, even if not to the same extent.

Overall, the new Seal 07 is a strong entrant into a competitive segment in a very competitive market. It might be a challenge to choose it over one of the many other excellent options from BYD and its competitors in China. However, having too many excellent, affordable EV options to choose from is a good challenge for customers to have.

Larry lives in Queens, NY, with his wife and cats. While he has spent much of his professional career leading global marketing efforts, his passions focus on clean technology and the automotive industry. He believes in Green Free Trade and the potential for the application of engineering talent to create solutions to the world’s most challenging problems.

#5766. Rubix Battery: Storage Designed for Solar Installers

By James Mast, Managing Partner, Rubix Battery

AI and data center growth, extreme weather, and baseload retirement are straining the US electric grid. For homeowners and businesses, reliable access to electricity is increasingly important. That need is driving solar battery adoption. Solar power with battery storage can help keep systems running when grid power is unavailable and give property owners greater control over energy.

Energy storage is becoming an increasingly important part of residential, commercial, and off-grid power systems. In fact, the off-grid power systems market is expected to increase to USD 7.35 billion by the year 2032, while growing at a Compounded Annual Growth Rate (CAGR) of 15.0%. For solar contractors, that growth brings new opportunities, but they will need battery solutions that accommodate different project requirements without adding needless complexity. As more customers consider storage, solar installers need battery solutions that can be configured for different loads, applications, and long-term goals.

Rubix Battery is a U.S.-based energy storage manufacturer specializing in lithium iron phosphate (LiFePO4) solar batteries. Drawing on personal, hands-on experience with solar and off-grid installations, the company develops battery systems with an emphasis on practical installation, scalable capacity, and long-term performance.

Battery specifications are important, but they’re only one part of what makes a battery good for a project. Installation requirements, wiring access, inverter compatibility, and the ability to expand a system all affect how well a product fits.

Rubix Battery knows what makes installation easier because the batteries were designed by installers with hands-on, real-world experience. They bring that knowledge and experience into product design. Features like enclosed wiring compartments, magnetic covers, modular stacking, and closed-loop communication with compatible inverters help simplify system setup. Integrated safety components and designs that eliminate the need for external racking or exposed cabling further streamline installation.

This installer-focused approach also gives contractors flexibility when planning systems for customers with different energy demands. No matter what type of project, both residential and off-grid, Rubix offers two battery platforms to address a range of storage needs while giving customers reliable energy storage that grows as their needs do.

Battery performance over time depends in part on how effectively individual cells remain balanced. Rubix Battery uses Impactiv Balancing™ technology in every battery to help maintain consistent voltage throughout the battery pack.

Unlike traditional passive balancing, which dissipates excess energy from higher-voltage cells as heat, Impactiv Balancing™ transfers energy from higher-voltage cells to lower-voltage cells. The system also incorporates dissipative balancing when a cell reaches a critically high voltage.

With Impactiv Balancing™, Rubix batteries balance cells at up to 20 times the speed of passive balancing and can add up to 30% more battery life. Effective cell balancing is an important consideration when evaluating battery capacity, performance, and long-term reliability for installers and system owners.

Rubix Battery offers two primary product lines: the modular Stack Series and the high-capacity R-Series. Both use LiFePO4 chemistry and include Impactiv Balancing™ technology, closed-loop communication with compatible inverters, and a 10-year warranty.

Rubix Stack Series solar batteries are modular LiFePO4 energy storage systems designed for residential and light commercial applications. Each Rubix Stack Series battery module delivers 5.12kWh of energy storage, scalable up to 32 batteries in parallel for a maximum system capacity of 163.84kWh.

Rubix R-Series solar batteries provide higher-capacity storage for demanding applications, including off-grid, agricultural, and larger energy systems. Each Rubix R-Series battery module delivers 14.336kWh to 16kWh of energy storage across three configurations (24V/560Ah, 48V/280Ah, and 48V/314Ah) and is scalable up to 63 batteries in parallel for a maximum system capacity of 1,008kWh.

Selecting a battery platform involves balancing capacity, installation requirements, and a customer’s long-term energy needs. Rubix Battery’s Stack Series and R-Series give installers two approaches to system design, from modular residential storage to high-capacity off-grid applications.

With LiFePO4 chemistry, Impactiv Balancing™ technology, and features designed around installation realities, Rubix Battery offers battery options for contractors working on a range of solar projects.

Explore the product specifications and connect with the team at Rubix Battery to learn more about its energy storage solutions.

#5763. 5 Years In, Mustang Mach-E Battery Data Shows What Real-World Electric Vehicle Durability Looks Like

Five years ago, the first Mustang Mach-E customers began adding something important to the Ford electric vehicle battery validation work: real-world ownership at scale.

As part of our quality process, design and validation engineering starts long before a vehicle reaches a customer. We model battery performance, run physical tests, expose packs to heat, cold, and charging profiles, and build in margin for demanding real-world conditions.

These rigorous quality tests are designed to help us understand how battery systems perform across a wide range of climates, charging behaviors, and driving conditions before customers get behind the wheel.

Then David Blenkle drove past 400,000 miles on the original battery in his 2022 Mustang Mach-E. He gave us a real-world example of what is possible — and begged the question of whether he’s an outlier or not.

His battery health has remained strong through unusually high-mileage use. His experience gave our team a chance to compare one exceptional ownership case with what we are seeing across the broader fleet.

The bigger question was if David’s experience reflected a broader pattern. So, we decided to look back at the Mustang Mach-E owners who started it all: The Class of 2021.

Over five years ago, the first Mustang Mach-E vehicles — model year 2021 — reached customers. Those electric SUVs have been on the road, in customers’ hands, ever since.

We analyzed the battery state-of-health data for the entire fleet.

Here is what the Class of 2021 looks like at the five-year mark:

More than 36,000 vehicles. Two billion all-electric miles, across all 50 states and across Canada.

Our Class of 2021 has almost reached Neptune — not a practical comparison, but a useful way to understand the scale and distance.

The number I look at closely is the spread. Ninety-five percent of those vehicles fall between 87% and 97% health. Averages can hide variation, so that range matters.

In some cases, David has driven 300,000 miles more than many in our 2021 cohort. Then there are also the people who are road-warriors, like David.

More than 2,000 Class of 2021 owners have crossed 100,000 miles on their original battery, averaging better than 91% battery health. A smaller group of high-mileage drivers — more than 30 drivers past 200,000 miles — is averaging 88%.1

David’s mileage remains exceptional, but the broader Class of 2021 data shows he is part of a larger pattern of strong battery health across real-world use.

Long-term battery health remains one of the biggest questions for many people considering an electric vehicle, and real-world data like this helps make that conversation more concrete.

Two billion customer-driven miles is meaningful because it reflects how people use their vehicles — commuting, road-tripping, and running their everyday lives.

And it’s not just the 2021 Mustang Mach-E.

Aggregated across the full battery-electric lineup released over the last five years in the U.S. and Canada, the pattern holds: our packs are designed to last at least 10 years, and in the wild they’re maintaining, on average, greater than 90% health at 100,000 miles.2

The Class of 2021 is the cohort old enough, right now, to tell the most detailed version of that story.

While this story focuses on the 2021 Mustang Mach-E, the learning doesn’t stop there.

Ford uses field data from vehicles on the road to inform current and future electric vehicle programs such as the Universal Electric Vehicle platform and Fathom. This includes how we think about battery management, charging guidance, and long-term ownership confidence.

Battery chemistry is complicated, but a few simple everyday habits can help support long-term battery health:

Owners should not have to become battery chemistry experts to take good care of their electric vehicles.

The 2021 Mustang Mach-E vehicles in this data set use NCM battery chemistry, where frequent full charges are generally not needed for daily use. Some newer Ford EV batteries use LFP chemistry, which benefits from an occasional charge to 100% to help the battery management system stay calibrated.

Ford over time has implemented new features, like guidance in the Ford App, to help owners look after their batteries, specific to the vehicle and battery system they drive.

I have spent my career on the battery side of this business — cell chemistry, pack design, thermal management, and the systems that help a battery perform over time.

For an owner, the battery is central to the EV experience. It shapes confidence in every commute, road trip, and charging stop.

This is one of the most valuable feedback loops we have had at this scale: more than 36,000 vehicles, five-plus years, and billions of real-world miles.

The data is encouraging, and the learnings continue to inform how Ford approaches battery systems, software guidance, and future electric vehicle programs.

The Class of 2021 has added a real-world chapter to what our validation work was designed to understand. And we have many more electric miles ahead of us.

By Kevin Vander Laan, the Technical Leader of High-Voltage Battery Cells and Systems at Ford.

1. Battery state-of-health data is as of August 2026 for all 21MY Mustang Mach-E vehicles sold to U.S. and Canada retail and fleet customers, covering approximately 36,000 owners with an average ownership period of 5.2 years. Figures reflect usable battery capacity.

2. Based on a 2026 manufacturer field data study of high-voltage battery state of health on Ford battery electric vehicles.

#5762. 1961 Chevy Impala Bubble Top For Sale With 1,111 Miles Shown Is A Wild LS V8 Restomod

When GM styling head Bill Mitchell set about redesigning the Chevrolet look for the 1961 model year, taking over from the iconic Harley Earl, he wanted to make a big shift away from the upright aesthetic with dramatic tail fins that defined the brand's 1950s. Moving into a smoother and cleaner jet age look, the 1961 Impala looked lower, longer, wider, and more streamlined. With wire-thin pillars and wraparound windows front and rear, the unique Sport Coupe model came to be known as a "bubbletop" in enthusiast circles. Decades on from its production date, this Impala design looks as beautiful as the day it was born. This particular example, currently for sale with a bubble-bursting $259,900 price tag, hides some modern improvements to make it an even better Impala.

Many car fanatics drive vintage cars because they're stylistically impressive, and it doesn't get much more impressive than a bubble top Impala scraping the ground on bags. There are downsides to driving a classic every day, though. Old cars typically don't have the modern amenities that American drivers have come to expect, like the unstoppable power of a fuel-injected small-block Chevrolet V8, a modern overdrive transmission, the stopping power of disc brakes, or modern air suspension. Thankfully, this Impala is fitted with all of these. This is a showstopper that was built to handle the rigors of modern traffic. What more could you want?

Take one part old Impala, one part modern Corvette, and one part creative hot-rod ingenuity, and you get a magnificent piece of automotive art. Albeit automotive art with the price tag of a long-lost Gertrude Abercrombie surrealist masterpiece. I want to know everything there is to know about this machine, and I bet you do, too.

While the car still carries all of the beautiful bodywork of a '61 Impala, it appears that just about everything under the skin has been replaced with modern alternatives. With new drivetrain components, suspension, steering, and brakes, this looks like a truly incredible driving experience waiting to happen. With the seller claiming just 1,111 miles on the odometer, it's just barely been broken in and should be taken on a nice long road trip with expediency. Imagine next summer cruising back and forth across the country on Route 66 in a black-over-red Impala and barely breaking a sweat because you have a nice fuel-efficient LS engine, graceful power steering, comfortable suspension, and ice-cold Vintage Air A/C blowing. Yeah, that's the American dream.

Back in 1961, Impala buyers would have been given three different engines to choose from, including a Blue Flame six-cylinder, a 283ci V8, and a 348ci V8. The most powerful Impala of its day featured 350 horsepower from a "Special Super Turbo-Thrust" V8 with three two-barrel carburetors. A modern LS2, cribbed from a C6 Corvette, makes an uncomplicated 400 ponies from its six-liter displacement. Presumably for the sake of simplicity and availability, the shop decided to pair this engine with a 4L60 four-speed automatic. This was an extremely common transmission, used in practically every rear-wheel drive General Motors product built from the mid-1990s to about 2010. This car should have no trouble delivering the giddyup, or laying some rubber if you want it to.

The seller describes the suspension setup thusly:

Equipped with a complete Ridetech Air Ride Suspension System, this Impala can transform from a pavement-hugging showpiece to a comfortable highway cruiser at the touch of a button!

Inside the Impala, there's an interesting juxtaposition of old and new, with gorgeous period-correct red interior upholstery wrapped around custom bucket seats, a unique center console, and plenty of retro-modern touches, like the full Dakota Digital gauge cluster. In addition to the modern air conditioning, this car is fitted with a modern (unspecified) multi-speaker sound system. I'm not sure how much music you'll need, considering the LS2's soundtrack is bolstered with a stainless-steel Borla exhaust.

I don't like the Billet Specialties wheels, and I think they're much too large for this car, but a nice set of 18s wouldn't go amiss here. Likewise, the steering wheel is a touch too far. Beyond that, I'm not sure there is much I would amend here. It's a nice car that appears to be nicely done. I'd want a few thousand more miles of testing on a completely custom car before taking it on a big trip, but the parts to fix it will be available at whatever parts counter you find along Route 66.

I'm not going to try to justify the price tag, because this is an absolutely absurd number, and I don't think they'll get anyone to pay it. If you have the spare quarter mil, though, there are a lot worse cars you could buy. Anything new in that price range is going to be faster, but will it be as effortlessly stylish? What's the point in buying a new Porsche 911 GT3 when a sea of them parks at your local cars and coffee? You could instead arrive in something that has show-worthy style, and you'd still have enough comfort to cross the country.

#5761. More than 300 Solar & Storage Companies Urge Congress to Pass Bipartisan Permitting Reform to Accelerate Clean Energy Deployment, Lower Costs

WASHINGTON D.C. — More than 300 solar and storage companies are urging Congress to pass the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act (BAAJA), S. 5653, warning that outdated federal permitting processes and grid infrastructure bottlenecks are delaying critical energy projects and driving up electricity costs.

In a letter to congressional leaders, the companies call on lawmakers to quickly send the bipartisan legislation to the President’s desk. The signatories note that solar and storage account for 70% of new electricity capacity added to the grid thus far in 2026, while $120 billion in clean energy investment remains caught in permitting delays.

“You can’t lower energy costs if you can’t build the energy projects, and these companies know that this bipartisan legislation will help get America building again,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA).

BAAJA would modernize the federal permitting system by ensuring fair treatment for all technologies, protecting lawfully issued permits from politically motivated delays, establishing predictable timelines for federal reviews and judicial proceedings, modernizing transmission planning, and expanding distributed energy resources’ access to wholesale electricity markets.

New SEIA analysis shows that BAAJA could unlock 167 gigawatts of additional solar capacity between 2028 and 2035, bringing solar to over 18% of U.S. electricity generation by 2035. The analysis finds that the bill’s reforms could reduce cumulative power sector CO2 emissions by approximately 20% and avoid $54 billion in public health damages.

The message from more than 300 companies comes as voters made clear in the midterm elections that lowering energy costs is a top concern. Permitting reform is urgently needed to meet rising demand and get more reliable, affordable solar and storage projects built.

About SEIA®: The Solar Energy Industries Association® (SEIA) is leading the transformation to a clean energy economy. SEIA works with its 1,200 member companies and other strategic partners to fight for policies that create jobs in every community and shape fair market rules that promote competition and the growth of reliable, low-cost solar power. Founded in 1974, SEIA is the national trade association for the solar and solar + storage industries, building a comprehensive vision for the Solar+ Decade through research, education and advocacy. Visit SEIA online at www.seia.org and follow @SEIA on Twitter, LinkedIn, and Instagram.

#5760. How A Salesman Accidentally Convinced Shaquille O'Neal To Buy 3 Ridiculously Expensive Cars

Shaquille O'Neal doesn't do small or understated. The 7-foot-1 four-time NBA champion has an instantly recognizable brand. Oh, he's worth around $500 million, too. Naturally, as with many other well-to-do star athletes, he has a penchant for flashy cars. Problem is, he seldom fits in them without some contortions and modification. It's what drove him to have a Lamborghini lengthened into a Franken-Gallardo. That stretched supercar isn't the only time Shaq did some silly stuff with expensive cars.

In what was almost certainly a prideful purchase, O'Neal bought three outrageously expensive cars on the spot. Why? All it took was one condescending question from a less-than-respectful Rolls-Royce dealership salesman, and Shaq bought the lot. Just like that, the champ and business mogul had three extra opulent cars he says "I never drive."

Don't believe it? One of them, a 2004 Rolls-Royce Phantom, just sold on Bring-a-Trailer. If that wasn't enough, the once-undisputed heavyweight champion of the world, Mike Tyson, may have had a hand in nudging the Diesel to buy three ultraluxe cars instead of a solitary sedan. Of course, it's not difficult to get carried away when shopping for Rolls-Royces; just look at the one-off Phantom inspired by "Goldfinger."

Some 30 years ago, Shaquille O'Neal signed a groundbreaking $120 million deal to don Los Angeles Lakers colors and put away his Orlando Magic jersey. Several years later, he wandered into a Rolls-Royce showroom with his friend Alex and started browsing the hand-built grandeur.

Now, O'Neal had money to spare, but he wasn't quite up for indiscriminate spending. "I like to spend money, but I don't like to waste money. So I don't want to spend $400,000 for a car," O'Neal recalled in a Revolt interview. He went about asking a salesperson about the prices of the showroom's many Roll-Royces. That's when the salesperson said the wrong thing. Or the right thing, considering the commission on three hilariously expensive cars.

In O'Neal's book, "Shaq Uncut: My Story,"  he recalls the question as especially disrespectful, be it accidental or deliberate. The salesperson gave the Lakers sweatpants-clad O'Neal a glance and, per Basketball Network, asked, "Are you sure you can afford it, sonny boy?" That did it. Shaq lost it. "I said, 'What the f*** did you just say to me?'" So, he pointed around the showroom. "I want that silver hardtop one, and I want the green one, and give that red one over there, too."

One of those cars, the silver hardtop O'Neal selected, was a 2004 Rolls-Royce Phantom. That car sold in November 2026 on Bring-a-Trailer for $62,000. Not exactly the $320,000 or so O'Neal likely paid for it when it was new in 2004. Although he was shopping in a Rolls-Royce dealer, Shaq's book mentions a handful of Bentleys as part of the splurge. Whichever British luxury marque made O'Neal's list of picks, it was quite the spendy visit to a dealership.

Shaq's financial flexing and what is likely the most impressive commission that Rolls-Royce salesperson has ever made makes for a pretty crazy story. Believe it or not, it gets even more wild from there. According to O'Neal's book, "Iron" Mike himself, Mike Tyson, was in the mood for Bentley shopping just as the Laker found himself at the dealership.

Shaq recalled seeing Tyson picking out a couple of Bentleys and the need to out-do the champion pugilist. "Next thing I know Tyson picked out two. He's buying two of these cars, so you know what I've got to do. I've got to buy three!" Though it's not clear what ratio of Rolls-Royce to Bentleys made up his impulse shopping spree.

Be it Iron Mike or the mouthy salesperson who drove Shaq to pull the trigger, the outlandishly expensive price tag for the purchase hit him after. "A million dollars gone in 10 minutes," the massive athlete once lamented in an interview with Vanity Fair. Frankly, that's not the only time O'Neal has had the rotten luck with luxury cars. After spending the money to have it customized to fit his considerable frame, his Lucid Air Coupe ended up in a salvage yard less than two years later. Bummer.

#5759. Smarter Cooling, Stronger Reliability: Advancing Wind Turbine Gearbox Performance

As the wind energy industry matures, operators and service providers are increasingly focused on maximizing turbine availability while controlling long-term maintenance costs. Although major drivetrain components often receive the spotlight, one overlooked system can have an outsized impact on turbine reliability and performance: gearbox oil cooling.

Heat exchangers play a critical role in maintaining proper oil temperature and viscosity within wind turbine gearboxes. When cooling systems become clogged with airborne debris, dust, or organic material, oil temperatures rise, lubrication performance declines, and component stress increases. Over time, these conditions can contribute to reduced gearbox efficiency, elevated maintenance demands, and avoidable downtime.

“Wind operators today are balancing increasing performance expectations with the realities of aging fleets and rising maintenance costs. Technologies that improve cooling efficiency and reduce service exposure can have a meaningful impact on long-term turbine reliability and operational profitability,” according to Mike Erickson, HYDAC’s Market Manager for the Wind Industry.

Traditional “panel cut” heat exchanger designs have long been susceptible to clogging due to their tightly packed fin structures. In many wind environments — particularly agricultural, dusty, or high-pollen regions — debris accumulation can severely restrict airflow. Oil leaks can further compound the issue by trapping contaminants within the exchanger core, making cleaning more difficult and labor intensive.

This operational challenge has driven the need for more resilient cooling technologies designed specifically for real-world wind farm conditions.

HYDAC’s new “Square Wave” heat exchanger design represents a significant advancement in addressing these reliability concerns. Engineered to allow debris to pass more freely through the exchanger fins, the design minimizes clogging while improving cooling efficiency under demanding operating conditions.

The importance of effective cooling cannot be overstated. Wind turbine gearbox oil typically operates within an optimal temperature range of approximately 45°C to 65°C. Even modest temperature increases can significantly impact oil viscosity. According to field data, a 12°C rise in oil temperature can reduce viscosity by nearly 28 percent — affecting lubrication quality and accelerating wear on critical drivetrain components.

Field trials have demonstrated measurable operational benefits from the Square Wave design. During a one-year trial on GE 1.x turbines, the upgraded exchanger consistently reduced clogging and lowered maintenance requirements compared to traditional panel cut cores. Operators reported reduced up-tower cleaning time and fewer temperature-related cooling faults.

Field validation across multiple turbine platforms has further reinforced the operational advantages of the Square Wave design. In one case study involving legacy 1 MW-class turbines operating in the Pacific Northwest, a major renewable energy operator installed pilot sets of Square Wave heat exchangers as part of a cooling performance evaluation. Installation teams reported the upgraded exchangers integrated seamlessly into the existing platform with no retrofit complications.

After initial operating data demonstrated improved cooling consistency, the operator expanded the evaluation program with additional units. Long-term SCADA analysis later showed turbines equipped with Square Wave technology operate approximately 20 to 25 percent cooler than comparable turbines using conventional heat exchanger designs under similar operating conditions. The data also highlighted another important advantage: the Square Wave cores continued to maintain airflow and cooling efficiency in debris-heavy environments where traditional exchanger designs became increasingly restricted over time.

The successful deployment also generated interest from additional wind service organizations seeking retrofit-ready cooling upgrades that could improve turbine reliability while reducing maintenance burdens across aging fleets.

“What operators are increasingly recognizing is that small improvements in thermal management can create meaningful gains in turbine reliability, maintenance efficiency, and long-term asset performance,” said Erickson. “The field data we’re seeing continues to validate the value of designing cooling systems specifically for real-world wind operating environments.”

More recently, long-term SCADA data from Mitsubishi platforms further validated the technology’s performance. After two years of operation, turbines equipped with Square Wave heat exchangers operated approximately 19 to 25 percent cooler than reference turbines using standard exchanger designs under comparable operating conditions.

For wind asset owners and service providers, the implications are substantial: Improved thermal management supports healthier oil viscosity, more stable gearbox operation, and potentially longer component life. At the same time, reduced cleaning frequency and fewer cooling-related service events translate directly into lower operational expenditures and increased turbine availability.

As the industry continues to prioritize lifecycle optimization and energy production efficiency, innovative subsystem upgrades such as advanced heat exchanger technology are becoming increasingly valuable. Rather than waiting for costly failures or performance degradation, operators are seeking practical retrofit solutions that improve reliability without requiring major system redesigns.

“Effective thermal management is critical to protecting gearbox health and maintaining oil performance over time. By addressing one of the most common causes of cooling degradation — debris accumulation — operators can improve uptime while reducing unnecessary maintenance interventions,” said HYDAC’s Erickson.

For wind stakeholders evaluating ways to improve gearbox reliability, reduce maintenance exposure, and enhance turbine performance, cooling system optimization deserves greater attention. Proven retrofit technologies like advanced heat exchanger designs can offer a practical path toward improved uptime and operational efficiency without requiring major system overhauls.

As fleets age and performance expectations continue to rise, collaboration between operators, service providers, and technology partners will be essential to building more resilient wind assets for the future. Now is the time to evaluate whether existing cooling systems are supporting — or limiting — long-term turbine reliability goals.

“HYDAC’s goal is to help wind owners and service providers solve practical operational challenges in ways that create long-term value across the entire asset lifecycle. Innovations like the Square Wave heat exchanger are designed to improve reliability, simplify maintenance, and support greater energy production over time.”

Don’t let cooling system limitations impact turbine performance.

Proactive cooling optimization can help operators reduce maintenance exposure, protect critical drivetrain components, and maximize energy production across the asset lifecycle. To learn how HYDAC’s Square Wave heat exchanger technology is helping wind operators improve reliability and lower operating costs, connect with our wind energy specialists today or visit: https://www.hydac.com/en-us/industry-solutions/renewable-energy/wind-power/

#5758. Colorado nonprofit launches plug-in solar program ahead of new state law

By Marie DeFreitas, Contributor | November 10, 2026

A new Colorado nonprofit is looking to bring distributed solar to households that have traditionally been left out of the residential solar market.

Solar Access Colorado, which bills itself as the state’s first organization dedicated to plug-in and balcony solar systems, has launched with pre-orders for small-scale solar kits ahead of the implementation of Colorado House Bill 26-1007, also known as the Improve Customer Use of Distributed Resources bill. The legislation establishes consumer protections for qualifying portable-scale solar devices, including provisions intended to make adoption easier for renters and homeowners living in communities governed by homeowners associations.

The Denver-based organization is positioning plug-in solar as an alternative for residents who cannot install traditional rooftop systems because they rent, live in multifamily buildings or lack suitable roof space.

“Colorado has taken an important step toward expanding access to distributed solar energy,” said Adam Harris, founder of Solar Access Colorado. “We’re launching Solar Access Colorado to help renters, apartment residents and homeowners participate in the clean energy transition with practical, accessible solar solutions.”

HB26-1007 creates a framework for personal, portable-scale solar generation devices. Beginning Jan. 1, 2027, the law will protect qualifying systems from unreasonable restrictions on their installation, use and operation.

The legislation also addresses HOA restrictions by treating qualifying portable-scale solar devices as energy efficiency measures under Colorado’s common-interest-community laws. This could help expand access to solar for residents who previously faced restrictions on making property modifications.

However, the law does not eliminate all installation requirements. Reasonable safety-related restrictions remain permissible, including provisions addressing secure mounting, fire prevention, electrical overload and potential property damage.

Solar Access Colorado plans to help consumers understand the new regulatory landscape and navigate product requirements and installation considerations as the law takes effect. Its initial product offering is designed for balconies, patios, fences and other outdoor spaces, giving residents additional options for generating electricity without committing to a conventional rooftop installation.

Beyond product sales, Solar Access Colorado plans to work with municipalities, housing organizations, property managers and community groups to broaden access to plug-in solar. The nonprofit also intends to prioritize education and outreach to low-income communities and multifamily households.

As states explore policies supporting smaller, customer-sited generation systems, plug-in solar could become another entry point for residential clean energy adoption. Colorado’s upcoming legislation is providing a framework for that market to develop.

#5757. Australian Plugin Vehicle Market Steady in October — Update

… with a spotlight on the Great Wall Motor ORA 5 rising star.

CleanTechnica readers were probably hoping for the surge to keep surging Down Under. I was. However, it appears to have remained steady. Not the flash in the pan petrolheads were hoping for at least. In September 2026, 108,778 passenger vehicles were sold, including all drivetrains. See the charts here.

Battery electric vehicles (BEVs) achieved a market share of 24.2% (26,287 units), outselling petrol-only and diesel-only vehicles. Add in 10,072 plugin hybrid (PHEV) sales and you get a plugin penetration rate of approximately 34%. PHEV sales are the fastest growing segment in Australia — increasing 122% year on year. For those who care, 17,843 HEVs sold in October (a YoY increase of 20%). I am looking forward to peak HEV, followed by peak PHEV, as we have seen in more mature Scandinavian markets.

Australia’s EV penetration rate (including BEV and PHEV) has hovered in the low to mid 30% for the last few months. Not sure what the future holds, but even just maintaining this rate for a few months will make a difference. Nothing beats lived experience. My neighbours are now asking about how much it costs to fill up. One came for coffee yesterday and stayed to go for a drive in our new Model Y. He was very impressed with the tech, the finish, and above all the running costs. He confided that he had just filled up his wife’s Honda Civic — AU$82 — and his Isuzu D Max — AU$121.

On a recent shopping expedition, we counted 10 electric cars in the car park, out of 50 vehicles. Not just Tesla and BYD, but also Zeekr, Geely, the GWM ORA 5, and a Deepal. Battery electric vehicles are making their presence felt. But not all my friends can see them. They can spot Tesla and BYD vehicles, but they tell me they don’t see any other EVs on the road. Yes, we have tried to educate them, but it is not working just yet. “See if it has an exhaust pipe. Does it have a sealed front?”

One car that I am seeing in ever increasing numbers is the refreshed Great Wall Motors ORA — now in an SUV body and a much cheaper price. When it launched back in 2023, I expected great things (see our review here). Sadly, the Funky Cat (as it is known in China) did not live up to expectations. It was the cheapest EV in Australia at the time (AU$46,000) but couldn’t compete with the BYD Dolphin and the MG 4. Now, relaunched as an SUV and with a more appropriate price (AU$34,000 — scratch that, just got an email that knocks another grand off the price), I am seeing them everywhere — 6 on the highway just yesterday.

So, on to the best-selling BEVs on the Australian market in October 2026:

Pretty much the same as last month. Obviously, BYD was the best-selling brand, closely followed by Tesla. BYD has six cars among the top twenty BEVs. There are no cars from Korean, Japanese, or European carmakers in the top ten BEV numbers. When all powertrains are considered, BYD is the second highest selling brand behind Toyota in the Australian market. There seems to be some talk in the auto news about Tesla and BYD being considered “legacy” brands — who would have thought?

Expect big things from Geely as it aggressively expands throughout Australia. Geely recently announced that it has opened 100 dealerships over the past 18 months. Its EX5 sales figures are putting pressure on the competition in the medium-SUV-segment Mazda CX-5, Hyundai Tucson, Mitsubishi Outlander, and Nissan X-Trail.

Utes still dominate the Australian market. When a compelling BEV offering becomes available, the top ten may change again. I was excited to see the KGM Musso BEV ute, but it has failed to gain traction. Only 269 have sold in the past few months. The Toyota BEV Hilux only has 14 units in the country — likely to be demonstrators. PHEV utes seem to be doing much better, led by the BYD Shark. Over 15,000 have sold so far this year.

Now for some newsy titbits: Dealers for established brands are calling for a temporary ban on new car brands (read Chinese made cars) entering Australia, as they cannot compete. The boss of the Australian Automotive Dealer Association says the industry has reached a crisis point, with the last quarter delivering the first loss since the global financial crisis. Chinese brands now own 36% of new car sales.

“This is a clear warning that the commercial settings underpinning our industry are becoming unsustainable,” Mr Voortman said. He speculates on the possible risks to consumers: “… the influx of new brands also puts consumers at risk, as it can lead to rapid depreciation of vehicles, a lag in parts supply, longer repair times, and orphaned vehicles should those companies choose to leave the country.”

Though, what we are seeing in Australia is that legacy brands, mainly European, are the ones leaving the country.

The FUD continues as misconceptions and outdated information are rolled out as facts. If you want to revisit these arguments, check this out. Trigger warning: some of you may wish to yell at the screen. The author twists himself in knots trying to make the argument that the wrong Australians are buying electric vehicles. I would contend that the right people are buying EVs — those commuting from the outer suburbs of our sprawling cities. Data shows that it is these people who are behind the EV sales boom we are currently experiencing, and these are the ones benefitting most from not paying inflated petrol costs caused by Iran’s unwillingness to admit defeat and grovel at the feet of the orange one. Please excuse my sarcasm. All of this is documented in the 10th annual Electric Vehicle Council Report. We reviewed this report here.

Despite the desperate attempt to promulgate fear of “these here new-fangled doodads” — the creation of doubt and uncertainly — every month shows how intelligent the average consumer is as they seek a better, electrified, and brighter future.

#5756. Experience The Gales Of November With This Real-Time Great Lakes Simulator

The wreck of the SS Edmund Fitzgerald became a bit of a meme last year during the 50th anniversary of the disaster. Gen Zers rediscovered the great Gordon Lightfoot song and it was all over the interwebz. Those youths can now experience the thrill, terror and wonder of the Great Lakes firsthand with my new favorite obsession, the website In The Lakes; a free site which shows you the real-time conditions on any one of the great lakes without tracking your data.

Using buoys located on Lake Superior, Lake Michigan, Lake Huron, Lake Erie, and Lake Ontario and honorable mention Lake St. Claire, In The Lake users can view a digital simulation of conditions. Different colors on the map represent real-time wave heights. As of the writing of this story, both Lake Superior and Lake Michigan are mostly red, indicative of choppy waters and waves reaching 10 feet or more. My first "Float" sent me just north of Sault Ste. Marie in Lake Superior where it was, predictably, cold, grey, and extra choppy — just the sort of thing the Edmund Fitz faced on that cold day in November, 1975.

But it's not all rough seas and wind. Pick a calmer section of lake and spend a few hours of your work day sipping a beverage, listening to a radio that features local radio stations as well as multiple genre stations, or meditate. The meditate feature is fairly entertaining, with inspirational messages meant to resonant with folks from the Great Lakes. The last time I pressed it, a message popped up reading "Somewhere, someone's shoveling a driveway. Not you. Not today." Damn right.

But it's not just floaties. You can find the option to "Take the helm in these conditions." And the familiar lake scene will load with a kayak, schooner, or even a Coast Guard ice breaking ship. I tried out the kayak to see what it was like for my husband when he puttered around Lake Superior on his own kayak, a feat that nearly killed him. You can also dive the depths of the lakes, or write a message and pop it in a bottle, then set it into the lake and see where the real forecast takes it.

In the Experiences tab you can take on several adventures from repairing the Cort for sailing duties to Cruising the Soo Locks. There's also a plethora of adventures to go on. I opted for the Brigade to the Rendezvous, an 1815 canoe trip to deliver beaver furs to Fort William, a midway trading point between the Midwest and Montreal. Teachers can also find a dedicated hub with tons of resources to go over critical things related to the Great Lakes, appropriate for certain grade levels.

Or keep it simple and continue to enjoy the occasional float on one of the Great Lakes. You can use it to escape your kids as easily as educate them, after all. An extra click will allow your setting to reset (change) every five minutes without your interference. So relax, ride the waves, and beware the gales of November.

#5755. A Tribute to Bunker Roy: The Man Who Turned Village Grandmothers into Solar Engineers

Sanjit “Bunker” Roy, who taught illiterate grandmothers to become solar engineers, passed away peacefully this morning at his home in Tilonia, Rajasthan, aged 81.[1] I first encountered his work not in India, but in the rain forests of Borneo.

It was October 2016, and as night fell, the surrounding Borneo tropical rain forest had come alive with noise, dominated by the trill of frogs and the rhythmic pulse of cicadas. I was about to embark on the Sabah study tour of the Emerging Leaders Dialogue Asia (ELDA). Over the next two weeks, we would visit timber plantations, mountain communities recovering from a deadly earthquake, and marine parks.

But undoubtedly the highlight for me was discovering the inspiring story of Barefoot College, which trains village grandmothers to be solar engineers. On our first evening, while enjoying a mini feast of banana fritters, pomelo, and langsat (a bit like lychees but more delicious), we were told about the college’s remarkable partnership with the Sabah Women Entrepreneurs and Professionals Association (SWEPA) in Borneo.

Barefoot College was the brainchild of Bunker Roy, and he was an unlikely revolutionary. Born into privilege in Bengal in 1945, he was educated at the Doon School and St Stephen’s College, Delhi—two of India’s most elite institutions—and was a national squash champion. A conventional career beckoned. Then, during the Bihar famine of the mid-1960s, he saw rural poverty firsthand, and his life changed course.[2] When he told his mother he wanted to live and work in a village, he later joked, she “went into a coma” and didn’t speak to him for a long time.[3]

In 1972, Roy settled in Tilonia, a dusty village in Rajasthan, and founded the Social Work and Research Centre (SWRC), which later became known as Barefoot College. Its rules turned conventional education on its head. Anyone with a university degree was disqualified from studying there, and no degrees were awarded. In 2011, Roy said that nobody at the college earned more than $100 a month. The campus itself was built in 1986 by a dozen local “barefoot architects” who could not read or write, and it went on to win an Aga Khan Award for Architecture. At the college’s night schools, children aged 6 to 14 elect their own prime minister and cabinet.[4]

Roy’s guiding conviction was simple: “There is no urban solution to a rural problem.”[5] He was awarded the Padma Shri, one of India’s highest civilian honors, in 1986, and named one of Time magazine’s 100 most influential people in 2010.[6] He is survived by his wife, Aruna Roy, the former civil servant who led the grassroots movement behind India’s Right to Information Act.[7]

The positive impact of his work has been immense. By the college’s own count at its 50th anniversary in 2022, it had trained 1,708 illiterate and semi-literate rural women from 96 countries as solar engineers, bringing light to more than a million people in over 75,000 households and saving about 45 million liters of kerosene. Its rainwater harvesting had captured some 70 billion liters and brought safe drinking water to more than 1,650 schools. More than 90,000 working children had studied in its night schools, and its barefoot health workers had treated over 300,000 patients.[8]

During our study tour, SWEPA arranged for us to meet one of Barefoot College’s beneficiaries, a so-called solar mama. She was part of the program that takes illiterate grandmothers from poor rural villages and, within six months, trains them at the college in India for their new job. They return to their villages to install and maintain full solar lighting systems. Roy deliberately chose grandmothers because they are rooted in their communities; men and younger people, he found, were more likely to take their new skills and leave for the city.[9] Because the women shared no common language, much of the training was done through sign language and hands-on practice.[10]

Malaysia’s first solar mama—very likely the woman I met that evening—was Tarihing Masanim, from Kampung Sonsogon Magandai, near Kota Marudu in Sabah. She completed her training in India in 2015 and returned to bring electricity to around 100 homes in her village.[11] Roy loved to tell stories like hers. One of his favorites was about a 55-year-old Afghan grandmother who electrified 200 houses and set an Afghan engineering department head straight on the difference between AC and DC.[12]

The story of the solar mamas captures many elements of what it means to harness the power of responsibility to create a more inclusive society and economy. So many barriers are being broken through, as these amazing women challenge stereotypes about age, gender, and rural capacity. Barefoot College shows that sustainable development—including adopting sustainable technologies—is an option for people living with low incomes in remote villages. The Barefoot model empowers people to be agents of their own destiny.

Bunker Roy stands out as an inspiring example of inclusive leadership. His impact, which is so strongly evidenced by the solar engineers program, is as much about restoring dignity to those who are traditionally excluded as it is about developing or transferring practical skills. Roy, who said he had “a very elitist, snobbish, expensive education in India” that “almost destroyed” him, explained:

“So we started the Barefoot College, and we redefined professionalism. Who is a professional? A professional is someone who has a combination of competence, confidence and belief. A water diviner is a professional. A traditional midwife is a professional. A traditional bone setter is a professional. These are professionals all over the world. You find them in any inaccessible village around the world. And we thought that these people should come into the mainstream and show that the knowledge and skills that they have is universal.”[13]

Roy ended that talk with a line often attributed to Mahatma Gandhi, whose principles guided his life’s work: “First they ignore you, then they laugh at you, then they fight you, and then you win.”[14] For the solar mamas of Barefoot College, and for the more than a million people whose homes they have lit, Bunker Roy won.

This article draws from content in Wayne Visser’s book Thriving: The Breakthrough Movement to Regenerate Nature, Society, and the Economy (New York: Fast Company Press). For more information, see waynevisser.com.

#5754. At $14,000, Is This 1993 Toyota MR2 A Red-Hot Deal?

According to its seller, today's Nice Price or No Dice MR2 is a highly optioned model and was once sold on Bring a Trailer. It's been demoted to a Facebook Marketplace ad, and it's up to us to decide whether its price matches that lower-tier venue.

Rumored federal rollover safety standards effectively killed off convertible production in the U.S. from 1976 to the early '80s. The convertibles that boldly remained on the market during this time were all older cars like the MGB and Mercedes-Benz SL.

It was that latter convertible, the two-seater Benz, that served as the role model for the 1977 Cadillac Seville Milan we looked at on Monday. A custom-bodied two-seater convertible, the Milan attempted to fill that obvious gap in North America's car lineup. Unfortunately, the car's odd proportions gave the impression that upon arrival, its occupants would be revealed to be a congress of clowns rather than a chic couple with wind-tousled hair. That factor, plus questions about the car's condition, made us all question the seller's set $8,000 asking price. In short order, that dropped in an 82% 'No Dice' loss.

When it comes to styling and proportions, it's safe to say that Toyota hit the mark with the second-generation MR2. Larger, more cohesive, and roomier, the SW20 edition traded the first MR2's odd mini-Ferrari Mondial look for one more reverent to the better-looking Ferrari 328 GTB. It still checked all the boxes that made the first generation popular: a mid-engine layout, pop-up headlamps, and an optional T-top roof. Where the second-generation car strayed from the first was in size (it's substantially larger in almost all dimensions), and in the breathing of its top-models as the earlier cars' supercharger was traded for a turbo. One other distinction of the SW20 (perhaps unfairly) is that its handling at the limit can bite unwary drivers on the butt.

This 1993 MR2 comes in the officially named Super Red, making it a perfect accompaniment to the Man of Steel's cape and booties. This is a non-turbo car, so horsepower is limited to 135 ponies from the 5S-FE DOHC four. Fortunately, that engine is mated to a five-speed manual to make the most of that output. Even better, the seller says a new clutch was installed 46k miles ago, and the timing belt and water pump were refreshed just 26,000 miles back. New motor mounts, pads and rotors, and T-top seals are additional milestones in the car's service history.

This MR2 looks the part of a well-cared-for car, too. According to the seller, it was bought in Florida and spent much of its life in the South before being brought to New Jersey in 2018. When the present owner made that move, the car had 118,000 miles on the clock. It now has 136,788 miles, which averages about 2,400 miles a year under the current owner's care.

Overall, the car looks to be in great condition. The paint still pops, set off against factory alloys that show no evidence of injury. The engine looks cramped under its cover, and the sweeping buttresses make maintenance even more of a back-breaker, but at least it all looks intact.

The cabin doesn't appear cramped at all and looks quite handsome in its black-and-biscuit color scheme, with swanky leather upholstery on the seating surfaces. It all appears stock in here, save for a TRD-branded leather shift knob, although that does keep it in the family.

On the downside, the seller warns that the A/C compressor is grinding and hence they've removed the belt to keep it from turning and potentially doing more damage. The ad also makes no mention of the tires' age or condition. With so few miles driven each year, it's easy to let tires hang on longer than they should. The MR2 features a staggered setup, with larger, wider wheels in the back, meaning that rotating front to rear is out of the question, making the meats even more out of sight, out of mind.

On the plus side, the ad notes that all of the car's glass has been VIN-etched as an anti-theft measure. In addition, the car comes with a clean title, not one but two options for T-top storage, and three keys. Oddly, though the seller says this was a heavily optioned car, it does not seem to have a remote-entry key fob. Weird. There are also two manila folders full of maintenance records going back to the previous owner. Those will accompany the car in the sale.

On the face of it, this appears to be a solid example of a stock, mostly complete second-gen non-turbo MR2 with all the right specs and the promise of a well-managed service history. What's not to like, right? Well, there's that sweaty-Betty non-working A/C. That's going to cost a pretty penny to fix. We should also probably consider a new set of tires unless, upon inspection, they still seem to be in the game. Other than that, there don't seem to be any major red flags on this car. Plus, who doesn't dig an old MR2?

That only leaves us with the car's $14,000 asking price to either rejoice or bemoan. Let's talk about that. What's your opinion on this MR2 at that $14,000 asking? Does that sound fair given the car's presentation and specs? Or, at that price, is this Mister a miss?

Facebook Marketplace out of Tenafly, New Jersey, or go here if the ad disappears.

#5753. Heliene and Caelux combine PV cells and solar glass for a hybrid module

Heliene, a solar modules manufacturer, and Caelux Corporation, a perovskite solar technology company, are starting commercial production of their Hybrid-Tandem solar module.

This module combines perovskite glass from Caelux and silicon N-type cells from Heliene, placing two energy generating sources in a single solar panel. Heliene is producing the modules in its plant in Mountain Iron, Minnesota, with first deliveries slated for 2027.

“One of the advantages of Caelux’s technology is that we can integrate its Energy Producing Glass into our existing manufacturing lines without having to build an entirely new production setup,” said Martin Pochtaruk, CEO and founder of Heliene. “With both companies manufacturing in the U.S., we’re bringing together two fully domestic supply chains to advance what’s possible with American-made solar, besides the fact that partnering with true friends also makes it fun.”

The companies are reporting a 27% efficiency from the Hybrid-Tandem module. Heliene and Caelux desiged this module to have a higher power density in a smaller project footprint.

“Heliene is a true leader in U.S. manufacturing, and we share a belief that when we lead the market, our customers win. Together, we’re commercializing ultra-high-efficiency solar modules that will set a new bar for American-made solar,” said Caelux CEO Scott Graybeal.

Heliene will showcase the Hybrid-Tandem module at RE+ in Las Vegas from Nov. 17 to 19, at booth #W16546.

#5752. Could an Independent Agency for Vaccine Safety Strengthen Public Trust?

— Daniel Salmon proposes something modeled on the National Transportation Safety Board

Routinely used vaccines in the U.S. are very safe, but questions about potential adverse events still require thorough, credible investigations. Daniel Salmon, PhD, MPH, of the Johns Hopkins Bloomberg School of Public Health in Baltimore, argues that an independent approach to investigating vaccine safety signals could help strengthen public trust.

In a recent invited perspective in the Journal of Infectious Diseases, Salmon and colleagues proposed establishing a National Vaccine Safety Board modeled on the National Transportation Safety Board (NTSB), bringing together existing data and expertise to conduct rapid, independent investigations.

In this MedPage Today video, Salmon explains how such a board would operate and prioritize potential safety concerns, as well as why separating scientific assessments from policy decisions could improve public confidence.

The following is a transcript of his remarks:

I mean, whenever I talk about vaccine safety, and especially when I'm talking about things we could do better, I'm careful to point out that routinely used vaccines in the U.S. are very safe.

Where the idea [for a National Vaccine Safety Board] came from for me was 25 years ago I was at CDC and I was working under Dr. Bob Chen, who ran vaccine safety for CDC, and he brought it up as an analogy. And I really didn't know much about the National Transportation Safety Board. I mean, I knew the name and I'm like, "Oh, those are the people that figured out what happens with plane accidents." And as I learned more about it, I realized how it's a great model for vaccine safety in many, many ways. Most importantly, it's an incredibly trusted organization.

There's a bunch of pieces to this that we outlined in the commentary, and I think that there's ways in which the NTSB model is very useful.

So for one, the party system. So while the NTSB leads the investigation, they work with other parties that have data and expertise, which is really important. So they provide the leadership, they provide the independence.

So in the vaccine-safety world, the analogy would be lots of federal agencies have data, as does industry. And that's great, they should. You want CDC and FDA to be looking at vaccine safety data for their own purposes and their needs. And industry also has data because people make reports to industry and they know their product very well.

But it would be a National Vaccine Safety Board that would lead those efforts. And in doing so, you leverage all the expertise. And they may have their own data just as the NTSB does, but they benefit from everyone's data. But the leadership is independent and it's insulated from other pressures and it makes sure that the best investigation is done and the results are credible.

If you have a vaccine safety signal, you want to investigate it rapidly. So in this case, it's an unusual situation where the speed of spending money is important and they should have the authority to do so.

Are we talking about a vaccine that's given to every child or only used by a much smaller proportion of the population? And is it a vaccine given to vulnerable people like children or pregnant women? How severe is the health event that people are worried about? If we're talking about a low-grade fever, it's probably not going to be a top priority. But is it a serious adverse health outcome? You want to look at scientific concerns because clearly if scientists feel there's a biological mechanism or doctors have concerns, they need to be investigated.

But you also want to look at public concerns because if a lot of people are worried about something, we need science to know. And simply telling people, "Oh, well, we're not worried about that. It's fine," is really kind of insufficient.

So it's a great question. How do you prioritize? I think those are the criteria, and I think it needs public input. It needs input from a broad range of people: the public broadly, parents, pregnant women, doctors, scientists. And it should be a living document because as you're doing science, you should hopefully be able to check things off the list because you've got really good answers. And undoubtedly new things will arise.

So there's a basic principle, and it's well accepted in environmental health and it's well accepted in many domains, which is you need a separation of risk assessment from risk management. And in this case, risk assessment is assessing the safety of vaccines. What is the safety profile of vaccines? That's a scientific question. Now it can be impacted in terms of what you study by the public, but it's fundamentally a scientific question. And risk management is when you consider things like the benefits of vaccines, the cost of vaccines, how hard they would be to implement, or easy.

And there needs to be a separation from risk assessment from risk management so the people that are doing the science around vaccine safety are insulated or protected, but we need that science to be done independently and that will really lead to trust. And we see that with NTSB as a model, as well as other examples.

→ Baca artikel asli

#5751. Oh, So That’s Who’s Buying All Those New Subaru Trailseeker EVs…

After EV sales in the US crashed into a brick wall last year, the Subaru Trailseeker has been one of the few points of light. Newly introduced this year, Trailseeker has racked up a relatively decent sales track record in the crowded SUV field. As for whether or not Subaru can continue to grow its EV sales in the US, the automaker’s elaborate, multi-faceted “Subaru Loves Pets” campaign has emerged as the secret weapon…

The Subaru Loves Pets campaign first appeared 18 years ago, back in 2008. A lot of demographic and cultural water has passed under the bridge since then. More so than in past generations, pets have taken on the role of family members equal to, if not superior to, anyone else in the household. And if that’s the case, shouldn’t pets have their own seatbelts, too?

Now Subaru’s longtime relationship with the pet-centered population is beginning to muscle into EV territory. One indication of a connection between pet and EV preferences surfaced in October, when Subaru released a new report indicating that pets wield a noticeable influence on purchasing  preferences in the US auto market overall.

Commissioned from the firm Talker Research, the report is based on interviews with 2,000 and pet owners in the US. Of that cohort, a resounding majority of 73% said they are more likely to drive for the holidays, specifically in order to travel with their pet.

“As the holidays approach, more than two-thirds (68%) said their pet will affect their travel plans this year, and nearly half (45%) have turned down travel opportunities that couldn’t accommodate their pet,” Subaru adds.

Households with pets also have distinct priorities for a vehicle. “Plenty of room between pets, passengers, and luggage tops the list of features pet owners say matter when traveling with an animal,” Suburu reports.

“More than one in four (26%) prioritize cargo space for crates, carriers, beds, or gear. Among hybrid and EV drivers, that climbs to 35%,” the automaker further notes.

If you missed the money quote, it’s that thing about 35% of hybrid and EV drivers prioritizing space for pet gear, compared to just 26% of drivers overall. In addition to the traveling-with-pets angle, that generational difference also reflects a sharp generational divergence in attitudes towards EVs.

“Electric vehicles have become a political flashpoint in Washington. But new national polling finds the biggest divide among potential EV buyers isn’t political. It’s generational,” reports the advocacy organization ZETA (the Zero Emission Transportation Association) in September.

“Seventy-five percent of adults ages 18–34 and 72% of adults ages 35–49 say they are likely to purchase or lease a new EV within the next five years,” ZETA elaborates. “Among adults ages 50–64, that number falls to 50%. Among those 65 and older, it drops to just 23%, a striking 52-point gap between the youngest and oldest Americans.”

Now let’s take another look at the Subaru survey. Talker Research found that 75% of Gen Z and 70% of millennials traveled with a pet within the past five years, compared with just 58% of Gen X and only 40% of baby boomers (looking at you, Mitt Romney).

A similar generational difference surfaced in other areas of the survey:

Younger pet owners are also more likely to say they plan travel around their pet, with 42% of Gen Z and millennials doing so compared with 27% of Gen X and boomers.

Among pet owners who haven’t yet road-tripped with their pet, 74% of Gen Z and millennials would consider it, compared with 54% of Gen X and boomers.

Subaru pulled back on its overall electrification plan for the US market after last year’s EV sales crash, but it did follow through on the new Trailseeker, offered for sale on a limited basis in January of this year. Volume deliveries to dealers began in April of this year, and in May the Trailseeker racked up 1,074 in sales. The number is vanishingly small compared to industry leader Tesla but a promising start in the challenging US market. By August, Trailseeker had a total of almost 5,000 in sales for the year to its credit, rising to 6,006 sold by the end of October.

All else being equal, recognition from Wards Auto may help keep the momentum going. On October 13, Subaru announced that the Trailseeker earned a slot in the 2026 Wards 10 Best Engines & Propulsion Systems list.

“For 2026, Wards evaluated 26 powertrains from returning winners as well as all-new or significantly updated cars, trucks, or SUVs. They were evaluated on several criteria, including power, torque, refinement, efficiency, and innovation,” Subaru notes.

“Its 375 horsepower combined with immediate electric powertrain response always satisfies. Its range estimates also hold up well in real-world driving, interstate and all. Yet Subaru keeps the price completely reasonable given such capability,” said Robin Warner, who manages the 10 Best Engines & Propulsion Systems program.

By “completely reasonable,” Warner means the Trailseeker’s starting MSRP of $39,995 and its estimated range of 281 miles. According to Subaru, the Trailseeker can go from 10% to 80% charged in as little as 28 minutes, with up to 150 kW on a DC-fast charger.

“With an onboard battery preconditioning system, enabled either by navigating to a charging destination or by the driver via touchscreen controls, the Trailseeker can charge its battery in cold climates — as low as 14 degrees Fahrenheit — at speeds comparable to those in normal weather conditions,” Subaru also advises.

In addition to the seal of approval from Wards, on October 29 Subaru announced that Trailseeker earned the “Car of the Year” award in the 2027 TopGear.com US Car Awards program

The automaker cites Top Gear US Correspondent Alex Kalogiannis, who was particularly enthused about the vehicle’s off-road performance. ““Off the beaten path, the set-and-forget X-MODE figures out all the tricky terrain stuff as you trundle through the bush, and with about 8.5 inches of ground clearance, it can scale all but the scariest obstacles in your path,” Kalogiannis remarked, somwhat colorfully.

“People still carry a torch for the WRX and its many rally-inspired incarnations, but the heart of Subaru fandom now is its dutiful, rugged, useful wagon – the Trailseeker,” he added.

That remains to be seen. Trailseeker will have to compete for the hearts and minds of Subaru fans against other models in the automaker’s lineup, including gasmobiles. The SUV-styled Outback wagon, for example, clocked 118,470 in sales from January to the end of October this year, and that was not the only Subaru model to hit six figures.

Still, US President Donald Trump has provided Subaru and other automakers with a new opportunity grow their EV sales profile this year. On February 28 the President launched his war against Iran, sending global oil prices on an upwards trajectory and improving the affordability case for EVs regardless of the loss of the $7,500 federal EV tax credit, which Trump and his Republican majority in Congress axed last fall.

Ever since dropping the first bombs on Iran, Trump has repeatedly claimed that the war is over, or almost over, or over any minute. In the latest pronouncement, on  Monday Trump said that multilateral negotiations over a peace settlement in Iran are in a state of “positive momentum toward an end.” And if you believe that…

Photo: Subaru has racked up a fairly respectable EV sales record so far this year for its new Trailseeker SUV (2026 Trailseeker cropped, courtesy of Subaru).
