#5750. What Car Looks Best On Aftermarket Wheels?

Let's face it: Most stock wheels are, at best, fine. Occasionally one will stand out for its looks, usually because it's been made by Mercedes, but most OEM wheels are just some basic metal that you can wrap a tire around. That's good enough for getting from point A to point B, but we car enthusiasts often want better. Enter the entire world of aftermarket wheels, in any style you could dream up — rally turbofans, classic five-spokes, even teddy bears.

Today, though, we're not here to talk about the wheels themselves. We're here to talk about the cars they go on, and that brings us to a very broad question: What car looks best on aftermarket wheels? I'll accept answers to this in two forms, either cars that most need aftermarket wheels or cars that look best on the most different designs of aftermarket wheel. Are those criteria wide-open enough for you?

For both versions of the question, I think there's a single answer: The Toyobaru, most specifically the first-generation Scion FR-S/Subaru BRZ. It's a car that looks great on lightweight track wheels like Volk TE37s or Titan 7 T-S5s, but its style is just as well-suited to a set of bags and a BBS mesh design. Even a vintage wheel like the RS Watanabe, when scaled up to modern fitment, looks great on the Toyobaru chassis.

I think the car that looks best on aftermarket wheels is the first-generation Scion FR-S/Subaru BRZ, but what do you think? Do you think the Civic most needs a new set of shoes, or that the Lexus LS400 can do no wrong with any style of wheel face? Let me know in the comments below, and I'll collect my favorite answers later in the week. Bonus points if you've tried aftermarket wheels on a chassis yourself.

#5749. Norway’s Electric Snowplow Worked. Sweden Added Hydrogen Anyway.

Norway has already demonstrated battery-electric snowplowing on one of its toughest mountain highways. A Scania 45 R covered roughly 7,000 km on Hardangervidda, plowing, grading and spreading sand through a Norwegian winter. Its performance exceeded expectations. Meanwhile, Sweden has unveiled a hydrogen snowplow that is, in fact, another large battery-electric truck, with a fuel cell and hydrogen tanks added for extra range. The engineering question is why that second energy system is worth having when batteries are already doing the work, and when a conventional generator running on renewable diesel could offer a simpler, potentially cheaper route to occasional extended operation.

The Swedish vehicle has to compete with more than diesel snowplows. My full TFIE Strategy Briefing examines Norway’s measured electric operation, China’s expanding snow-removal equipment market and the additional costs hydrogen must justify.

Sweden’s project brings together Scania, MaserFrakt and Peab. The vehicle carries a 356 kWh rechargeable battery and 56 kg of hydrogen. The fuel cell generates electricity to extend the truck’s operating time between charging sessions. That gives the vehicle two energy-storage systems, plus the equipment to convert hydrogen into electricity. It will still need electric charging, and it will also need hydrogen production, delivery, storage and refueling.

Norway’s experience makes the starting point uncomfortable for hydrogen advocates. The Norwegian Public Roads Administration’s 2025 trial demonstrated that a production-based heavy electric truck could do demanding winter-maintenance work. Its measured consumption averaged approximately 2.02 kWh/km, with around six and a half hours of operating endurance under evaluated mixed-duty conditions. The trial identified shortcomings in charging compatibility and payload, but these were engineering and operating problems to resolve, not evidence that battery-electric snowplows were incapable of doing the job.

China is moving toward broader deployment. Municipalities have used smaller electric snow-removal equipment for years, while manufacturers have developed full-sized specialized electric vehicles. Beijing’s Mentougou district awarded contracts for 24 multifunction snow-removal trucks in September 2026. The final chassis specifications have not all been confirmed as battery-electric, but the procurement demonstrates a market moving beyond isolated demonstrations.

China’s much larger battery-electric truck industry is also providing specialized equipment manufacturers with an expanding supply of powertrains, batteries and heavy-duty chassis. Electric snowplows benefit from components developed and manufactured for freight vehicles at much greater volumes.

The Swedish approach looks increasingly difficult to justify against that background. If six or seven hours of productive operation is adequate, a battery-electric truck can already do the work. If longer shifts are required, larger batteries, better charging arrangements, mobile charging equipment or operational changes deserve assessment first. Hydrogen cannot claim the extreme-duty market merely because charging requires planning.

There is another alternative that the hydrogen comparison tends to omit. A conventional generator running on hydrotreated vegetable oil, or HVO, could supply additional electricity when the battery needs support. Diesel generators, fuel tanks and liquid-fuel distribution systems are mature technologies. Major generator manufacturers already approve suitable equipment for HVO, which can use conventional diesel-type storage and handling systems. Integrating a generator into a particular electric truck still requires engineering, certification and emissions controls, but it avoids constructing a hydrogen supply chain for a small fleet of seasonal vehicles.

For some operators, the even easier solution is to retain existing diesel snow-removal equipment and use certified renewable diesel for the relatively few extreme shifts that electric vehicles cannot cover. Copenhagen Airport has already adopted that approach, progressively electrifying its equipment while using HVO in compatible heavy vehicles, including snow-removal machines.

HVO is not unlimited, and its climate benefits depend on feedstock origin and production. Burning it also produces exhaust emissions. These constraints are important, particularly in populated areas. But a relatively small quantity used for exceptional winter duty is a different proposition from trying to replace a large share of road transportation with biofuels.

The hydrogen proposal must beat all these alternatives. It needs to demonstrate that the additional hours gained from the fuel cell are worth the vehicle cost, maintenance, fuel premium and refueling infrastructure. It also needs to outperform the cost of providing those hours with conventional generating equipment or retaining a small reserve fleet.

Snow-removal authorities have an obligation to maintain roads during severe weather. They should purchase equipment that meets that obligation with the lowest defensible whole-system cost and emissions. Norway has shown that batteries can perform demanding winter work, and China’s industrial scale is making specialized electric vehicles more accessible. Sweden’s hydrogen truck is entering a market in which the simpler electric solution is already working.

The full TFIE Strategy Briefing analysis examines the operating results, procurement evidence, charging limitations, airport fuel-cell trials and the practical alternatives that make hydrogen’s business case so difficult.

#5748. Novel ADC Boosts Survival in Pretreated Endometrial Cancer

— Sac-TMT is currently under review at the FDA for a post-platinum/immunotherapy indication

Results from the TroFuse-005 trial presented at the annual European Society for Medical Oncology congress showed that the investigational antibody-drug conjugate (ADC) sacituzumab tirumotecan (sac-TMT) significantly improved progression-free survival (PFS) and overall survival compared with physician's choice of chemotherapy in patients with advanced or recurrent endometrial cancer who had previously been treated with platinum-based chemotherapy and immunotherapy.

In this exclusive MedPage Today video, investigator Domenica Lorusso, MD, PhD, of Humanitas University in Italy, discusses the phase III findings, which are currently under review with the FDA.

Following is a transcript of her remarks:

In TroFuse-005, we did compare sac-TMT versus treatment of physician's choice (between doxorubicin or weekly paclitaxel) in advanced or recurrent endometrial cancer patients [previously] exposed to platinum [chemotherapy] and an immune checkpoint inhibitor, given separately or in combination. The trial had a dual primary endpoint, [PFS] and overall survival -- [PFS] evaluated by blinded independent central review -- and secondary endpoints were overall response rate, duration of response, and quality of life.

The trial met both the primary endpoints. A statistically significant and clinically relevant 41% reduction in the risk of progression was reported in patients treated with sac-TMT, with a median PFS increasing from 3.9 to 7.2 months.

But also a statistically significant and clinically relevant reduction in the risk of death was reported in patients treated with sac-TMT -- the hazard ratio was 0.68, so a 32% reduction in the risk of death, with a median overall survival increasing from 12 to 19 months.

And a significant increase in overall response rate was reported, with overall response rate doubling in patients treated with sac-TMT -- overall response rate was 17% versus 35% -- with a median duration of response of 6.9 months in the standard arm versus 11.1 months in the experimental arm.

This is the most important aspect. This did not impact the quality of life of the patient. From baseline to the pre-specified week 12, there was no difference between quality-of-life scores [between] treatment groups.

So, for all these reasons -- increased [survival] advantage, manageable toxicity profile, no impact in quality of life -- we really believe that this drug should be considered the new standard of care in this population and the new control arm in the next generation of clinical trials.

#5747. Honda Goes All-In On Hybrids With New $3 Billion Plant In Ohio

Good morning! It's Tuesday, November 10, 2026, and this is The Morning Shift, your daily roundup of the top automotive headlines from around the world, in one place. This is where you'll find the most important stories that are shaping the way Americans drive and get around.

In this morning's edition, Honda is spending billions on a new hybrid vehicle plant in Ohio, Waymos still love blasting past stopped school buses, Stellantis angers a Canadian auto workers union by officially selling its Brampton assembly plant to a defense contractor, and NHTSA is probing 1.2 million Fords because their side airbags are allegedly burning passengers when they're deployed.

If you'd like a recap of the latest auto news sent to your inbox each weekday, you can sign up for Jalopnik's free Morning Shift newsletter here.

Honda's EV plans flopped hard and cost the Japanese automaker big time. Now, it's pivoting to hybrids in a huge way, and a large part of that is a brand new $3 billion factory in central Ohio that is meant to boost capacity for its most popular vehicles. The new plant is slated to start cranking out vehicles in 2029, expanding its output by 240,000 vehicles per year.

The move is an inflection point for Honda — part of a strategic pivot that started earlier this year when it threw in the towel on electric vehicles in America. The write-down on that decision cost the company $15.7 billion and caused its first annual loss since going public in 1957. Now, it aims to introduce 15 new hybrids globally by March of 2030, with North America as the primary target. From Bloomberg:

The new facility will be located between two existing auto assembly plants it operates in Ohio. It will be tooled to build gas-only, hybrid and fully electric vehicles, but the company didn't provide a breakdown by production volume.

Honda also wouldn't say which vehicles will be made in the new plant, but its focus will be on boosting output of models offering a hybrid option, including all-new models.

In a separate statement, it announced an investment of C$166 million ($118 million) in its Canadian factory in Alliston, Ontario, to make next-generation vehicles and for "the reintroduction of engine manufacturing."

Honda was prepared to spend C$15 billion on an EV battery supply chain complex in Canada, but put that plan on indefinite hold in May. It also canceled a trio of battery-electric models it was preparing to manufacture in the US. But it began production this summer of battery cells at a $4.4 billion joint venture with LG Energy Solution in Ohio.

That facility was originally designed to build batteries for electric vehicles but has pivoted to make hybrid batteries, [Honda U.S. executive vice president Bob] Nelson said.

Honda has vehicle assembly operations in Ohio, Alabama and Indiana as well as in Canada and Mexico. In addition, it makes key parts such as engines and transmissions in Alabama, Georgia and Ohio. The company said on Monday that it will also make investments in a number of other facilities in North America, but not at its Mexican plant.

Despite the doom and gloom of this year's overall financials, the automaker itself is having a fairly solid year. Honda's U.S. sales have actually risen 5% in the first 10 months of 2026, thanks to strong demand for hybrid versions of the CR-V, Accord, and Civic. Clearly, Honda is following the money, and right now the money is in hybrids. As it stands, hybrids make up about a third of the company's total sales volume in the U.S., and that's while the lineup is missing hybrids in key players like the Pilot and Passport — not to mention, the entire Acura brand.

Last year, I visited Japan and got a look at Honda's next-generation V6 hybrid powertrain, which is meant for larger vehicles. Don't be shocked if it ends up in the MDX and aforementioned Pilot sooner rather than later. There's also news that Acura is working on a Passport-based off-road eight-passenger hybrid SUV, and that's sure to be something rather neat.

The U.S. National Highway Traffic Safety Administration said it had opened a recall inquiry into Alphabet's Waymo because the damn things keep passing stopped school buses. You and I, people who passed driver's ed, know you're not supposed to do that. But these robogoofuses somehow can't get that through their thick, electronic skulls. I mean, it's something we've reported on numerous times before.

The probe will look into nearly 3,000 Waymos with the goal of determining whether a software update that Waymo rolled out last year failed to stop the robotaxis from passing stopped school buses with flashing red lights. Odds are that it didn't, as most of the violations allegedly happened after the company said the remedy had been deployed. Oopsy. From Reuters:

In October 2025, NHTSA opened a preliminary evaluation into the issue of ​Waymo vehicles illegally passing stopped school buses. The agency closed that investigation on Monday ​in favor of the new recall query to determine the cause of the additional violations.

In Austin, Texas, the independent school district documented 19 distinct violations and issued numerous $300 tickets to ​Waymo. Six other violations were recorded by public schools in Atlanta.

NHTSA said it ​was reviewing the software update implemented by Waymo under a December 2025 recall. The auto safety regulator said ‌most ⁠of the incidents occurred after November 2025, when Waymo said it had completed the software remedy for all affected vehicles.

NHTSA's query is meant to assess how well Waymo's remedy actually worked and then determine the causes of the post-remedy incidents. Just to add to all the fun, the NTSB is also investigating.

The long saga is finally over. Stellantis seems to have a deal to sell its Brampton Assembly plant in Canada to none other than armored-car maker Roshel. The transaction, if it closes at the end of the year, would bring an end to vehicle manufacturing at the Ontario factory, which has been a flashpoint in the U.S.-Canada trade war. It's also likely to trigger all sorts of labor tensions among the company's unionized employees. Unifor said Stellantis' Brampton plans prompted an impasse in talks on a new labor deal, and they called on Prime Minister Mark Carney to block the sale. From The Wall Street Journal:

Stellantis' turnaround plan laid out earlier this year calls for using a higher share of its North American factory space by 2030. The automaker has too much factory space relative to its North American sales, said Sam Fiorani, vice president of consulting firm AutoForecast Solutions. "Taking Brampton offline removes some idle capacity," Fiorani said.

The Brampton factory was built four decades ago, and has floor space of nearly three million square feet, or about five football fields.

Roman Shimonov, the founder and CEO of Roshel, said he wants to build a top-tier manufacturing operation in Brampton, adding the transaction promises to drive job creation in Canadian manufacturing. Shimonov had previously said that Roshel was ready to bring affected Stellantis workers on board as part of the new production facility.

Unifor, unsurprisingly, is not thrilled with the news.

The reassurances didn't sit well with Lana Payne, president of Unifor. She said Stellantis "betrayed" the company's 9,000 unionized workers "with an underhanded deal to sell the plant and walk away from its commitments."

Stellantis said in September it was contemplating a sale of the Brampton factory to Roshel, as a return to auto production was deemed financially unfeasible.

The Brampton plant has remained idle since 2024 due to retooling, and Stellantis had plans to manufacture the next-generation Jeep Compass at the Canadian factory. Those plans changed after President Trump imposed hefty tariffs on foreign-made vehicles.

Instead, Stellantis said it would shift production of the Jeep Compass to Illinois, citing trade and market conditions. That move angered Canadian officials, given that federal and regional governments provided over $700 million in taxpayer-backed financing to retool the Brampton factory, as well as a plant in Windsor, Ontario.

Canadian officials have, on several occasions, threatened to sue Stellantis to recoup the money.

In a statement, Industry Minister Mélanie Joly said officials would focus on helping the 2,000 impacted workers in Brampton, and she urged the union, the automaker and regional authorities to meet this week to come up with some sort of a solution. However, her statement doesn't make any mention of pursuing legal action against the automaker.

Last month, Stellantis workers voted to formally give Unifor the mandate to pursue a strike in Canada amid stalled talks.

Federal safety regulators are probing 1.2 million 2021-2026 Ford Explorers after several drivers allegedly suffered burns after their side airbags deployed.

The National Highway Traffic Safety Administration says the investigation centers on complaints that the deployment of seat-mounted side airbags was causing serious burns. So far, it has received eight Vehicle Owner Questionnaires and four Early Warning Reporting "Death and Injury" reports related to the issue. From the Detroit Free Press:

"Consumers have described that during a crash involving deployment of the seat-mounted side airbag, they sustained burn injuries," NHTSA's report read. "Burn injuries have been reported for both drivers and front passengers and were typically diagnosed as second or third-degree burns requiring medical treatment."

The NHTSA document went on to say that some of the Vehicle Owner Questionnaires report injuries were to law enforcement officers following side airbag deployments in Ford Explorer Police Interceptors. According to a recent report by CNBC, more than half of the 12,000 U.S. police departments drive Ford vehicles. It said Ford claims it has two-thirds of the police market with the General Motors and Stellantis supplying the balance.

A spokesman for Ford Motor told the Detroit Free Press on Nov. 9 that the company might have a comment on NHTSA's announced preliminary evaluation later. Ford makes the Explorer at its Chicago Assembly Plant in Illinois.

I can't exactly say something like this is shocking. I mean, so far this year, Ford has issued 85 recalls, and second-place Stellantis is only at 41. It has literally lapped the field, and if this inquiry turns into a recall, it'll only add to that total.

It has been 51 years since the SS Edmund Fitzgerald sank, and not a day has gone by that someone from Detroit hasn't brought it up. If it wasn't for those damn early November gales, she'd still be sailing the Great Lakes today, I'll tell you that much. In any case, if you want to learn more about its doomed journey in early November, head over to History.com.

I've got some beautiful, breathtaking, wonderful, amazing, lovely news. The average price of a gallon of regular gas has fallen below $4 for the first time March 30. I could just about cry. Helping matters along are the fact that both WTI Crude Oil futures and Brent Crude prices are retreating — sitting at $85 and $91, respectively, at the time of publication.

Here's where national average prices stand right now, according to AAA:

This good news means the average price of a gallon of gas dropped another 3 cents overnight to $3.98, according to AAA. Diesel saw its own 2-cent drop, and it's now at $6.16. Fingers crossed this trend holds, but you never really know, do you?

Since I've already covered my Edmund Fitzgerald thoughts, I'll just say this: my buddy once decided to sing this song at a karaoke bar in Williamsburg, Brooklyn, full of people as a joke, and no one thought it was funny. No one got it. No one was in on the bit. Make sure you pick your karaoke songs carefully, or the crowd will turn on you.

#5745. Introducing the Waymo Accessibility Network UK: Co-Designing the Future of Autonomous Mobility

Today, we’re launching the Waymo Accessibility Network UK to inform the future of autonomous mobility in the UK and enable a more independent future of transport. Whether it’s travelling to work, visiting friends, attending an appointment or simply enjoying everything a place has to offer, everyone deserves the freedom to travel with confidence and independence. Getting around should be about possibility, not barriers, and fully autonomous driving technology can provide more people with independent means of travel.

“Establishing the Waymo Accessibility Network UK is a vital step in ensuring our rollout of autonomous technology in London is inclusive, equitable, and aligned with local needs,” says Chris Ludwick, Waymo Product Management Director. “Working hand-in-hand with our UK partners allows us to refine, test, and deploy solutions that give passengers the freedom of truly independent travel.”

Waymo’s Accessibility Network provides a forum for leading British disability advocates and charities to engage with our team in a consistent, two-way dialogue, ensuring that diverse voices within the UK’s disability community actively shape how our technology is deployed on UK streets. Waymo’s Accessibility Network UK is an extension of our longstanding commitment to inclusive mobility and how we continuously improve our service based on feedback from the disability community. That feedback lives within our product today, helping inform features like audio cues and vehicle-finding tools.

Robin Spinks, Head of Inclusive Design at the Royal National Institute of Blind People (RNIB) says: “RNIB warmly welcomes the Waymo Accessibility Network, and we look forward to contributing alongside the blind and partially sighted community.”

London is a global leader in transport. But as we have been hearing from UK accessibility groups over many years, getting around can still present daily challenges, in particular for disabled and older passengers. For sight impaired Londoners commuting at peak times, finding the right vehicle on a busy street can be difficult. Guide dog users still face refusals in private hire vehicles. For older passengers and people with different physical or cognitive needs, even seemingly small aspects of a journey can make a significant difference. As we shared in our Vision for London, opening travel to all matters – and autonomous driving technology offers the opportunity to rethink some of these experiences.

The Waymo Accessibility Network will work directly with member organisations to support user research and participate in product testing. The feedback from members helps inform the passenger experience of our fleet and build upon Waymo’s current accessible design features, including:

We are honoured to collaborate with an exceptional group of national and community-based organisations that champion inclusion across physical, sensory, and cognitive disabilities. We welcome our 10 inaugural participants: RNIB, Re-Engage, AgeUK, Transport for All, Thomas Pocklington Trust, Guide Dogs UK, Sense, Motability Foundation, National Centre for Accessible Transport and Scope.

“Autonomous technology offers enormous potential to transform independent travel, but it must be shaped by disabled people from day one. By joining the Waymo Accessibility Network UK, NCAT hopes to ensure that disability-centred design sits right at the heart of the UK’s autonomous vehicle future.” says Clive Gilbert, Consortium Board Member at the National Centre for Accessible Transport and Head of Accessible Transport at Policy Connect.

“Thomas Pocklington Trust is delighted to be joining the Accessibility Network, along with other disability stakeholders. It is important that the voices of blind and partially sighted people are heard and that travelling in a Waymo can become a reality as the autonomous vehicle improves its accessibility, safety and other features. We want to build confidence for blind and partially sighted people, with independent travel on all modes of transport,” says Bhavini Makwana, Policy and Campaigns Lead at the Thomas Pocklington Trust.

Technology alone doesn’t remove every barrier. True accessibility depends on the streets we share, the vehicles we ride in, and the passengers we support. While Waymo can offer a major leap forward in independent mobility, we’ll always have more to learn. Bringing Waymo to the UK means doing so in partnership. As we work towards welcoming our first passengers in London, our goal is that Waymo becomes a trusted and valuable addition to the UK’s existing and iconic transport networks, and provides a new mobility option. Through Waymo’s Accessibility Network UK, we are ensuring that the people who experience barriers to mobility have a meaningful voice in shaping its future. We look forward to welcoming more UK organisations to the network as we work together to build safer, more inclusive streets for everyone.

#5744. Toyota Revives The Scion Brand In The U.S. With A Right-Hand-Drive Kei Truck

Toyota announced a new sub-brand called Scion by Toyota on Tuesday, which will first start by selling a genuine kei truck, the 11-foot-long KEI-01, in the United States in the second half of 2027. The truck is sold as the Daihatsu Hijet and Toyota Pixis in other markets, and the U.S.-spec KEI-01 will be right-hand-drive and powered by a 660cc three-cylinder engine that sends its power through a continuously variable transmission to all four wheels. Though Toyota didn't announce official power figures, the 660cc three-cylinder produces about 50 horsepower in other applications. Regardless of how much power it makes, the KEI-01 will be limited to a top speed of 25 mph in order to comply with low-speed vehicle (LSV) regulations.

At the same event where it showed media the KEI-01, Toyota also brought out its Scion SxS Concept, a gasoline-electric hybrid side-by-side that first debuted at the SEMA show in 2025, and the BKE Concept, a collapsible electric micromobility bike. Only the KEI-01 is officially headed to production in the second half of next year, and it will be built in Toyota's Indiana manufacturing facility.

Toyota says the KEI-01 will feature standard selectable four-wheel drive with an auto mode and a push-button lock up for tackling more treacherous terrain. Its tiny truck bed is enclosed by three-way fold-down cargo walls to make loading and unloading easier, and the extended cabin design allows for sliding and reclining seats. It has a payload capacity of 600 pounds in the bed, and structural rigidity for towing small trailers and equipment.

The KEI-01 is intended for "select use cases," according to Toyota, and will initially only be sold to fleet customers through a network of participating Scion By Toyota dealers. A Toyota spokesperson said the company may consider retail sales of the KEI-01 at a later date, but for now they envision it as a solution for small business owners, ranchers, and operators where space and efficiency are at a premium.

Scion by Toyota is launching what's called a "freedom lab," which it says is all about exploring mobility possibilities. The hybrid SXx and electric BKE are two examples of that, with the SxS serving as high-speed, high-adrenaline off-road fun machine, and the BKE being a "concept designed to expand the possibilities of how people move through and experience the outdoors." It reads as Toyota's answer to the Honda Motocompacto, which we are quite fond of, and Toyota says it folds up small enough to fit into the trunk of a GR86. Both the SxS and BKE are strictly concept vehicles, so Toyota has not announced formal plans to produce either vehicle just yet.

#5743. New Medicare Payment Model for Back Pain, Heart Failure Riles Docs

— Physicians will face payment cuts for subpar performance in treating those conditions

Physician groups are seeking a delay for a new Medicare demonstration model debuting January 1 that mandates doctors who treat low back pain and congestive heart failure (CHF) patients meet performance standards or face payment cuts.

The Ambulatory Specialty Model (ASM) will affect doctors who treat as few as 20 patients with back pain or 20 patients with heart failure. Those physicians will automatically be placed in the model, which will apply to specialists in any of 235 geographic areas nationwide that have been selected for the 5-year demonstration program.

According to the Centers for Medicare & Medicaid Services (CMS), the model is aimed at addressing the fragmented care that chronic disease patients often receive. "ASM will promote preventive care and more effective upstream chronic disease management by rewarding ASM participants ... for improving patient health outcomes and coordinating with primary care providers," the agency said on its website. "Successful outcomes include reducing avoidable hospitalizations and unnecessary procedures, improving patient experience, and lowering costs to Original Medicare."

Under the model, "ASM participants will receive a positive, neutral, or negative payment adjustment on future Medicare Part B claims for covered professional services," which includes any service covered by Medicare for any Medicare patient -- not just patients with low back pain or CHF -- according to CMS, with payment adjustments ranging from -9% to +9% in the first 2 ASM years, increasing to -12% to +12% by the final year.

An individual doctor's payment increase or decrease would depend on how well that doctor performed on a set of outcome measures compared with their peers, rather than being measured against a pre-set standard benchmark -- an approach known as a tournament model.

Physician groups have several concerns. "As proposed, the model guarantees that most participating physicians will see their payments cut no matter how well they perform on the measures," John Whyte, MD, MPH, CEO and executive vice president of the American Medical Association (AMA), wrote in a comment letter to CMS when the model was proposed last year.

Instead of achieving Medicare savings by reducing physician payment rates, the AMA urges CMS to help physicians in reducing avoidable Medicare spending on hospitalizations and other services, the letter stated. The AMA also suggested the model should be voluntary and that "CMS should also set a performance standard in advance instead of using a 'tournament' approach."

Physician practices that have already begun preparing for model implementation are running into problems, according to a joint letter sent on Oct. 27 to CMS Deputy Administrator Abe Sutton, JD, and signed by the AMA as well as the American Hospital Association, the American Medical Group Association, the Medical Group Management Association, and the National Association of Accountable Care Organizations, among others.

"These delays and setbacks include, but are not limited to: delayed receipt of the final participant list; delayed access to the ASM portal; challenges with logging into, accessing, or delegating authority within the portal; and receiving timely help and guidance from ASM subject matter experts," the letter said. In addition, the final ASM participant list wasn't published until Sept. 15, about 2 months later than CMS originally said it would be, leaving participants only 3 months to prepare.

Due to these and other difficulties, "the undersigned strongly urge [CMS] to delay ASM's launch until all operational and technical infrastructure is available, tested, and ready for the model to start," the letter said. "If [the agency] maintains the January 1, 2027, start date, participants should not be subject to downside financial risk for the initial performance period while operational infrastructure stabilizes."

That immediate downside risk is new for a CMS demonstration model, according to Jennifer Hananoki, JD, senior counsel at the Holland & Knight law firm in Washington, D.C.

"The risk there is no on-ramp here," Hananoki said in an online interview. "A lot of times, when CMMI [the Center for Medicare & Medicaid Innovation] is putting in place new models, especially models in areas where there hasn't been an alternative payment model in the past, they'll have a ramp-up period --  a period of upside-only risk" where practices get paid more for doing well but don't suffer penalties if they perform worse than average. "But that doesn't exist here. The risk [of penalties] really starts almost immediately."

The lack of a firm benchmark is also a problem, she added. "They need to see the benchmark in advance. They need to see how they would have performed in the past in order to understand -- 'Hey, for some reason my costs are below the median or above the median, and I need to figure out why because I need to continue that trend forward, or I need to make adjustments.' And so, there's concern that some of these really fundamental aspects of what makes for successful alternative payment models, all those ingredients might not be there yet."

Another way the ASM isn't like other Medicare physician payment models -- such as the Merit-Based Incentive Payment System, or MIPS -- is that it is meant to be money-saving rather than budget neutral, said Harold Miller, president and CEO of the Center for Healthcare Quality and Payment Reform in Pittsburgh, and the author of a report on the ASM.

"This is not budget neutral," he said in a phone interview, explaining that CMS is taking a 15% cut of the money saved with physician penalties, instead of putting all of that money into bonuses for higher-performing physicians, as MIPS does. Basically, CMS is saying, "'For every $100 we'll take away, [physicians] will get $85 back,'" he said.

There's also a concern that more doctors could be added to the participant list over time, said Jeffrey Davis, a senior policy advisor at Holland & Knight, in an online interview. "They did release a participant list the first year with 5,500 folks," said Davis. "But they could add more depending on if they want to expand the geographical regions or even, you know, [add] the new physicians that come into that region."

Rather than delay the program, CMS should abandon it entirely, Miller suggested. "It doesn't solve a problem. If we're stuck with it, don't make it mandatory," he said, "and have a predefined performance score. That would make this a less bad program."

#5741. 2027 Honda CBR1000RR-R Fireblade Has 215 Horsepower And A Titanium Tank

As motorcycle wings get ever bigger and more ostentatious, one bikemaker is bucking the trend. Honda revealed the latest version of its flagship CBR100RR-R literbike at EICMA this year in three trims, two of which lack front wings entirely. That makes it the sleekest looking top-tier sportbike on the market, and it looks to have the performance to match.

Honda says the Fireblade is made up of 75% new parts for 2027, despite making the same 215 horsepower (though the company claims there's more midrange power available than the prior bike). It's sharper looking than the outgoing model, with a more pointed front end and sleeker headlights, and the lower cowl opening has been widened for even further airflow. The coolest upgrades lie beneath all that shiny new plastic: A titanium fuel tank, a bigger air intake, and a chassis that's designed to be 20% less rigid than the outgoing model. Yes, you read that right — Honda made a bike more wobbly in pursuit of speed.

When we think about rigidity in cars, we're talking about preventing the chassis from twisting, something Honda still doesn't want the new Fireblade to do. When a motorcycle is on its side, leaned over through a corner, a bit of flex in the chassis is actually desirable for eating up bumps in the road without upsetting the tires. That's called lateral rigidity as opposed to the torsional rigidity of a chassis twisting, and Honda claims to have lowered the former by 20% on this new bike without affecting the latter. The swingarm has been updated too, losing 12% of its lateral rigidity.

In concert with that new flex, Honda has updated the Showa suspension on the base Fireblade and the electronically-controlled Ohlins on the Fireblade SP. All variants of the bike also get the new titanium fuel tank, though only the SP gets a bit of exposed titanium on the back to probe that's really what the bike's made from. Honda also added a new HRC Works version of the bike, which adds to the SP's pedigree by mounting front aero and removing the electronic suspension in favor of lighter manual components. The company claims the HRC Works bike is a true homologation special for World Superbike, at least when equipped with the warranty-voiding HRC Racing Kit that includes new fuel injection and iginiton systems, as well as a race-oriented shift pattern and updated pivot points.

Of course, given Honda's track record of offering sportbikes in the U.S., it's unclear when exactly the new Fireblade will hit our shores. It's already on European sites as a 2027 model, but our 2027 bike is already confirmed to be a carryover. We'll have to wait, like always, for the latest and greatest. Still, if we can get this bike at the specs Honda's promising, it'll be well worth the wait.

#5740. CAR-T Therapy: Mixed Results in Rheumatoid Arthritis Trial

— Six patients treated: three show strong responses, three not so much

ORLANDO -- Rheumatoid arthritis (RA) may not be as amenable to chimeric antigen receptor (CAR) T-cell therapy as has been the case in lupus and certain other autoimmune conditions, research presented here suggested.

Among six patients with very refractory RA treated in the first part of a phase I/II trial called COMPARE, three achieved low disease activity within 6 months after a single infusion of CAR T cells, and no severe adverse reactions were seen, according to David Simon, MD, of Charité University Hospital in Berlin, speaking at the American College of Rheumatology annual meeting.

That was the good news. The not-so-good was that the other three did not achieve low disease activity by standard criteria, and one of those who did experienced a disease flare during the final months of the yearlong study.

This was a more "heterogeneous" response pattern than has been seen with CAR-T therapies in lupus, Simon said. It may not be all that surprising, however, because RA itself is heterogeneous (not all patients develop autoantibodies, for example) and the role of B cells -- CAR-T therapy's main target -- is not as prominent as in lupus and some other autoimmune disorders.

One reason for trying this approach has been that the anti-B-cell drug rituximab (Rituxan) has been helpful in difficult RA cases. That suggests that, at least for some patients, B cells do help drive RA and that the "immune reset" concept with CAR-T treatment, which aims to wipe out B cells' autoimmune memory, could work effectively in RA.

Indeed, these initial results indicate that it's a realistic proposition -- it's just that the pathology differs enough between patients that the efficacy will be uneven.

The phase I portion of COMPARE recruited patients with heavily pretreated RA. Simon said the six who were enrolled had previously tried a mean of 6.5 targeted therapies, in addition to conventional drugs such as methotrexate, without achieving adequate disease control. Disease Activity Score values across 28 joints (DAS28) ranged from 4.6 to 6.8 among the six patients, who were in their early 30s to late 60s. Disease duration spanned 4.5 to 24 years. Three were women and three were men.

As a phase I study, safety was a top priority to examine. Simon said all patients experienced cytokine release syndrome, which is nearly universal with CAR T-cell therapy in oncologic as well as rheumatologic use. None, however, developed more severe reactions such as immune effector cell-associated neurotoxicity syndrome (ICANS) or a condition called LICATS (local immune effector cell-associated toxicity syndrome) that resembles ICANS but affects other organ systems besides the nervous system. As well, no infections more severe than grade 2 occurred; the only adverse effect rated as serious was elevated liver enzymes in one patient.

All patients showed deep depletion of B cells, as expected, almost immediately after CAR T-cell infusions. But other effects were more variable. Four of the six patients showed abolition of autoantibodies, and as noted above, only three achieved DAS28 scores below 2.8, the threshold for low disease activity; none got to zero, whereas full remission has been common with CAR-T therapy in lupus.

Simon spent several minutes discussing the patient who flared. This was a 42-year-old woman with a DAS28 score of 5.5 at baseline. Crucially, and unlike the other five patients, analysis of CAR T-cell counts in circulation showed very poor expansion in the first weeks after the infusion -- short of 100 cells per μL at the maximum, compared with approximately 150-400 cells per μL in the others. B-cell reconstitution was already well underway at week 8, whereas it was week 16 for the next earliest, and still later for others. Analysis of reconstituted B cells indicated that autoreactivity had not been abolished, thus paving the way for relapse that began sometime after week 36.

"Whether the observed immune remodeling translates into durable drug-free disease control remains uncertain," Simon concluded. "Follow-up is limited to approximately 1 year, and re-emergence of autoreactive B-cell populations in one relapsing patient suggests that immune reconstitution may influence long-term outcomes."

Ten more patients will be recruited for COMPARE's phase II portion, which, as the name suggests, will include a control group treated with rituximab.

#5738. America's Oldest Active Turnpike Will Pull Down All Its Toll Booths In 2027

Tossing the coins into a metal bin at a toll booth is as dated as looping the dial on a rotary phone. The Pennsylvania Turnpike announced last week that it will switch to open-road tolling at the start of next year. The transition won't be immediate, as contractors have been tasked with removing physical toll booths over the next two years. Officials tout full implementation of open-road tolling as more convenient for drivers and far safer.

Pennsylvania Turnpike opened back in 1940, stretching 160 miles from Harrisburg across the Appalachian Mountains to Pittsburgh. Extensions during the 1950s took the toll road to the state borders with New Jersey and Ohio on either side of the Keystone State, for a total of 360 miles. The turnpike's design served a blueprint for the Interstate Highway System. Besides the payment receptacles, some of the original toll booths are still in place, according to PennLive.

Implementing open-road tolling on the Pennsylvania Turnpike has been a 16-year process. According to the Pittsburgh Post-Gazette, officials commissioned a feasibility study in 2010, which deemed the payment system impossible because the turnpike lacked a fiber-optic infrastructure. Because of the COVID-19 pandemic, the toll booths went contact-less in 2020 and relied solely on electronic payments. By 2025, the turnpike began transitioning to open-road tolling.

For those familiar with open-road tolling, overhead gantries photograph the front and rear of your vehicle as you drive past. Antennas are also mounted on the gantries to read EZPass transponders. Sensors embedded in the pavement can determine how many axles your vehicle has, which helps distinguish commercial vehicles. There are no toll plazas, so drivers don't have to slow down and wait for a gate arm. On the Pennsylvania Turnpike, tolls are calculated based on how far you travel along the route, so the gantries monitor when you enter and exit.

There is obviously a massive upfront cost to removing the toll booths and installing the gantries. The improvement work in Western Pennsylvania is estimated to cost $134 million. However, the savings will recoup the costs over time. The transition is expected to save the Pennsylvania Turnpike Authority roughly $25 million a year. For those concerned about the turnpike's history, one of the original 1940 toll booths has already been removed and relocated to the State Museum of Pennsylvania as part of an exhibit on the roadway.

Open-road tolling is also safer than traditional toll plazas. It might seem natural to slow down as you approach the toll booth, but your safety depends on all the drivers behind you doing the same. After investigating a fatal 2024 crash on Interstate 80, the National Transportation Safety Board recommended in September that the Ohio Turnpike "permanently close low-speed tolling lanes and implement fully electronic tolling at its toll plazas." The driver of an SUV abruptly braked and swerved right, attempting to leave the high-speed electronic toll lanes for the booth-controlled low-speed lanes. The SUV cut in front of a tractor-trailer, which struck the smaller vehicle from behind. The crash killed two passengers inside the SUV, as well as seriously injuring a third passenger and the drivers of both vehicles.

Toll booths are gradually going extinct. While I do feel some nostalgia for the ritualistic payment process, open-road tolling is becoming more widespread for good reason. The bridges and tunnels around New York City demolished their toll booths in 2022. Florida's Turnpike and the Illinois Tollway ditched their booths in 2021 and 2024, respectively. Many other toll roads across the country are in the midst of their own open-road tolling transition.

#5737. Ford Teases Reborn Cobra R That Picks Up Where The Shelby GT350 Left Off

Folks, the Cobra is back. In a 36-second teaser posted by Ford Racing on X, the everything app, the Blue Oval said an "all-motor heart born from racing" is on the way for the Mustang, and if you read between the lines, "all-motor" means naturally aspirated. The engine audio clip playing in the background certainly lends credibility to that idea. With a 2027 launch date, there aren't any technical details to speak of, but the name "Cobra" gives me a lot of confidence this bad boy is going to sit above the GT and possibly even the Dark Horse.

You'll want to hear this. For the drivers, with an all-motor heart born from racing.  Ford Mustang Cobra, a Legacy Reborn.  The story continues in 2027. pic.twitter.com/fogI982jfl

— Ford Racing (@FordRacing) November 10, 2026

Of course, nothing is official, but leaked dealer information lends some credence to how this new Cobra will be positioned within the Mustang lineup. You can think of it as the "spiritual successor to the Mustang Shelby GT350," according to Ford Authority, and it'll launch next year as a 2028. Get it while you can, though, because it's expected to have a relatively short production run through the 2029 model year. Under the hood will be a naturally aspirated V8, obviously, and it's either going to be 5.2 or 5.4 liters, depending on how much freedom Ford feels like dispersing. Don't worry, it's also expected to have a Tremec six-speed manual transmission.

There's no word on horsepower numbers just yet, but we can look at the data as a guiding light. Currently, the Dark Horse's 5.0-liter V8 pumps out 500 horsepower and 418 pound-feet of torque. The Dark Horse SC takes those numbers all the way up to 795 and 660, respectively. Looking back, the 5.2-liter Voodoo V8 in the GT350 produced 526 hp and 429 lb-ft. If I were a betting man, I'd put my money on the horsepower number being somewhere around the 550-575 mark.

It has been quite a while since the Cobra name appeared on a Mustang from Ford. The last time it popped up was in the early 2000s supercharged SN95 Mustang SVT Cobra, which was a beast for the time. Since then, there have been some Shelby-specific Cobras, but nothing from Ford itself.

You may be asking yourself, "Why did Ford revive the name Cobra rather than just call the Shelby GT350's successor a Shelby GT350?" Well, it's because of money. As we explained earlier this year, Ford has to pay to use the name Shelby on its cars, and it doesn't want to anymore. Shelby America owns the name. It's the same reason the Dark Horse SC has its name rather than Shelby GT500. It hasn't been cheap, either. Ford was paying about $800 in royalties per Shelby Mustang it sold, racking up over $30 million in licensing fees in the process.

At the end of the day, it doesn't really matter what this thing is going to be called. Of course, "Cobra" is sick as hell, but it could be called the Ford Mustang Beige Wallpaper for all I care. It's going to be sick as hell.

#5736. Trump Administration Continues Systematic Dismantling of Public Lands

Proposed Rule Would Shut the Public Out and Allow the Administration to Make Sweeping Decisions About 250 Million Acres of Western Public Lands Behind Closed Doors

Yesterday, the Administration announced a proposed Bureau of Land Management (BLM) rulemaking that would drastically weaken the public’s involvement in land use planning and gut protections for Areas of Critical Environmental Concern (ACECs), one of BLM’s primary conservation designations, across 245 million acres of Western public lands. The rule proposes to stifle the voices of local communities by significantly shortening public comment periods and cutting out robust environmental analysis. These changes would make it easier for the BLM to fast-track the sale, transfer, or use of public lands for oil and gas drilling, mining, data centers, and other industrial or commercial development, all while shutting out the American people. The announcement kicks off a 60 day comment period (January 9th deadline).

The public lands overseen by the BLM contain crucial wildlife corridors, critical habitat, cultural resources and landscapes, watersheds, historic sites, and access to nature and scenery.  The rule threatens to strip vital safeguards for ACECs and environmental review that protect these values, leaving the West’s most sensitive lands vulnerable to irreparable harm.

This aggressive regulatory rollback is the latest chapter in a coordinated, systematic dismantling of America’s public lands. It directly connects to a broader pattern of hostility toward balanced stewardship, which includes the recent rescission of the landmark Public Lands Rule, stated intention to roll back the Roadless Rule and Wilderness policies, the evisceration of National Environmental Policy Act (NEPA) standards, and repeated attempts to give away public lands to corporations and developers. This rulemaking contributes to this administration’s singular focus: fast-tracking industrial exploitation and drilling at the expense of our shared heritage, tribal sovereignty and the outdoor recreation economies that sustain rural Western communities.

The proposal comes as Congress debates permitting legislation that would also weaken public participation in decisions affecting shared lands and give more power to development interests.

Planning serves as the foundational blueprints governing public lands for decades; eroding these processes is a profound threat to the long-term health of the West. Making robust environmental review optional and weakening ACEC standards intentionally ties the agency’s hands, preventing it from effectively responding to the compounding threats of climate change, drought, and habitat fragmentation. This action rejects the unmistakable demand from the American public, including local leaders, Tribal representatives, and local elected officials, who have consistently called for public lands to be managed transparently and protected for future generations.

“The Trump Administration is once again plotting to undermine our world-renowned system of shared lands and the public processes that guide their management,” said Jackie Feinberg, Sierra Club’s National Lands Conservation Manager. “Weakening protections and public participation would fast-track the industrialization and privatization of places essential for wildlife, clean water, cultural and historic preservation, and access to nature. We stand firmly against these attempts to cut out the “public” from public lands for the benefit of corporate polluters and profiteers.”

“Gutting transparency, review, and public engagement requirements within the West’s most ecologically sensitive wildlife corridors, watersheds, and cultural resources are the actions of cowards trying to fast-track industrial development at any and all social costs,” said Kara Matsumoto, senior policy director at the Conservation Lands Foundation. “Resource management plans are the blueprints for keeping public lands and waters healthy, and weakening them will weaken local economies and fragile ecosystems. We’ll continue to fight this corporate greed because the overwhelming majority of Americans want more nature protected, not less.”

“The opportunity to review and comment on land management actions keeps the public in public lands.” said Hilary Eisen, federal policy director at Wild Montana. “Resource management plans provide the long-term framework for how vast swaths of public land across the West are managed and it is essential that these plans be crafted with robust public engagement. This proposed rule sidelines the public, as well as local, state, and tribal governments, in BLM land use planning.”

“Resource management planning is meant to provide the public a real say in how BLM manages our shared public lands, and Areas of Critical Environmental Concern are how the agency turns that input into on-the-ground protections for special places and important wildlife habitat,” said Maddy Munson, senior policy and planning specialist at Defenders of Wildlife. “Cutting back public participation and narrowing the agency’s analysis isn’t streamlining, but rather stacking the deck against the places and species that need protection the most.”

“For Nevadans public land is our way of life. Whether we have jobs dependent on mining, grazing, the outdoor industry or just love taking our families camping, these public lands in Nevada are a huge part of who we are. We want to have an active role in determining how these lands are managed and efforts to take the public out of this planning process and weaken protections are just wrong,” said Shaaron Netherton, Executive Director at Friends of Nevada WIlderness. “We need to stand up and keep the public in our public lands.”

“The Trump administration continues to eliminate tools that the Bureau of Land Management has relied on for decades for conserving wildlife habitat, protecting clean water, and working with local communities. The objective is clear: replace the multiple uses and values of our public lands with the singular use of fossil fuel development. That agenda has no footing in Colorado, where 96% percent of Coloradans support conserving public lands and open space and 81% believe oil and gas development is not an acceptable use of public lands,” said Juli Slivka, Senior Director of Policy and Programs at Wilderness Workshop.

“These are the plans that decide whether a valley gets drilled or a trout stream gets protected, and this proposal is built to make those decisions faster and with fewer people in the room,” said Bobby McEnaney, Senior Lands Analyst at NRDC (Natural Resources Defense Council). “Cutting the public’s time to weigh in by two thirds, scrapping the full environmental review, and letting a political appointee invoke an emergency to skip the rest is not modernization. It is a way to lock in drilling and mining decisions before anyone can object. Governors get 30 days and no appeal, Tribal plans drop out of the review entirely, and the agency gives itself the power to shrink or erase existing protected areas. Interior should withdraw this proposal and keep the public’s seat at the table.”

“These lands are called “public lands” for a reason: They are owned by the people of the United States. The Bureau of Land Management’s job is to manage them in the public’s interest, for the common good. By so severely restricting opportunities for the public—including, importantly, Tribal Nations—to be involved in planning for their management, the BLM is depriving all of us of our rights as the owners,” said Michael Painter, Coordinator, Californians for Western Wilderness.

“The Trump administration continues its relentless agenda of handing over public lands to corporations,” said Thomas Delehanty, senior attorney with Earthjustice’s Rocky Mountain Office. “Cutting the public out of land management benefits no one except the extractive industries hellbent on controlling these shared resources for their own private gain. Anyone who values public lands must be loud and clear: these attacks end now.”

“Public lands belong to all Americans, and decisions about their future should be made through transparent processes informed by science, meaningful public participation and consultation with Tribes and others in the community. This proposed rule would give us less of a say in the management of 245 million acres of our shared lands and weaken areas of critical environmental concern, the BLM’s primary tool for protecting important natural and cultural sites. It is another clear attempt to give drilling, mining and other development interests more influence over our public lands, in the same spirit as rescission of the BLM Public Lands Rule—and only days after voters delivered a stinging rebuke to this administration’s agenda of doing favors for oil and gas companies and other powerful corporations. Our public lands deserve better, and so do future generations that will inherit them.” said Michael Carroll, BLM Campaign Director, The Wilderness Society.

“In New Mexico, the BLM planning process gives communities, Pueblos, Tribes, acequias, recreationalists, conservation organizations, ranchers, hunters and anglers a real say in what happens to the 13.5 million acres of public land that the agency manages here,” said Sally Paez, senior staff attorney for New Mexico Wild. “Places like the Greater Chaco landscape, the Permian Basin’s remaining wildlife habitat, and our desert grasslands have protections today because New Mexicans showed up and spoke out during planning. New Mexicans shouldn’t be shut out of decisions about their own public lands.”

“In Oregon, the BLM manages 2.6 million acres of Oregon and California Lands that are home to imperiled wildlife, endemic botanical resources, wild rivers, and climate change-mitigating ancient forests that provide outstanding recreational opportunities and socioeconomic benefits to Oregonians and all Americans,” said Susan Jane Brown, attorney with Silvix Resources. “The public and other stakeholders must have a central role in the science-informed stewardship of these cherished resources, all of which will be placed at risk of existential loss with BLM’s proposed rulemaking.”

“Oregon’s high desert community has modeled a long, successful history of federal planners, tribal interests, ranchers, hunters and anglers, recreationists, and conservationists working together to craft management strategies that balance land use and promote conservation across 12 million acres of our shared public lands,” said Mark Salvo, Senior Conservation Advisor for Oregon Natural Desert Association. “Now the Trump administration is recklessly dismissing these decades of collaboration and compromise, trampling public processes, and sidelining those who have worked to protect these lands and resources for future generations–all to advance a narrow agenda of exploitation at the expense of the public interest.”

“The public lands are the people’s lands and the American people have spoken loud and clear. The Bureau’s proposal to cut public participation, reduce protections for cultural sites and critically important habitat, and shortcut environmental analysis is just one more indication that the Trump Administration isn’t listening and will do anything to silence the public, Tribes, and scientists and ignore the reality of what’s happening on the ground,” said Josh Osher, public policy director for Western Watersheds Project. “Millions of acres of climate-stressed Bureau-managed lands are already being degraded by extractive industries, and we need planning rules that include thorough and honest science-based assessments and as many voices as possible to help improve ecological conditions and resilience.”

This is just the latest attempt from the Trump Administration to dismantle our public lands piece by piece,” said Sandra Schubert, Executive Director of Tuleyome. “The proposed Planning Rule undermines protections that ensure that our public lands and wild forests remain accessible and are used sustainably. Resource management plans are built on science and centuries of management experience. They give local land managers and communities the ability to maintain healthy ecosystems while supporting the diverse ways communities depend on public lands. Undermining planning undermines our public lands”

“Once again, Doug Burgum is doing everything in his power to cut the public out of public lands,” said Aaron Weiss, executive director of the Center for Western Priorities. “This new rule gives political appointees in DC more power to shrink protected places and ignore local voices. Public lands belong to all of us, and good land use planning means giving everyone the chance to weigh in.”

“America’s national parks are supported by the public lands that surround them, and this proposal is another blatant attempt to unravel decades of conservation progress that the public overwhelmingly supports,” said Beau Kiklis, associate director of energy and landscape conservation at the National Parks Conservation Association. “Roughly 80 national park sites share a border with BLM lands, and Areas of Critical Environmental Concern have helped protect Dinosaur National Monument from impacts of oil and gas development, support Joshua Tree’s wildlife by protecting the neighboring Pinto Mountains, and protect ancestral sites near Hovenweep among countless others. This is another attempt to sell off and sell out our public lands, and the public deserves a real say before more is lost.”

#5735. BYD’s BEV Sales Soar While Its PHEV Sales Slide

Looking at changes in BYD’s passenger vehicle sales overall, the company saw solid growth in October. Sales were up 11.8%, going from 436,856 in October 2025 to 483,125 in October 2026. However, that overall growth hides the fact that the company’s BEV (full electric vehicle) sales soared while its PHEV (plugin hybrid electric vehicle) sales actually declined a bit.

BYD’s BEV sales rose 40.6% year over year in October! They went from 222,559 in October 2025 to 305,743 last month. Month over month, they rose 11.9%, up from 273,143 in September.

Meanwhile, PHEV sales were on the other side of the coin. They dropped 19.6% year over year, going from 214,297 in October 2025 to 177,382 in October 2026. Month over month, they dropped 3.4%, down from 183,570.

Looking at overall passenger vehicle sales once more, BYD’s sales were up 5.8% month over month, rising from 456,713 in September 2026 to 483,125 in October 2026. The charts really help to show all of these changes. Below are static versions of the embedded charts above.

#5734. Australia's Cheapest New Car Is This $13,000 MG Hatchback, And It's Far From A Stripper

Chinese cars have been beating down on long established performance and luxury car brands for a while now, all while costing a fraction of the price. From jumping over potholes to setting top speed records to beating Land Cruisers in off-road tests and even having emergency water travel capabilities, there are some mighty impressive pieces of kit across the Chinese car industry. What I'm most interested in, though, are the ultra-cheap economy cars that sell for the price of a used shitbox, and that's exactly what the MG3 offers to Australian car buyers. It's a compact hatchback with the face of a plecostomus, which isn't especially exciting, but its $13,020 USD price sure is.

It's no stripper, either. That bargain price includes features like Apple CarPlay and Android Auto, steering wheel controls, air conditioning, a digital driver's display, an auto-hold function, automatic power window switches, and adaptive cruise control with lane centering. It even has a standard feature that the new Toyota RAV4 doesn't even offer on its most expensive trim level that happens to be four times the price of the MG3: padded front and rear armrests. OK, so the MG3's actual MSRP in Australia is $13,890.85 USD at current exchange rates, but the fact that a dealer was even willing to discount it further is remarkable, too.

As you can see from CarSauce's review, the MG3 is woefully slow. Its 1.5-liter four-cylinder engine sends just 109 horsepower and 105 pound-feet of torque to the front wheels via a continuously variable transmission, which results in a leisurely 0-to-62 mph time of 12.57 seconds. It ain't quick, but it gets the job done, and again, it's a $13,000 car. The last car sold in the United States that was near the $13,000 mark was the diminutive Chevrolet Spark, which ran the 0-to-60 sprint in 10.8 seconds in Car and Driver's testing, so the MG3 isn't too far off that pace. Plus, looking beyond straight-line speed (or lack thereof), the reviewer even says it rides great and handles respectably, which are not always the case with bargain-basement cars.

Sadly the MG3's weak engine doesn't equate to exceptional fuel efficiency, with the presenter's real-world economy equating to about 36 mpg on the highway and more like 26 mpg around town. Those aren't abysmal numbers, just perhaps not as good as one might hope from such a weak engine in a tiny car.

Beyond the lower-than-low base price, the MG3's value proposition really comes into focus when you take into account its 10-year manufacturer warranty. For those who say, "why spend $13,000 on the cheapest new car available when you can buy a bigger, better used car for the same price?" Consider the idea of spending $13,000 on a car with a 10-year-long warranty versus spending that same amount on a used car with no warranty. The new car comes with an entire decade of mechanical coverage so the buyer doesn't have to worry about unexpected repair costs for those 10 years, whereas the used car could end up requiring costly repairs within the first year of ownership.

There are some quality concerns noted in the video, including infotainment software glitches and some apparent inconsistencies in the operation of those fancy automatic windows, but considering the price and that stellar warranty, I'd say the pros outweigh the cons in this case. For those who view their cars as another appliance, or those who simply want the financial security of not worrying about repair costs for a whole decade, the MG3 must be a pretty tempting option. Provided you live in Australia, of course — or Mexico, Europe, the Middle East, or other parts of the world where it's pretty damn cheap, too.

#5733. Nanobody Shows Promise in Myasthenia Gravis

— PREVAIL trial found rapid improvement with a once-weekly subcutaneous injection

Results from the phase III PREVAIL trial presented at the American Association of Neuromuscular & Electrodiagnostic Medicine annual meeting showed that treatment with the investigational once-weekly nanobody gefurulimab significantly improved symptoms in adults with anti-acetylcholine receptor antibody-positive generalized myasthenia gravis, with a safety profile generally comparable to placebo.

In this exclusive MedPage Today video, James Winkley, MD, of Baptist Health Medical Group in Lexington, Kentucky, discusses the findings, including the potential convenience of self-administered treatment with the subcutaneous injectable and compatibility with intravenous immunoglobulin (IVIG) therapy.

Following is a transcript of his remarks:

The PREVAIL study was a double-blind, placebo-controlled trial with gefurulimab as the treating agent. Gefurulimab is a unique agent in that it's the first nanobody that'll be used in myasthenia gravis, which makes it a smaller molecule, which allows for greater tissue penetration and solubility -- so it can be given as a [subcutaneous] injection rather than the larger monoclonal antibodies, which have to be given in larger volumes underneath the skin or as IV infusion. So this would represent a greater ease of access for patients to have a [subcutaneous], once-a-week injection that doesn't require them to go to the infusion center.

As we had seen in the trial, similar to eculizumab [Bkemv] and ravulizumab [Ultomiris], which are C5 inhibitors, it met its primary outcome of MG-ADL [Myasthenia Gravis Activities of Daily Living] reduction, with a minus 4.2 reduction versus 2.1 on placebo, which was statistically significant. We did see a quick onset of action within 1 week, with a statistically significant reduction in the MG-ADL for those patients, as well as 4 weeks for the [Quantitative Myasthenia Gravis].

There were similar side effects between the treatment group and the placebo group, with injection-site reactions being slightly higher in the treatment group, but all of those reactions tended to be mild to moderate. The most common adverse events were headaches, upper respiratory infections, and muscle aches and pains. One thing that was significant at any time we're using complement inhibitors, there were no meningococcal infections. Everybody made sure they were fully vaccinated, and if they'd had to be started prior to 2 weeks, they received antibiotics. But we had a very typical safety profile, as I said, between the placebo arm and the treatment arm -- the side effects were similar.

This is going to make for ease of access for patients. You still have other molecules out on the market. You've got a once-daily complement inhibitor and you've got now a once-every-2-weeks IV infusion versus every 8 weeks. So kind of depending on what the patient prefers, this gives them greater access. We've got an easily portable complement inhibitor that patients no longer are tethered to an infusion center that they have to worry about. When's our next infusion? Do I have access? And I think that's going to be a huge advantage for the patients and their quality of life.

I would say that one thing that's unique about [gefurulimab] is that it is compatible with IVIG. And so the other larger monoclonal antibodies like the FcRn [neonatal Fc receptor] and the larger eculizumab and ravulizumab, it gets recycled by a different mechanism because it goes through the albumin on the FcRn receptor. It was combined with IVIG during the trial, patients could be on stable dosages of it, so it has that advantage of being able to be compared and not have to give supplemental dosing.

So I think that it will have combination with other available medications. I think that's a big advantage for it.

#5731. CMS makes it official, codifying Medicare drug price negotiations established under IRA

CMS makes it official, codifying Medicare drug price negotiations established under IRA" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Against a backdrop of repeated industry challenges in court, the Centers for Medicare and Medicaid Services (CMS) is locking in the drug price negotiation framework established under the Biden-era Inflation Reduction Act (IRA).

In turn, the process under which CMS selects certain high-spend Medicare Part B or Part D drugs and negotiates lower prices with manufacturers will carry on into 2029 and beyond. The rule codifies and expands policies previously established through guidance for the initial 2026-28 period.

The final rule is slated to go into effect Dec. 5, CMS said in a fact sheet accompanying Thursday’s announcement. By Feb. 1 of next year, CMS will select up to 20 drugs covered under Medicare Part B or Part D for price negotiations taking effect in 2029, with the potential to also select medicines included in previous rounds for renegotiation.

That upcoming group of drugs will make up the fourth cycle of pricing negotiations, with negotiated prices taking effect in 2029. CMS asserted that the rule gives both patients and drugmakers “a clear path forward as the agency builds on the program’s results and continues negotiating prices for high-cost drugs.”

The initial rounds of price negotiations have already included a slew of prominent brands, including Novo's semaglutide franchise of Wegovy and Ozempic and cancer meds like Bristol Myers Squibb’s Pomalyst, Pfizer’s Ibrance and AstraZeneca’s Calquence.

Meanwhile, the latest tranche of drugs selected earlier this year for negotiation, whose new prices would go into effect in 2028, includes notable entrants like Gilead’s HIV treatment Biktarvy, AbbVie’s Botox and Eli Lilly’s diabetes drug Trulicity, to name just a few.

Beyond codifying the framework for price negotiations in 2029 and subsequent years, CMS has also taken steps to fine-tune several elements of the program.

Namely, the rule finalizes a policy on how CMS classifies certain fixed-combination drugs, with the aim of addressing “potential program integrity concerns” that could encourage drugmakers to evade negotiated prices by steering patients toward new product formulations.

The rule also includes a provision establishing a temporary “small biotech floor” that prevents CMS from offering or accepting a counteroffer below a specified price floor for certain eligible small biotech drugs in 2029 and 2030. It also codifies two policies related to Medicare Part D intended to ensure plan coverage of medicines with a maximum fair price in effect and limit the prices plans pay pharmacies and other dispensing entities.

The IRA and the Medicare price negotiations it empowered have been frequent targets of the biopharma industry, which has broadly alleged that the policy is unconstitutional and that the pricing talks aren’t as voluntary as advertised. But attempts by major drugmakers and powerful trade organizations like the Pharmaceutical Research and Manufacturers of America (PhRMA) to push back on Medicare price negotiations have repeatedly stalled out.

In the interim, the U.S. government has made a separate and more recent push to rein in high prescription drug costs by leaning on President Donald Trump’s “most favored nation” (MFN) strategy.

“President Trump directed us to lower drug costs for Americans using every tool at our disposal,” CMS Administrator Mehmet Oz, M.D., said in a statement announcing the new rule. “This rule provides additional tools to help CMS negotiate better deals, achieve greater savings for taxpayers and beneficiaries, and improve health outcomes for seniors who rely on the Medicare Prescription Drug Program.”

MFN, for its part, has yielded at times, controversial deals with more than two dozen drugmakers, generally involving commitments to price new medicines in line with prices in high-income comparator countries and offer MFN pricing on certain existing drugs through Medicaid.

Through those deals, which have also included investment commitments in the U.S., drugmakers have been able to secure temporary exemptions from the Trump administration’s threatened pharmaceutical import tariffs.

Around this week’s midterm elections, several Big Pharma executives have signaled that they expect little immediate change to their thinking around MFN. Nevertheless, the lack of transparency surrounding the deals has already drawn scrutiny from Democratic lawmakers. With Democrats set to control both chambers of Congress beginning in January, the party may soon press harder to uncover the details of those agreements.
