#5730. Tesla 'Full Self-Driving' One Step Closer To Recreating Human Driving By Overtaking Bicycles, Exceeding Speed Limits And Misidentifying Road Signs

Humans are imperfect, so at least in theory, robotaxis are supposed to save us by doing all the perfect driving we aren't able to do. Of course, had we poured every dollar that's been spent on robotaxi development into improving public transportation, self-driving cars would feel unnecessary. But the bigger problem is, after all those billions of dollars and years of development, Momentum Mag reports that a recent test of Tesla's so-called "Full Self-Driving" software found that it loved to speed through neighborhoods, illegally pass cyclists, and break other traffic laws. So basically, it drives like your least-favorite neighbor.

Woke 3 already killed the Cybertruck, so Tesla's school bus-passing software could be next on the chopping block after liberals figure out how to make every man a little shorter every time he disappoints a woman. Until then, it's important to know how good or bad the software controlling some of the most popular new cars actually is, which is why Belgian mobility organization johanna.be put the software to the test and found it frequently breaks speed limit laws in areas where drivers are most likely to hit pedestrians or cyclists. So that's not great.

Additionally, while there aren't many places in the U.S. where drivers can't legally pass a cyclist, that isn't the case in Belgium. You'd think fine-tuning the software to account for changes in laws between countries would have been a pretty basic part of Tesla's development, but nope. In johanna.be's testing, it found that Tesla's FSD system "repeatedly tries to overtake cyclists in cycle streets, something that is explicitly illegal in Belgium."

Coincidentally, right around the time johanna.be published the results of its Tesla safety test, the European Transport Safety Council shared the results of a separate safety test. The ETSC found that Tesla's "speed offset" and "contextual max speed" features don't follow United Nations or European Union rules regarding safety, and it's called on member states to reject both features later this year when the EU plans to hold an important vote on advanced driver assistance system regulation.

The ECF is also asking the EU to create a European Road Safety Agency that would act as "an independent EU body able to investigate issues involving automated systems and share the subsequent findings with other Member States." Even before the ETSC got involved, johanna.be's findings were concerning enough to warrant some serious attention from international regulators that Elon Musk wasn't able to gut. As the ECF put it, Tesla's so-called "Full Self-Driving" software (emphasis is the ECF's):

If a human loved to drive at 150% of the posted speed limit, regularly told people the speed limit was higher than it was, and kept trying to make illegal passes, we'd take their license and tell them to take the bus. With robotaxis and cars with driver-assist systems, it should be the same. Until they can prove they actually follow the rules of the road, there's no reason to let them on our roads. We also shouldn't allow automakers to sell driver-assistance systems that haven't been proven safe, either. Even if you disagree, I don't think you need a PhD in traffic safety to understand the argument there.

That said, it's entirely possible that safety researchers are criticizing Tesla for mistakes that every automaker's software makes. The problem with that argument is that johanna.be ran the same test with a Citroën C3 that managed to correctly identify all seven speed limit signs. For comparison, the Tesla got six of the seven speed limits wrong. It only got one correct. Imagine not only being wrong, but being more wrong than the French. Self-immolation is probably the only option.

While we wait to see how the EU's member states vote, the ECF also noted that otherwise docile behavior is no excuse for Tesla's software behaving badly in other ways. As it noted:

the study also found that FSD behaved cautiously and courteously around pedestrians and cyclists in general, yielding early, giving space, and even slowing for a sleepy cat in the road! However, this should not compensate for a pattern of speeding and illegal overtaking in streets that are primarily designed for safe walking and cycling

#5729. Another Drug Shows Potential for Functional Cures in Chronic Hepatitis B

— With highest dose of siRNA therapy, 19% achieved a functional cure 24 weeks after treatment ended

Reductions in hepatitis B surface antigen (HBsAg) were observed with a novel small interfering RNA (siRNA) therapy in patients with chronic hepatitis B virus (HBV) infection, according to a phase II study.

Among patients randomized to monotherapy with one of four doses of the siRNA HRS-5635, all 32 on the highest dose (400 mg) had HBsAg levels less than 100 IU/mL after 48 weeks of treatment, with 88% still at that level 24 weeks after treatment ended.

Among this high-dose group, 16% achieved a functional cure, with an HBsAg clearance level of less than 0.05 IU/mL after 48 weeks -- a percentage that rose to 19% by 24 weeks post-treatment, reported Xieer Liang, PhD, of Southern Medical University in Guangzhou, China, at the American Association for the Study of Liver Diseases annual meeting in Denver.

"A dose-dependent antiviral response was observed with HRS-5635," Liang said. "All HRS-5635 regimens were generally safe and well-tolerated."

An estimated 240 million people worldwide have chronic HBV infection, including about 640,000 U.S. adults. Chronic HBV can cause liver damage, cirrhosis, liver cancer, and death. Five antivirals and two immune modulators are approved in the U.S. to treat chronic HBV, with treatment regimens lasting from 6 months to over 1 year.

In a previous phase II trial, siRNA therapy with an immunomodulator reduced HBsAg in patients with chronic HBV infection. Last month, the FDA approved another nucleic acid-based therapeutic, the antisense oligonucleotide drug bepirovirsen (Hibsago), for patients with chronic HBV taking antiviral therapy based on results from the B-Well 1 and B-Well 2 studies. In those trials, 19% to 20% of patients with HBsAg levels between 100 and 3,000 IU/mL had a functional cure with bepirovirsen at week 72.

The current trial included patients with chronic HBV on stable nucleos(t)ide analogue therapy who had HBsAg levels over 100 and less than 3,000 IU/mL and HBV DNA levels less than 20 IU/mL. Patients could be hepatitis B envelope antigen (HBeAg) positive or negative, but they couldn't have cirrhosis. All patients received tenofovir amibufenamide during 48 weeks of HRS-5635 treatment.

Mean age ranged from 41.5 to 44.6 years in the four treatment groups, 59.4% to 76.7% were men, and mean HBsAg ranged from 1,058.2 IU/mL to 1,200.4 IU/mL. Most patients were HBeAg negative (65.6% to 73.3%).

The investigators defined functional cure as sustained HBV DNA suppression and HBsAg clearance of less than 0.05 IU/mL, with or without seroconversion, sustained for 24 weeks after treatment.

All four HRS-5635 doses achieved the study's primary endpoint of HBsAg reduction from baseline to week 12, with mean reductions of -1.26 log10 IU/mL in the 50-mg group, -1.90 log10 IU/mL in the 100-mg group, -1.99 log10 IU/mL in the 200-mg group, and -2.12 log10 IU/mL in the 400-mg group.

Among patients in the 400-mg group, 45% converted from HBeAg positive to HBeAg negative after 48 weeks of treatment, a percentage that slid to 27% at 24 weeks after treatment.

In patients receiving the 400-mg dose who were HBeAg negative and had HBsAg below 1,000 IU/mL at baseline, 33% reached the functional cure rate of HBsAg less than 0.05 IU/mL.

Across the four groups, 46.9% to 60% had a treatment-related adverse event. There were only two such events that were grade 3 or higher, and no serious adverse events related to treatment and no deaths occurred. Upper respiratory tract infections were the most common adverse event, occurring in 25% to 33.3% of patients in the four groups.

Alanine transaminase elevations occurred in up to 6.7% of patients during and after treatment, Liang said, but levels were generally stable during treatment, and no elevations reached a grade 3 level.

The follow-up period of 24 weeks after treatment ended was relatively short, Liang cautioned, but a longer-duration extension study is ongoing, along with a phase III trial.

#5728. Hims & Hers inks deal with Eli Lilly to offer branded weight loss drugs

Hims & Hers inks deal with Eli Lilly to offer branded weight loss drugs" style="max-width:100%; height:auto; border-radius:8px;" loading="lazy">

Hims & Hers struck a deal with Eli Lilly to offer the drugmaker’s branded obesity treatments through its online health and wellness platform.

Under the agreement, Eli Lilly will give Hims & Hers pharmacies direct access to its U.S. Food and Drug Administration-approved GLP-1 medications at self-pay prices. Eligible patients on the Hims & Hers platform can be prescribed and order Eli Lilly's injectable Zepbound (tirzepatide) and daily oral medication Foundayo (orforglipron) without leaving the company's platform.

Prior to this agreement, Lilly drugs were available to patients on Hims & Hers only if they left the site and went to Lilly's direct-to-consumer platform, LillyDirect.

Shares of Hims & Hers jumped 8% Friday following news of the Eli Lilly partnership.

The agreement brings together Lilly’s obesity medicines and Hims & Hers’ consumer platform to expand consumers' access to FDA-approved weight loss drugs, the companies said.

"Patients can complete a consultation, receive a prescription if their provider determines it is appropriate and have an FDA-approved Lilly medicine shipped directly to their home, all in one seamless experience," the companies said in a press release.

Self-pay pricing starts at $149 per month for Foundayo and $299 per month for Zepbound.

"At Lilly, we are always looking for new ways to help more people get the medicines they need, and that means meeting patients where they already are," said Ilya Yuffa, executive vice president and president, Lilly USA and global customer capabilities, Eli Lilly and Company, in a statement. "Millions of people turn to Hims & Hers for their care, and through this agreement, those patients now have a simple, more direct way to get FDA-approved Lilly medicine at an affordable price if prescribed."

Hims & Hers' technology platform supports provider outreach, follow-up care and treatment planning. Weight loss patients on Hims & Hers receive a membership experience that includes 24/7 access to a care team, nutrition guidance, regular clinical check-ins and access to the Hims & Hers Weight Loss community, according to the company.

The agreement with Eli Lilly mirrors Hims & Hers' partnership with Novo Nordisk, which has been selling its branded weight loss drugs Wegovy and Ozempic through Hims & Hers since March. Novo had previously sued the telehealth company for offering compounded versions of its medications. Hims & Hers' agreement with Novo marked a shift in its obesity strategy toward FDA-approved branded GLP-1 treatments and away from cheaper compounded GLP-1 drugs.

Earlier this year, Hims & Hers took heat from the pharmaceutical manufacturer and federal regulators after announcing plans to launch a knockoff of Novo's oral pill. It backed down from those plans under pressure.

Hims & Hers stated that it never offered access to compounded copies of Lilly’s GLP-1 drugs and has committed not to do so in the future.

As part of the agreement, the companies have committed to protecting independent clinical decision-making, so that providers on the Hims & Hers platform continue to prescribe the treatment they determine is right for each patient.

“Lilly is one of the most innovative pharmaceutical companies in history, and I’m incredibly proud that Hims & Hers is a part of making sure more people can access the medicines they’ve created,” said Andrew Dudum, co-founder and CEO of Hims & Hers, in a statement. “By working together, we’re not just reaching more people with obesity with seamless, personal access to FDA-approved Lilly medicine. We’re showing the industry what innovators can achieve for customers everywhere when we work together to make the world well.”

Hims & Hers has faced financial pressure as it shifts from high-margin compounded GLP-1s to lower-margin branded weight-loss drugs. The company posted a $92 million loss in Q1, including $33 million in restructuring costs, largely from write-downs of its compounded GLP-1 supply chain, which executives said faced “risk of obsolescence.”

In Q2, Hims & Hers reported an $86 million loss, compared with a $43 million profit a year earlier. The quarter included $81 million in one-time costs, including expenses tied to its exit from compounded GLP-1s.

#5727. Polestar Formula 2030 Concept Makes Us Hope Polestar Will Be Back In America By 2030

It already looks great at a glance, but things get way more interesting once you start opening stuff up. Both the hood and rear hatch open up clamshell-style, exposing the natural fiber composite chassis underneath and orange Öhlins dampers. The half-size rear doors are coach doors, giving the Formula no B-pillar and a huge opening when all doors are open.

Inside, a very curved instrument screen measuring 20 inches sits on top of the steering column while a 12.2-inch center touchscreen sits in the middle. That touchscreen can physically lower when the driver switches drive modes to Performance, and raise when the car is in Autonomous. Below this screen is an array of up to eight customizable rocker switches and knobs. You can swap the specific switches or knobs out, like you can with, say, a pro-grade video game controller, and their individual functions can also be configured to your liking. All four bucket seats sit low in the cabin and get inflatable bolsters and cushions for better support.

I've never really thought about how rare four-spoke wheels are, but looking at the 22s on this Polestar concept, I'm simultaneously sad that they aren't more common but also completely understand why that's the case. They're definitely weird, but I also sort of dig it?

It all adds up to a funky yet beautiful design that makes us hope Polestar will return to the U.S. market by the Formula 2030's namesake. Given the political conditions that led to its banning and how those tides are turning in this country, that scenario actually feels like a real possibility.

#5726. BYD Commercial Vehicle Sales Rise 68% in October!

Following up our usual monthly report on BYD’s passenger vehicle sales, let’s have a look at the companies commercial vehicle sales — all electric, of course.

The company’s bus sales were up 62.1% year over year in October, rising from 367 in October 2025 to 600 in October 2026. Month over month, compared to September 2026, sales were actually down 24.1% (from 791 in September), but sales vary quite a lot from month to month in this segment, and 600 is still one of the company’s best months ever for electric bus sales.

In terms of non-bus commercial vehicle sales, they did even better. They rose 69.1% year over year, from 4,483 in October 2025 to 6,443 in October 2026, and they rose 6.4% month over month, from 6,057 in September 2026.

Overall, BYD’s commercial vehicle sales rose 68.3%, from 4,850 in October 2025 to 7,043 in October 2026. They rose 2.8% month over month, from 6,848 in September. BYD’s commercial vehicle sales total 55,489 units so far in 2026.
